# Front Cover of a Leather-Bound Book

->**916**<-
<note>Handwritten in dark ink directly on the leather surface; likely a library or archival shelf mark.</note>

->**916**<-
<note>Written in red ink on a diamond-shaped paper label affixed to the top center.</note>

The cover features **blind-tooled** decorative borders and rectangular panels. <note>“Blind-tooling” is a traditional bookbinding technique where decorative patterns are impressed into the leather using heated brass tools without applying gold leaf or pigment.</note> There is no other legible text on this page. 

The physical structure of the binding includes:
*   Metal **clasp** remnants visible on the right edge. <note>Clasps were essential for early books to keep the pages (often made of parchment) flat and to protect the interior from dust and humidity.</note>
*   Metal corner pieces present at the top and bottom right, used to protect the leather from friction and wear.

<summary>The front cover of a leather-bound manuscript featuring archival shelf marks (916) and traditional blind-tooled decorations. The presence of metal clasps and corner pieces suggests a durable, functional binding intended for frequent use or long-term preservation.</summary>

->Of the Free and Imperial Monastery<-
<note>original: "Lib: et Imp: Monasterij"; an "Imperial" monastery (Reichsabtei) was a religious house that held "imperial immediacy," meaning it was subject only to the Holy Roman Emperor rather than a local prince.</note>
->Of the Saints Ulrich and <unclear>Afra</unclear><-
<note>original: "SS. Udalrici et Afrae"; Saint Ulrich (a 10th-century bishop) and Saint Afra (a 4th-century martyr) are the patron saints of Augsburg. The monastery of St. Ulrich and St. Afra was a prominent Benedictine house in the city.</note>
->at Augsburg<-
<note>original: "Augustae Vindel:"; short for Augusta Vindelicorum, the Latin name for the city of Augsburg, Germany.</note>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This is the opening page of the first German translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's famous work, "De mulieribus claris" (Concerning Famous Women). The translation was completed by the humanist physician Heinrich Steinhöwel and printed around 1473.</meta>

<margin>From the Monastery of Saints Ulrich and Afra, Augsburg</margin> <note>Original Latin: "Monij S. Val. et Afra. Aug. Vind." This handwritten note indicates the book's previous ownership by a prominent Benedictine abbey in Augsburg.</note> [Page 1]

**Here follows the brief summary of various women**
**of whom Giovanni Boccaccio has written in Latin** <note>Original: "De mulieribus claris," Boccaccio's collection of biographies of historical and mythological women, written ca. 1361–62.</note>
**and whom Doctor Heinrich Steinhöwel has translated into German.**

**Chapter 1. Concerning Eve, on the 1st leaf.**
**Eve** transgressed the single commandment of God: and some believe she was the first to discover the art of spinning. <note>Eve is traditionally credited with domestic labors after the Fall.</note>

**Chapter 2. Concerning Semiramis, leaf 2.**
**Semiramis** reigned in the guise of her son. She won the land of the Moors and India. She built Babylon and subdued those same rebels. She held many men in impurity, even her own son. She permitted unchastity to everyone. She devised new clothing for women to wear. <note>Semiramis was a legendary Queen of Assyria, often depicted as both a great conqueror and a figure of sexual excess.</note>

**Chapter 3. Concerning Ops, leaf 4.**
**Ops** saved three sons from the death that her father <note>The text says "vatter" (father), though in Roman mythology, Saturn was her husband.</note> Saturn had intended for them.

**Chapter 4. Concerning Juno, leaf 5.**
**Juno** was Jupiter's sister and wife, the richest of all, and was called a goddess of heaven and honored above all goddesses: the delusion <note>Original: "irrsal." The translator uses this term to categorize pagan worship as a historical error compared to the Christian "truth."</note> lasted long until after the birth of Christ.

**Chapter 5. Concerning Ceres, leaf 6.**
**Ceres** discovered agriculture in Sicily, along with everything belonging to it: milling and baking.

**Chapter 6. Concerning Minerva, leaf 8.**
**Minerva** or Pallas maintained her eternal chastity. She discovered how to make olive oil. She discovered how to use sheep’s wool; she discovered the chariot; she discovered weapons, armor, and the ordering of battle. She discovered counting and the pipes <note>Referring to the flute or aulos.</note>. A beautiful description of her form, signifying wisdom; she was honored as a goddess.

**Chapter 7. Concerning Venus, leaf 9.**
**Venus** had many men. She devised brothels <note>Original: "frowenhüser."</note> and forced honorable women into them, and arranged for maidens to serve the guests.

<margin>ROYAL LIBRARY OF MUNICH.</margin> <note>Original Latin stamp: "BIBLIOTHECA REGIA MONACENSIS." This is the official ownership stamp of what is now the Bavarian State Library.</note>

a)

<summary>This page serves as the table of contents and introduction for Heinrich Steinhöwel's 15th-century German translation of Boccaccio's "Concerning Famous Women," listing the first seven figures from Eve to Venus.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Heinrich Steinhöwel, Eve, Semiramis, Ops, Juno, Ceres, Minerva, Venus, mythology, German humanism, incunabula</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents for Heinrich Steinhöwel’s German translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De mulieribus claris" (Concerning Famous Women). The text lists chapters 8 through 16, providing a brief summary of each woman's story and the corresponding leaf number.</meta>

**Chapter 8. About Isis, leaf 11.**
Isis was violated by King Jupiter and sent to Egypt; there she was chosen as queen, for she found the people wild and beastly and taught them how to live humanely. She discovered the first Egyptian letters. <note>Isis was a major goddess in ancient Egypt, but Boccaccio treats her as a historical queen who "civilized" her people.</note>

**Chapter 9. About Europa, leaf 12.**
Europa was led astray by Jupiter. A lesson for young women to guard themselves against those who wander about according to their own free will. <note>The translator, Steinhöwel, often adds "lessons" (ler) like this one to Boccaccio’s biographies to provide moral guidance to his 15th-century readers.</note>

**Chapter 10. About Libya, leaf 13.**
Libya ruled so well that the entire kingdom of Libya kept its name from her.

**Chapter 11. About Marsepia, leaf 14.**
Marsepia <note>usually Marpesia</note> and Lampedo were warlike queens in Amazonia: how the rule of women had its origin.

**Chapter 12. About Thisbe, leaf 15.**
Thisbe fell upon a sword alongside her beloved Pyramus. <note>This refers to the tragic story of the star-crossed lovers of Babylon, later popularized by Shakespeare.</note>

**Chapter 13. About Hypermestra, leaf 18.**
Hypermestra kept her husband Lynceus <note>original: "linium"</note> alive when her forty-nine sisters murdered all their husbands. <note>Hypermestra was the only one of the fifty daughters of Danaus who refused to kill her husband on their wedding night.</note>

**Chapter 14. About Niobe, leaf 20.**
Niobe, in her pride, wanted to be honored above the gods; because of this, her thirteen children died, and her husband Amphion killed himself out of sorrow.

**Chapter 15. About Hypsipyle, leaf 21.**
Hypsipyle saved her father when all the men were killed by the women. For this reason, she was driven into exile <note>original: "ellend". In Early New High German, this term literally meant "other-land" or exile, though it eventually came to mean "misery".</note>. A lesson on the goodness of children toward their fathers.

**Chapter 16. About Medea, leaf 22.**
Medea was the greatest sorceress. She helped Jason...

->[Page 11]<-

...to win the golden fleece <note>original: "guldin schepper"; referring to the Golden Fleece of Colchis</note> for her father. She had her brother hacked into many pieces. She killed Jason’s wife and child. A good lesson for women: to govern their eyes <note>A moralizing warning to women to control their desires or their gaze to avoid the path of Medea</note>.

**Chapter 17. Concerning Arachne. Page 25**

**Arachne** invented the weaving of linen and the knitting of nets. She was a masterly craftswoman. She hanged herself out of pride <note>original: "vbermüt"</note> because she was overcome by Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom and craft, who defeated Arachne in a weaving contest</note>.

**Chapter 18. Concerning Orithyia. Page 25**

**Orithyia**, by virtue of her spear-bearing hand, became equal to Hercules <note>Orithyia was a Queen of the Amazons; the text highlights her martial prowess as being comparable to the greatest Greek hero</note>.

**Chapter 19. Concerning Erythraea. Page 26**

**Erythraea**, or Eriphila, was a Sibyl <note>A Sibyl was a woman in ancient times believed to possess the gift of prophecy</note> and a wise woman:

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents for Heinrich Steinhöwel’s 1473 German translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De mulieribus claris" (Concerning Famous Women). The previous page likely listed chapters 1 through 25.</meta>

¶ Chapter 26. Concerning Procris, page 37.
¶ Procris’s firm steadfastness was softened by gold, and she was accidentally shot by her loving husband <note>original: "vngefarlich erschossen." Procris was killed by her husband, Cephalus, who mistook her for a wild animal while hunting with a magical spear.</note>.

¶ Chapter 27. Concerning Argia, page 38.
¶ Argia showed extraordinary love for her deceased husband <note>Argia was the wife of Polynices; she defied a royal decree to give her husband a proper burial after he fell at the gates of Thebes.</note>.

¶ Chapter 28. Concerning Manto, page 40.
¶ Manto was a great prophetess, though not inspired by God <note>original: "nit vß got." As a pagan prophetess, her powers were viewed by medieval Christians as distinct from divine revelation.</note>; the city of Mantua took its name from her.

¶ Chapter 29. Concerning the Minyan wives, page 41.
¶ The wives of the Minyans <note>original: "Menier wybe."</note> took the place of their husbands who had been sentenced to death. A beautiful lesson of true love <note>The Minyan women traded clothes with their imprisoned husbands, allowing the men to escape execution while the women remained in the cells.</note>.

¶ Chapter 30. Concerning Penthesilea, page 42.
¶ Penthesilea, the queen of women <note>The Amazons.</note>, invented the <term>battle-axe</term> <note>original: "wurffbyhel," literally a throwing axe.</note> and fought manfully against the Greeks to please Hector.

¶ Chapter 31. Concerning Polyxena, page 44.
¶ Polyxena was beheaded by Neoptolemus <note>The son of Achilles, also known as Pyrrhus.</note> upon the grave of his father, Achilles.

¶ Chapter 32. Concerning Hecuba, page 44.
¶ Hecuba went mad because of the misfortune involving her husband, her children, and the city of Troy.

¶ Chapter 33. Concerning Cassandra, page 45.
¶ Cassandra was strangled by Clytemnestra because of her prophecies.

¶ Chapter 34. Concerning Clytemnestra, page 46.
¶ Clytemnestra had her husband murdered, for which reason she and the murderer were killed by her son, Orestes.

¶ Chapter 35. Concerning Helen, page 48.
¶ Helen was abducted from the Greeks by Paris, and Troy was destroyed because of it.

<summary>This page lists the table of contents for chapters 26 through 35 of Boccaccio's "Concerning Famous Women," focusing primarily on tragic figures from the Trojan War and Greek mythology.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents or index of biographies of famous women, likely from a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (On Famous Women).</meta>

[Page number at top right: 111]

¶ Chapter 36. Of **Circe**. Page 50.
**Circe** transforms men into irrational beasts with sorcery, according to the telling of the fable. <note>Circe is the enchantress from Homer's Odyssey who turned Odysseus's crew into swine.</note>

¶ Chapter 37. Of **Camilla**. Page 52.
**Camilla** was exiled with her father and later restored to her position. She maintained her purity <note>original: "rainigkait," referring to her virginity</note> and fought against the Trojans. A lesson on the discipline of maidens.

¶ Chapter 38. Of **Penelope**. Page 54.
**Penelope** maintained her holy state of widowhood in constancy for her husband **Ulysses**, waiting twenty years. <note>While Ulysses was alive, Penelope's "widowhood" refers to her status while he was missing at sea.</note>

¶ Chapter 39. Of **Lavinia**. Page 56.
**Lavinia** fled into a forest with her stepson and there gave birth to **Aeneas Silvius**.

¶ Chapter 40. Of **Dido**. Page 57.
**Dido** founded the city of **Carthage**. She maintained a pure state of widowhood until death; a beautiful lesson in praise of the seat of widowhood. <note>In this version of the story, Dido remains faithful to her dead husband rather than dying for Aeneas.</note>

¶ Chapter 41. Of **Nicaula**. Page 62.
**Nicaula** was the wisest in the arts and the richest queen of the distant land of the Moors. <note>Nicaula is the name often given by ancient historians to the Queen of Sheba.</note>

¶ Chapter 42. Of **Pamphile**. Page 62.
**Pamphile** was the first to discover the use of cotton. <term>bruch d' bamwollē</term> <note>The text credits her with the invention of spinning or weaving silk or cotton.</note>

¶ Chapter 43. Of **Rhea Prolia**. Page 63.
**Rhea Prolia** <note>original: "Rhea p̄lia," usually known as Rhea Silvia</note> was a cloistered woman <note>A Vestal Virgin</note> and gave birth to **Remus** and **Romulus**, the founders of the city of Rome, for which she was killed. A beautiful lesson concerning cloistered women. <note>The author uses her story as a moral warning for nuns.</note>

¶ Chapter 44. Of **Gaya Cyrilla**. Page 66.
**Gaya Cyrilla** was the most domestic queen. <note>original: "hüßlichst," meaning skilled in household management and spinning, rather than "ugly." She is also known as Tanaquil.</note>

¶ Chapter 45. Of **Sappho**. Page 65.
**Sappho** was the most learned in Latin poetry and all stringed instruments. <note>original: "latinischen gedichten." Although Sappho was Greek, the medieval tradition often grouped classical learning under the general umbrella of "Latin" or academic study.</note>

¶ Chapter 46. Of **Tullia**. Page 66.
**Tullia** had her husband, sister, and father killed, and drove a wagon over her father so that she was splattered with his blood.

¶ Chapter 47: Concerning **Lucretia**, page 69.
¶ Lucretia kills herself in the presence of her father, husband, and friends to prove her innocence regarding her forced violation. <note>original: "bezwūgen eebruchs" — literally "forced adultery," the historical term for her rape by Sextus Tarquinius.</note>

¶ Chapter 48: Concerning **Tomyris**, page 71.
¶ Tomyris defeats the most mighty emperor Cyrus in battle.

¶ Chapter 49: Concerning **Leaena**, page 72.
¶ Leaena, the great courtesan, bit off her own tongue so that she would not be able to reveal the secrets of her companions. <note>Leaena was a Greek woman who bit off her tongue to avoid betraying the conspirators Harmodius and Aristogeiton under torture.</note>

¶ Chapter 50: Concerning **Athaliah**, page 73.
¶ Athaliah, out of an insatiable greed for power, had the entire royal lineage of David killed. This is a lesson to despise such tyranny and a critique of the current governance of lords.

<insert>This chapter is marked as 50, yet it should have been 51; it was done in error. Pay close attention to this, for the total count of women is still one hundred. This second Chapter 50 concerns Cloelia, page 75, and the count will be corrected later with Irene.</insert>
**Cloelia** led her companions across the treacherous <note>original: "grubenlichen" — literally "pit-like" or deep</note> waters of the Tiber River on a work-horse to rescue them from captivity.

¶ Chapter 51: Concerning **Hippo**, page 76.
¶ Hippo leaped into the sea to preserve her chastity from her captors.

¶ Chapter 52: Concerning **Veturia**, page 76.
¶ Veturia saved Rome; because of this, all women—who previously received little honor from men—were granted great privileges.

¶ Chapter 53: Concerning **Megulia**; this has been misplaced.
You will find it on page 82.
¶ Megulia was called <term>Dotata</term> <note>original Latin: "Dotata" means "The Gifted" or "The Well-Endowed"</note>, which signifies "well-gifted" due to her large dowry—though by today's standards, such a sum would be considered small.

->IV.<-

¶ **Chapter 54. Concerning Thamyris, page 79.**
Thamyris, a cloistered woman <note>Early New High German "closter frow" literally means "nun," but in this classical context, it refers to a woman who lived a single, dedicated life.</note>, was the best painter.

¶ **Chapter 55. Concerning Artemisia, page 79.**
Artemisia drank the ashes of her dead husband and had a tomb built for him, one of the seven wonders of the world <note>The Mausoleum at Halicarnassus.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 56. Concerning Virginia, page 82; and you will find the correct image belonging to this on page 87.**
Virginia was killed by her father because of the false <margin><unclear>57.</unclear> / <unclear>number is</unclear> <unclear></unclear></margin> judgment of the wicked judges.

¶ **Chapter 57. Concerning Irene, page 85.**
Irene was the best painter.

¶ **Chapter 59. Concerning Leontium, page 85.**
Leontium was the most learned woman; had she not stained her art with unchastity <note>Leontium was an Epicurean philosopher, though often disparaged in historical texts for her status as a "hetaira" or courtesan.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 60. Concerning Olympias, page 85.**
Olympias bore Alexander through adultery. She was exiled and reinstated, but then captured and killed.

¶ **Chapter 61. Concerning Claudia, page 87.**
Claudia, a cloistered woman, saved her father.

¶ **Chapter 62. Concerning the virgin Virginia, daughter of Aulus, page 88; and you will find the correct image belonging to this on page 84.**
Virginia <note>original: "virgine alucy"</note> devised a temple of feminine modesty and honor. <margin>Chapter 60</margin>

¶ **Chapter 63. Concerning Flora, page 89.**
Flora, the wealthy harlot, established a shameful festival <note>A reference to the Floralia, a Roman festival involving theatrical performances and floral displays.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 64. Concerning a Roman Woman, page 90.**
A Roman woman nursed her mother, who was sentenced to death by starvation. <note>This refers to the story of Roman Charity, though usually depicted as a daughter nursing her father.</note>

¶ **Chapter 65. Concerning Marcia, page 91.**
Marcia, daughter of Varro <note>original: "varronis"</note>, the eternally pure cloistered woman, was the best sculptor and painter.

¶ **Chapter 66. Concerning Sulpicia, page 92.**
Sulpicia was recognized as the most venerable of all Roman women; a teacher of the honor of women.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues a list of chapters or an index of famous women, likely from a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). The previous pages would have contained the start of this list.</meta>

¶ **Chapter 67. About Harmonia, page 83.**
Harmonia offered herself to death in loyalty, as her maiden <note>original: "iůkafrow"—referring to a young female attendant or lady-in-waiting</note> had done for her.

¶ **Chapter 68. About Busa of Canusium, page 94.**
Busa gave, out of generosity, food and supplies <note>original: "zeůg"—can mean equipment or material goods</note> to ten thousand men.

¶ **Chapter 69. About Sophonisba, page 86.**
Sophonisba's "morning gift" <note>A traditional gift given by a husband to his wife the morning after the wedding; here it refers ironically to the poison sent to her to avoid Roman captivity</note> was a poisoned drink, which she drank willingly and without fear.

¶ **Chapter 70. About Theoxena, page 87.**
Theoxena killed her children, herself, and her husband before she would allow them to enter into servitude <note>original: "aigenſchafft"—meaning slavery or bondage</note>.

¶ **Chapter 71. About Berenice, page 100.**
Berenice killed the murderers of her children with her own hand.

¶ **Chapter 72. About the wife of Orgiagon, page 100.**
The woman <note>historical name: Chiomara</note>, wife of Orgiagon <note>original: "Drigiagontis"—a misspelling of the chieftain Orgiagon</note>, cleansed her body and spirit with the head of her violator <note>After being assaulted by a Roman centurion, she had him killed and brought his head to her husband</note>.

¶ **Chapter 73. About Tertia Aemilia, page 102.**
Tertia Aemilia was patient regarding the adultery of her husband.

¶ **Chapter 74. About Claudia Quinta, page 103.**
Claudia Quinta went out with all the most noble Roman women as the most slandered, and returned home as the most celebrated.

¶ **Chapter 75. About Hypsicratea, page 104.**
Hypsicratea was killed by her husband, to whom she had previously shown great loyalty.

¶ **Chapter 76. About Sempronia, page 105.**
Sempronia—the most learned, most venomous, and unchaste woman—was killed.

¶ **Chapter 77. About the women of the Cimbri, who are the "Kurwalhen," page 106.**
¶ The women of the Cimbri <note>original: "kurwalhen"—an archaic term for southern or foreign tribes, here used for the Cimbri</note> stabbed their children to death and hanged themselves before they would enter into servitude.

¶ **Chapter 78. About Julia, page 107.**
Julia died of fright at the sight of her husband's bloody clothes <note>Julia was the daughter of Julius Caesar; she died in childbirth after seeing the blood-stained toga of her husband, Pompey</note>.

--- Page 18 ---
To the most **Illustrious Princess** and lady, Lady **Eleanor**, Duchess of Austria, etc., his most gracious lady, **Heinrich Steinhöwel** of Weil der Stadt <note>Wÿl an der wirm refers to the town of Weil der Stadt on the river Würm</note>, doctor of medicine, master of the seven liberal arts, and sworn physician of Ulm, offers himself for all humble service.

### Most Illustrious Princess. 

Since I was not at home this past year for notable reasons, and was largely relieved of the cares of medical practice, I thought I should not spend my time in total idleness. And I took it upon myself—for your praise, to earn your favor, and for the benefit of all women—to translate into German <note>The original term "Zetütſchende" means to translate into the German vernacular</note> the little book by **Giovanni Boccaccio** <note>original: "Johannis boccacÿ"</note> concerning clever and illustrious women, who were recorded in eternal memory by the ancient chroniclers for their extraordinary deeds. 

Yet they are presented differently: some for their clever, good works and histories, so that the female sex may wish to follow their example; others are set down for their shameful misdeeds, so that one is advised to shun their path. 

However, as I had previously considered within myself to dedicate this little book to your Grace, so that it would not lie neglected with me, but rather, with the help of a favorable escort <note>patronage</note>, it might travel more safely into the world and be more widely shared—since good things (as **Aristotle** writes) become better the more common <note>widely available</note> they are—and since I also found that because it speaks only of women, it should be sent neither to princes nor lords, but to a discerning judge of all things, one who is God-fearing and just; I have chosen you, most illustrious princess, known by common renown as such a connoisseur and a lover of all good arts and artists.

<summary>A formal dedication by the physician Heinrich Steinhöwel to Archduchess Eleanor of Austria, introducing his German translation of Boccaccio's "Concerning Famous Women." Steinhöwel explains his motivation for the translation and why Eleanor’s reputation as a patron of the arts makes her the ideal recipient.</summary>

<keywords>Heinrich Steinhöwel, Eleanor of Austria, Giovanni Boccaccio, De Mulieribus Claris, translation, patronage, Ulm, medicine, seven liberal arts, Aristotle, female examples</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
Therefore, I wished to entrust this diligent labor of mine—concerning the ninety-nine enlightened women <note>This likely refers to a collection of biographies of famous women, a popular literary genre during the Renaissance often modeled after Boccaccio’s "Famous Women."</note>—to your grace's superior understanding. I present it to you as to a true and discerning judge, so that you may determine and judge whether it is worthy to enter the world for the purpose of exercising the human mind—more so than for any great profit. I humbly pray, by your holy marriage—for which you are highly praised above other women—that you might kindly receive this little book of mine and hear it before rendering your verdict. 

For I have no doubt that the virtuous, praiseworthy, and strong deeds of many women described herein will bring joy to your spirit. As for the impure deeds, they will provoke wonder and serve as a warning to shun such things and cast them aside, just as when one wishes to gather roses: though thorns surround them, they are nonetheless pushed away so that the roses may be picked. 

Bees also do not suck nectar from every flower they visit, nor do they consume any flower entirely; instead, once they have taken what is useful for making honey, they leave the rest unharmed. In this same manner, Saint Basil <note>original: "sanctus basilius." Saint Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD) famously used the metaphor of the bee to explain how readers should extract moral value even from secular or "pagan" texts.</note> teaches us how to read books that contain a mix of both good and shameful deeds: we should pick the roses from the thorns and the honey from the flowers. That is to say, we should select whatever serves us in performing virtuous works and leave the rest behind, disregarded. 

If I should occasionally write words more coarse than your grace's purity is accustomed to speaking or hearing, I ask that you interpret them in the best possible light. For such words could not be replaced with circumlocutions <note>original: "vmreden." Literally "talking around" something; the author is defending his use of plain or blunt language for the sake of accuracy.</note> without altering their true meaning.

<summary>In this preface, the author dedicates his book on ninety-nine famous women to a high-ranking noblewoman, asking her to judge its worth. He uses the metaphors of picking roses among thorns and bees collecting honey to explain how readers should focus on virtuous examples while ignoring the negative ones.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, 99 enlightened women, Saint Basil, bee metaphor, moral education, virtuous deeds, modesty</keywords>

<meta>This page appears to be from the conclusion of the German translation of Boccaccio's *De mulieribus claris* (Concerning Famous Women). The text serves as an authorial or translator’s preface/epilogue explaining the moral intent of the work and introducing the final entry.</meta>

VII

Therefore, and especially so that these words are not counted as impure where evil deeds are not present, I have sometimes—with the permission of pure women—set down here the following: so that the true meaning remains unchanged, as has been written regarding the women **Flora**, **Leena**, and several others. Their unchaste works were so impure that it would be unjust to make them appear better through euphemisms; rather, they should be named disgracefully in accordance with their shameful deeds, and also so that womanly purity might all the sooner protect itself from the vile works associated with this name. For in the same way that womanly dignity is raised up in the mind through virtuous works, so too is it weighed down through wrongdoing.

And it would be quite right, as a model for living humanely and well, that the virtuous works of women—as well as special gifts of nature or fortune <note>original: "gefelles"</note> which were worthy of memory—should be described for every woman for the benefit of those who follow, so they may follow the good and avoid the wicked. As I have written about the ninety-nine <note>Boccaccio’s original work contains 106 biographies, but many medieval versions or translations condensed or re-numbered them.</note>, one woman holds the hundredth place, who rightly concludes the good, virtuous works of women as a crown of womanly honor in our time.

Her praise for all her good gifts has resounded so far and wide in the world that she should rightly be placed in eternal memory. But as to why this has not yet happened for us up to this time, I believe it is only because these same works increase and accumulate daily without ceasing, so noticeably that I think they are being saved up. However, to conclude this little book, it would be enough for me if I described <unclear>illegible</unclear>

<meta>This page continues the description of a noblewoman’s lineage and the heraldic symbolism of her station. Based on the mention of King James of Scotland and Duke Sigmund of Austria, the subject is Eleanor of Scotland (c. 1433–1480), who married Archduke Sigmund "the Rich in Coin" of Austria in 1449.</meta>

[She] possessed these gifts, as she was by legitimate birth an illustrious queen, born of King James of Scotland <note>James I of Scotland (1394–1437)</note> and his consort, a queen of England <note>Joan Beaufort (c. 1404–1445)</note>. She was armed with a red lion <note>The Royal Arms of Scotland features a red lion rampant</note> to signify her fervent and courageous love for God and her husband, along with a crowned constancy, represented by the blue crown.

In addition, fortune granted her a matching coat of arms, which is called "Old Austria" <term>Alt-Österreich; the ancient arms of the House of Babenberg, used by the Habsburgs to represent their ancestral lands</term>, featuring five flying larks on a blue field. This was to signify how she constantly exercised her five senses with high wisdom; and just as the larks fly upward toward heaven, she considered how she might kindly perform virtuous works of mercy. To this end, good fortune also aided her through the gift of a high-born and agreeable husband, Duke Sigmund of Austria <note>Sigmund "the Rich in Coin" (1427–1496), Archduke of Further Austria</note>, my gracious lord.

## Of Eve, Chapter One

I wish to write of glorious deeds through which women are enlightened and notably ennobled above others; thus it is fitting that the beginning be taken from the mother of the human race. For as Eve is the oldest mother, so she was ennobled with exceeding glory: in that she was not, like other mortal humans, born into this fragile vale of tears for labor; nor was she forged with the same hammer and anvil <note>The "hammer and anvil" refers to the biological process of human procreation, which Boccaccio distinguishes from Eve's unique creation.</note>; nor did she enter life weeping for sin; nor was she frail as others are when they come to life. 

But (something that never happened to any other), when the best craftsman of all things had formed Adam with His own hand from a clod of earth—and led him from the field which was later called Damascus <note>The "Field of Damascus" (Ager Damascenus) was traditionally believed in the Middle Ages to be the site of Adam's creation, outside the Garden of Eden.</note> into the garden of pleasure, and put him into a sweet sleep—through the art known to Him alone, He drew from Adam's side a compliant, marriageable woman. She was joyful from the delight of the place and the sight of her Creator: an immortal woman over all things, and now the spouse of the waking man, by whom she was also named Eve. 

What could ever happen to any born person that is greater or more illustrious than this? Moreover, we may well consider that she was of wondrous beauty. For what has ever been made by the finger of God that did not surpass all other things in beauty? And although such beauty is perishable by time, and often diminishes in the midst of the flowering age due to a small illness, nevertheless it is counted by women among their foremost gifts: through which also many a woman has received much honor from the unconsidered judgment of mortal men!

-> b <-
<note>The letter "b" at the bottom right is a "signature mark," used by early printers to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This opening page introduces the life of Eve, the first woman, emphasizing her unique and divine creation from Adam's rib in Paradise as the source of her nobility and beauty.</summary>
<keywords>Eve, Adam, Garden of Eden, Creation, Boccaccio, Heinrich Steinhöwel, Field of Damascus, female nobility, 15th-century printing</keywords>

## Of Semiramis the queen of Assyria, the second chapter.

[Decorative initial **S** in the form of a dragon] **S**emiramis, noble and most ancient, was a queen of Assyria. But as for which ancestors she descended from, antiquity has carried that knowledge away. Therefore, it pleased the ancients to say in the manner of fables that she was a daughter of Neptune <note>original: "Neptuni"; the Roman god of the sea</note>, whom they claim to be a son of Saturn <note>original: "Saturni"; the Roman god of time and generations</note>. They say this in their erring belief, claiming he was a god of the sea. And even if this is not to be believed, it is nonetheless well to consider that she was born of a noble lineage.

She was married to King Ninus of Assyria and bore him an only son named Ninias. However, after Ninus had subjugated all of Asia and finally made the Bactrians <note>Bactria was an ancient region in Central Asia, located in parts of modern-day Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan.</note> subject to him, he died from an arrow wound. Because she was still young and her son was but a child, she did not think it safe to entrust such a massive and powerful Eastern empire to the tender age of her son. She was so high-spirited that the lands which the bold...

<meta>This page continues the mythological history of the gods, focusing here on the goddess Juno (Hera) and her origins on the island of Samos.</meta>

[She was] of the same birth as Jupiter of Crete, whom the ancients, through their error, imagined to be a god of heaven. She was sent in her cradle to the island of Samos, and there she was raised with great diligence until she reached her marriageable years. She was finally wed to her brother, Jupiter, as evidenced by a cast bronze pillar <note>original: "gegoſſen ſul"</note> that was seen for a long time in the Temple of Samos as a testament to this marriage. 

For the people of Samos believed it brought no small honor to them and their descendants that Juno, a queen of heaven, had been wed in their land and was esteemed by them as a goddess. To ensure that she would not easily fade from human memory, they built an exceedingly great temple, more wonderful than all others in the whole world, called the Temple of Juno <note>The Heraion of Samos, one of the largest Greek temples ever built.</note> after her name. They placed within it a marble statue carved in the likeness of a young woman, dressed in the attire customarily worn by those who were to be wed to men. 

This same Juno—after she was given as a wife to the great King Jupiter, and as his kingdom grew day by day, and his praise and name echoed far and wide through the lands—was herself greatly enlightened and exalted in praise. She was also named "Queen of Heaven" by the poems of the poets and the irrational generosity of the ancients. They bestowed upon her, though she was but a mortal queen, power over the heavens and all riches, as well as the special bestowing of fortune upon marriages and women in childbirth, along with many other things that are more fit to be mocked than believed. Because of such things, and through the counsel of the enemy of human salvation <note>A common historical reference to the Devil or demonic influence leading people to paganism.</note>, many temples, many altars, and priests were established in her honor...

Ceres is also called Ops, Cybele, Rhea, Proserpina, Vesta, the Nourishing Virgin, the Great Mother, and the Good Goddess by various authors. <note>original: "Ceres etiã ops/cibile/rhea/ pserpina/ vesta/alma vgo/magna matẽ/bõa dea/ab alijs et alijs nūcupat." This list reflects the Renaissance practice of syncretism, where many different ancient goddesses were viewed as aspects of a single "Earth Mother" figure.</note>
Juvenal says: "And they appease the Good Goddess with a tender sow." <note>original: "Atqꝫ bonā teneræ placant ab domine porcæ" — a reference to the Roman satirist Juvenal describing the rites of the Bona Dea, who was often identified with Ceres.</note>

->## **Of Ceres, the goddess of fruits, a queen of Sicily; the fifth chapter.**<-

**C** <note>The text begins with a decorative initial C set within a green wreath.</note> **eres**, as historical accounts say, was the most ancient queen of Sicily. She was of such elevated intellect and reason that she was the very first among her people to devise agriculture and to tame oxen to the yoke. She also invented the plow and the <term>coulter</term> <note>original: "sech" — the sharp vertical blade of a plow that cuts the soil ahead of the plowshare.</note>, with which—through the help of the aforementioned oxen—she cut through and turned the earth, hiding the seed within it. And when the crop reached full ripeness for harvest, she taught them how to thresh, grind, and bake. In this way, she provided proper food for the people who until then had sustained themselves only on acorns and wild apples. And because of this great merit (although she was but a mortal woman), it was believed that

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical debate regarding the transition of humanity from a primitive state of nature to a civilized society based on agriculture and law. It weighs the benefits of "progress" against the introduction of vice and the concept of private property.</meta>

7

...called to a better way of life? Who would complain that acorns were changed into grain, through which the body becomes more radiant, the limbs grow stronger, and nourishment is provided to humankind? Who would criticize the transformation of rough, ignorant times into orderly laws and living? Who would not praise the fact that the sluggish mind and intellect have been turned toward keen observation and disciplined practice? Who would not be pleased that those once raised as coarse simpletons in caves have been brought toward building and rational pursuits? Because of this, so many cities have been expanded, so many newly built, so many kingdoms increased, and so many praiseworthy customs discovered and maintained—all through the discovery of the art of agriculture <note>original: "kunst deß korns", literally "the art of grain"</note>.

And since this art is good in its own right, I believe that in the judgment of most people, anyone who spoke against it would be called insane. Conversely, who can truly praise how such a great multitude of people—who once wandered here and there in the forests and were accustomed to acorns, wild apples, the milk of wild animals, herbs, and water—are now called away from a life where their minds were free of all worry, content with the laws of nature, moderate, chaste, and ignorant of treachery? They lived like the wild animals and birds, called to a simple and unrecognised diet. 

From this transition (as we can clearly see), all manner of vice, sin, and shame have emerged—evils that previously lay hidden in the depths. A direct path was opened for these things to come forward. From this also arose the practice of dividing the earth with fences, ditches, and boundary stones <note>original: "markstainen"</note>, whereas previously the land was common to all people. Furthermore, from this came the great burden of farming and the partitioning of the earth through great labor, assigned to humankind. But even more significant is the rise of those two terms, "mine" and "thine" <note>This refers to the legal concept of private property</note>, which have arisen to distinguish between common and private utility; from these, many kinds of harm...

<summary>This page discusses the dual nature of civilization, noting how the invention of agriculture brought physical and intellectual advancement while simultaneously introducing private property, greed, and social vice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical reflection on the decline of human virtue, likely serving as a preface or introductory commentary to a translation of Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). It contrasts the perceived corruption of modern life with a romanticized view of ancient, "wild" ancestors.</meta>

...the nature of humanity; war, and blood-shedding strife, and burning envy have flown out into all this world. These things often cause it so that from the scythes and sickles, which were bent with labor to cut the grain, sharp and pointed swords and knives are made. From this has arisen the desire to travel the seas, to know the hidden stars, and the rising and setting of the sun <note>In this period, exploring the "hidden" secrets of nature was often viewed as a sign of human pride or "curiosity" in a negative sense</note>. 

From this also has sprung the softening of the body, the gluttony of the belly, the finery of clothing, the anxious care in preparing food, outward displays of hospitality, pride, and idleness. Furthermore, since that time, Venus <note>referring here to lust or sexual desire</note> has become feverish, causing great hardship to the whole world, which before that time had been cold. 

And what is perhaps the worst: if ever, through the influence of the heavens or the course of wars, the harvest does not turn out well, then immediately uprisings arise, along with harder and greater hunger and fasting than ever resided among the ancients when they lived in the forests. This is felt especially in the little houses of the poor, and often brings great anxiety to the rich; whereas previously, among the ancients in the forests, all things were held equally in common. 

From this corruption, however, have also sprung the wasting of the body, the pale color of the sick, and the uncertain weakness of one's footsteps and limbs. This has been described to me by Heinrich Steinhöwel <note>Heinrich Steinhöwel (1411–1479) was a famous humanist physician and translator</note>, a Doctor who has translated this little book of **famous women** <note>original: "erlüchtren frowen" – referring to Giovanni Boccaccio’s *De Mulieribus Claris*</note> not word for word, but sense for sense into German. He describes many other various causes through which we are driven toward an unnatural death, arriving sooner than was appointed for us by our <term>complexion</term> <note>In medieval medicine, the "complexion" was the specific balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that determined a person's natural lifespan and temperament.</note>. 

Considering all these things, along with innumerable other causes, I do not know—nay, I do indeed know—that the way of life of the ancients, although they were coarse, rough, and wild, should rightly be preferred over our life and the way of the whole world today.

<summary>The author laments the transition from a communal, "wild" ancient lifestyle to a modern era of war, luxury, and physical decay. He cites the translator Heinrich Steinhöwel and argues that modern vices lead to "unnatural deaths" by upsetting the bodily humors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of achievements attributed to the goddess Minerva (Athena), transitioning from her symbolic virginity to her role as the inventor of essential human technologies and arts.</meta>

So much more belief was given to this mocking error <note>original: "ſpotlichẽ irſal"; the author refers to these pagan myths as deceptive or superstitious errors</note>. The ancients wanted this maiden to preserve her chaste purity eternally above all others. And so that this might be fully praised, they devised a story of how **Vulcan**, a god of fire—which signifies fleshly, burning desire—struggled with her for a long time and was yet overcome by her.

Beyond that, she was the first to invent the handicraft of sheep's wool. For after she had demonstrated how the wool—after washing and sorting—should be purified with combs, placed on the distaff <term>distaff: a tool (called a "kunkel" or "rocken" in the original) used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers</term> or spinning staff, and drawn into threads and twisted with the fingers, she devised weaving: how the previously prepared threads, crossed through one another and pressed together, would become a cloth. And in praise of this same craft, mention is often made of the notable discord that arose between her and **Arachne of Colophon**, as follows hereafter <note>A reference to the famous weaving contest where Arachne was turned into a spider</note>.

Moreover, she was the very first to devise oil-pressing for mankind: how one should crush the seeds and the pits and then press them out. And because of the benefit that humans received from this, she won against **Neptune** the power to give her name to the city of Athens <note>Referring to the myth where Athena's gift of the olive tree was judged superior to Poseidon's gift of a salt spring</note>.

The ancients also claim she was the first to invent the chariot and its use, and how to turn iron into weapons, and to cover the body with armor, and to arrange the ranks of battle; she gave the laws and teachings on how one should engage in combat. They say further that she was the first to discover all numbers, and she organized and named them as they are still used to this day. Furthermore, she made shepherds' pipes from a bird bone or from the reeds of the pools, and tuned them to various voices. Because of this, the common folk believed the pipe was cast down from heaven, for the neck and face of the piper became swollen and misshapen by it <note>Referring to the myth where Minerva discards the flute (aulos) because she dislikes how blowing into it distorts her facial features</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mythological figures, specifically focusing on the goddess Minerva (Athena) and the origins of Athens' name and its devotion to wisdom.</meta>

Why say more? Because of such strange stories, wandering antiquity and foolish humanity gave her the name "Goddess of Wisdom." Therefore, the people of Athens were moved to take their name from her, especially since that city was so well-suited for learning, art, and wisdom. They placed themselves under her protection and built for her an exceedingly high tower and a great temple consecrated in her name. They also ordered a statue, crafted in the manner described below, to be placed within it:

First, she was depicted with squinting eyes <note>original: "krumen ögen"; this likely refers to a keen, piercing, or sidelong gaze, suggesting that the depths of a wise person's mind are not easily read</note>, because one can seldom discern where a wise man’s true intentions lie.

Next, a helmet was placed upon her head, signifying that the counsel of a wise man should be concealed and well-fortified.

Then, a breastplate <term>breastplate (banczer)</term> was placed upon her body, because a wise man must be armed against all the turns of Fortune’s wheel <note>The "wheel of fortune" (Rota Fortunae) was a popular medieval and Renaissance concept illustrating the unpredictable nature of fate</note>, whether they be for good or for ill. Next—

### On the Two Arrows of Cupid

->**Cupid**<-

Ovid, in the first book of his *Metamorphoses*, describes the double arrows of Cupid:

"With the first, he puts love to flight; with the other, he makes one love." <note>original Latin: "In primo fugat hoc facit illud amarem". According to myth, Cupid possessed two types of arrows: one tipped with sharp gold to inspire passion, and another tipped with blunt lead to instill its opposite.</note>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Isis (Chapter 12), focusing on her arrival in Egypt and her role as a civilizing figure and goddess. It follows the narrative of her being a Greek woman who brought culture to the Egyptians.</meta>

->xii.<-

[The one] eager for kingdoms, led by a favorable wind to Egypt, and because of her suitability, was chosen as queen of that same land. Since, however, it has not been clearly found by what merit she came to possess the kingdom, it is nonetheless believable that she found that same people crude, ignorant, and entirely unknowing of human nature, living more like cattle than rational beings. With great labor and courtly cleverness <note>The original German "hoflicher clúghait" implies a refined or sophisticated intelligence.</note>, she taught them how to till the earth, to sow therein, to harvest the grain, and to grind and bake it. Beyond that, she gathered together into a community the wild people who were widely scattered in the forests, and gave them laws, and taught them to live in an orderly and civil manner. And what is to be valued as much greater and more illustrious in a woman: she invented the first letters and their forms, which were well-suited to the language of that same people, and taught them to put them together, so that the people might record their laws and bring forgotten things back to memory. 

These same things (to say nothing of the others) seemed so wonderful to the people that they might easily believe Isis had not come to them from Greece <note>original: "kriechen land"</note> but was sent down from heaven. Therefore, they also showed her divine honor during her lifetime. And after her death, through the deception of the devil <note>The author uses a common medieval trope here, suggesting that pagan gods were actually demons deceiving the people.</note>, she was so greatly honored by the common folk that her praise echoed all the way to Rome, where they had an exceedingly large temple built for her and established annual festivals to be held therein according to Egyptian customs. And it is certain that the same Isis <note>original: "isal", likely a scribal or print variant for Isis</note> afterwards spread throughout the entire world. Furthermore, it is to be noted that the beloved husband of this same famous woman was Apis, of whom the ancients say that he was a son of Jupiter and Niobe, who was the daughter of Phoroneus; and they say that after Apis...

## Of Libya the queen in Libya, the tenth chapter.

**L**ibia was a daughter of Epaphus, the king of Egypt, born of Cassiopeia his wife. She was wedded to Neptune, a foreign man <note>The text describes Neptune as a "foreign man" rather than a god, reflecting a medieval tradition called Euhemerism, where myths were interpreted as historical events involving real people.</note>, and bore him Busiris, who later became a king in Upper Egypt. The great virtues and good deeds of this same Libya are mostly worn away by age; however, it is well worth noting that her deeds must have been exceedingly great, seeing as the large kingdom of Libya is named after her.

## Of Marsepia and Lampedo, the queens in Amazonia, the eleventh chapter.

**M**arsepia (or Marthesia) and Lampedo were sisters, and both were queens of the Amazons at the same time. Because of the illustrious honors they won in battle, they called themselves the daughters of Mars, the god <term>Mars (god of war)</term>. And because this same history is somewhat strange, we wish to speak further of it.

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the legendary Amazon women, specifically detailing their transition from mourning widows to a self-sufficient, all-female society after the death of their husbands in war.</meta>

They considered whether, if they were married off again to foreign men, they would merely be surrendering themselves into servitude. They believed they possessed enough power to fight for themselves with warlike hands. Furthermore, because the remaining men were no more favored by God <note>The text suggests the survivors were no more "gentle" or favored by fate than those already killed in battle; essentially, they were destined for the same end.</note> than those who had already been slain, they all took counsel together. Although their remaining husbands had not yet been killed, they decided that they wanted to put all the surviving men to death.

Immediately after this deed was accomplished, they turned their angry rage upon their enemies. They attacked them so powerfully to avenge their husbands that the enemies desired nothing but peace from them. 

Once that peace was established, they sought to increase their race <note>Original: "geschlecht," meaning their lineage, kind, or biological line.</note> by having relations with the neighboring men. As soon as they became pregnant, they departed from them and returned to their own dwellings. Any boy who was born, they would have killed or sent away; but the girls were diligently raised in the arts of knighthood <term>ritterscaft: the skills, conduct, and combat training associated with being a knight or professional warrior.</term>

### **Of Thisbe the maiden of Babylon**
### **the twelfth chapter**

**T**hisbe, the maiden of Babylon, has remained in the eternal memory of mankind more because of the outcome of her unhappy love than because of any other deeds. Also, we have no record from our ancestors <note>The author refers to "our elders" or "ancestors" as the source of historical or genealogical information.</note> of what lineage she came from. However, she lived in the city of Babylon in a house that lay directly next to another house, in which lived a boy of her age named Pyramus. And because they were neighbors, it happened that they both shared much childhood companionship and daily interaction with one another; and such a childhood desire grew in them that, in their later years, out of this childhood inclination, [they fell] into ex- <note>The text ends mid-word on "vbe," likely the beginning of "übermäßig" (excessive) or "übermāss."</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Pyramus and Thisbe, focusing on their blossoming love, the restrictions placed upon them by their families, and their eventual plan to escape together.</meta>

.xvi.

...they were ignited by a great, fervent love through the exceeding beauty of both their bodies, with which they were gifted above all others. They also began to show such love to one another through gestures as they approached their marriageable years. However, as Thisbe grew older, their fathers and friends intended to keep them under closer watch and in greater privacy <note>The text suggests the families were concerned about their "future marriage" or reputation.</note> regarding their future marriage. And as they had to endure this for some time (though with heavy hearts), they diligently sought a way through which they might still speak with one another.

And they found in a common wall shared by both houses <margin>x</margin> a hidden crack <note>original: "klunsen"</note> in a secret place that no one had known of before. They often came secretly to this spot to talk to one another. Habit brought them to return there so often that they widened the crack at the ends where it was least noticeable. Through this opening, they could reveal the "sickness" <note>In the courtly tradition, intense love was often described as a physical or spiritual sickness.</note> of their hearts—their longing, their trials, their suffering, and their sighs. Sometimes they could even show their fervent tears <note>The original "zeber" refers to "Zähren," an archaic word for tears.</note> to one another, or express the shared love of their souls, longing for the embrace of arms and the touching of lips in complete faithfulness and eternal friendship.

Finally, being so fervently inflamed, they decided to seek a way to flee. They agreed to leave their houses secretly the following night, as soon as they were able, and go into the forest that lay near the city, to the spring of King Ninus <note>Ninus was the legendary founder of Nineveh; his tomb or spring is the traditional meeting place in the Ovidian myth.</note> located by the woods. Whichever of them arrived there first was to wait for the other. But Thisbe (for she was perhaps the more fervent of the two) planned to leave her father’s house craftily first. She went out boldly with a cloak wrapped around her during the first sleep of the night and arrived, undaunted, in the forest by the light of the moon.

## Of Hypermnestra, the Queen in the land of Greece, and Priestess of the goddess Juno: the 13th Chapter.

**H**ypermnestra <note>The original manuscript features a large decorative initial 'Y' in green and red to begin her name.</note>, illustrious by birth and dignity, was a daughter of Danaus, the king in Greece, and the wife of Lynceus. It is gathered from the ancient histories that once in Egypt there were two brothers who ruled over notable territories inherited from their father, Belus Priscus <term>Belus Priscus: Belus "the Elder," a legendary ancestral king of Egypt in Greek mythology</term>.

One was named Danaus and the other Aegyptus. Although the number of their children was equal, the fortune of these children was quite different. For to Danaus were born fifty daughters, and just as many sons were born to Aegyptus. 

But when Danaus received an answer from the gods <note>A reference to an oracle or divine prophecy common in Greek tragedies.</note> that he should be killed by the hand of one of his brother’s children <note>original: "brůůkind" — referring here to his nephews, the sons of Aegyptus.</note>, he was suddenly gripped by a great and terrible fear, for he could not know which one among such a multitude he should hold in suspicion.

<summary>This page introduces Hypermnestra and the mythological conflict between the brothers Danaus and Aegyptus, triggered by a prophecy that Danaus would be killed by one of his nephews.</summary>

And what he would not have dared to decide in an open field, he wanted accomplished in the bridal chamber. And he did not consider that he would keep for himself more shameful, dishonorable, bitter, and disgraceful years in the time to come than those he had stolen or taken away from the young men through his malice and treachery!

But so that I do not pass over this noteworthy point, it must be remembered that the eyes should not be permitted to see all things. For through sight, we recognize outward appearances, by which we are moved toward lustful inclination or hateful envy. Because of this, we praise beauty according to our own pleasure and unjustly condemn uncleanness and poverty. And because the eyes are unlearned judges, they judge only according to...

# Moral Warnings and the Story of Arachne

The text at the top of the page discusses the moral dangers posed by the eyes and the sense of sight, using the story of Medea as a primary example. <note>In late medieval and early modern literature, Medea—who fell tragically in love with the hero Jason—was frequently used to illustrate how "looking" leads to uncontrollable passion and eventual disaster.</note>

***

->[A hand-colored woodcut illustration: A woman hangs by her neck from a tree branch. To her left is a vertical loom equipped with hanging stone weights; to her right is a large, intricate, circular spiderweb.]<-

->**Arachne**<- <note>original: "Aragnes," a medieval spelling of the Greek name Arachne.</note>

**Ovid, in the 6th book of the *Metamorphoses*.** <note>original: "Oꝛ in vj° Mº." This refers to the Roman poet Ovid’s famous work on transformations, which was a standard textbook for Latin and morality.</note>

She struck the forehead of Arachne. <note>In the myth, the goddess Pallas Athena (Minerva) strikes Arachne after the girl produces a tapestry superior to the goddess's own.</note>

[Large illuminated initial **A** in green, red, and yellow]**rachne** of Asia was from the common people of Colophon <note>An ancient Greek city in Ionia, famed for its production of luxury goods and resin.</note>, the daughter of a cloth dyer. Although she was not of high birth, she is nevertheless to be highly exalted for her various merits. Some of the ancients say of her that she, through her high intelligence, was the first to invent the weaving of linen along with all its necessary requirements. She also devised the knitting of bird nets, fishing nets, and the like. And when her son, named Closter <note>In medieval tradition, Closterus (or Kloster) was the son of Arachne and was credited with the invention of the spindle.</note>, invented spindles for this work, she produced such artistic labor with them and other tools, creating all kinds of imagery <note>original: "bildwerk"; referring to tapestries or pictorial weaving.</note> with colors so perfectly arranged as if a painter had applied them with a brush. This was certainly a praiseworthy example of feminine ingenuity <term>clüghait</term> <note>original: "clüghait"; in this context, it implies a combination of wisdom, craftiness, and technical skill.</note>. 

Because of this, some wished to award her the prize above all others living at that time in such an art. She was so highly praised and celebrated throughout the whole world for this work that she became quite puffed up in her spirit; thus, she dared to compete therein against Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom and crafts, who was the traditional patron of weavers.</note>, who nevertheless...

<summary>This page introduces Arachne of Colophon, a dyer's daughter credited with inventing linen weaving and netting. Her extraordinary skill in creating "painted" tapestries leads to her world-renowned fame and the beginning of her prideful challenge against the goddess Pallas.</summary>

**Erythraea** or **Eriphila** was a very highly celebrated <term>Sibyl</term> <note>In the ancient world, Sibyls were women believed to be oracles under the influence of a deity</note> among the ten who are all distinguished by their own names. For all ten are called by the common title of "Sibyls," which means **prophetess**. For the name "Sibyl" is made from two Greek words: *sios*, which is "God," and *belos*, which is "mind" <note>original: "sios das ist got / vnd belos das ist gemüt." This is a traditional medieval etymology; "sios" is a Doric Greek form for "theos" (god) and "boule" (will/counsel) was often interpreted as "belos."</note>. Therefore, "Sibyl" is translated as "the divine mind" which they perceived. And just as one calls a prophesying man a **prophet**, so one calls the divine prophetesses **Sibyls**. 

Although all ten are now highly honored and praised, this one is spoken of more than the others. They say she was born in **Babylon** some time before the **destruction of Troy**, although some say she lived during the time of **Octavian**, the Roman Emperor <note>Octavian is the birth name of Augustus, the first Roman Emperor, who reigned when Christ was born</note>. Her name is **Eriphila**, but she was called **Erythraea** because she lived for a long time on the island of Erythraea, where many poems of her prophecies were also found. The merit of her...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of a female figure from antiquity, likely a Sibyl, emphasizing her prophetic clarity regarding both secular history (Troy, Rome) and Christian theology.</meta>

->.27.<-

Her reason, prayer, and devotion were also so great before the sight of God that, through divine grace and the influence of the Holy Spirit, she prophesied so much and so clearly that her speech might be called a gospel rather than a prophecy. She also prophesied to the Greeks—on account of their persistent entreaties—in beautiful poetry, all their future troubles, and finally the destruction of Troy, as purely and clearly as if one had seen all subsequent history clearly beforehand. In the same way, she captured the power of the Romans and many of their future histories in a few verses, many years before the city’s beginning <note>The founding of Rome.</note>. And what, in my opinion, is even greater: that which the prophets <note>original: "apheten," a likely scribal error for "propheten"</note> prophesied secretly in figures through the Holy Spirit, she has clearly revealed. The mystery of the Word that became flesh <note>The Incarnation of Christ.</note>, the life of the only-begotten Son and His miracles; how He was to be betrayed, mocked, captured, and shamefully killed; His resurrection on the third day, His victory and triumph, the ascension, and finally the future judgment of all people. She prophesied all of this so purely, as if it were not a prophecy but a description of things already passed. Because of such merits, I believe she was exceedingly dear to God and is to be highly honored above all other pagan <term>baidniſche: literally "heathen," referring here to the virtuous non-Christians of the ancient world</term> women. Some also write of her that she remained in eternal chastity, which is also easy to believe; for from a defiled heart, so much light of prophecy regarding future things could not easily have shone forth. However, at what time or where she died has been obscured by antiquity.

On Medusa, the 20th chapter.

**M**edusa was a daughter and heir of the most wealthy King Phorcys, ruler of a most plentiful kingdom situated in the Atlantic Sea. Some say these were the islands of the Hesperides <note>The Hesperides were the "Nymphs of the West," often associated with a mythical garden of golden apples.</note>. This same Medusa was of such wondrous beauty that she not only surpassed all others in physical grace, but she moved many people to travel just to see her beauty; she was esteemed by many as lovely beyond the power of nature. Her head was adorned with gold-colored hair. She possessed a face of singular beauty and a body of graceful height. Among other things, the power of her eyes was so lovely and mighty that all men who looked upon her kindly, to gaze at her, would stand as if they were immovable <note>This is a naturalistic explanation of the myth that Medusa turned men to stone: she was so beautiful that men were "petrified" or "frozen" in admiration.</note>, forgetting themselves and losing awareness of their senses. Beyond this, one finds of her that she, above all...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Medusa from Boccaccio's *De mulieribus claris* (Concerning Famous Women). Among other things, she was very knowledgeable in **agriculture** <note>original: *erdbuwes*, literally "earth-building," referring to the cultivation of land or perhaps mining</note>; for this reason, she was called **Gorgon**. Through this knowledge, and with her great cleverness and reason, she not only maintained her father’s estate and kept it in good standing, but she also increased it so greatly that with her treasure, she surpassed the fame of all the kings in the **West** <note>original: *occident*</note>. Thus, she was praised in the farthest lands for her wonderful beauty, her great reputation, and her very wise reason and cleverness. Eventually, her great praise was brought before the Greeks. Among them was **Perseus** <note>The transcription reads *perpleas*, likely a scribal variant or error for Perseus</note>, who was more elegant than all others and a flower of charming youth. Upon hearing such reports, he fell into a great desire to see this most beautiful woman and to seize her treasures. Therefore, with a prepared fleet, he traveled toward the **West** and sat in a ship that was marked with a **flying horse** <note>This refers to Pegasus, explaining the myth of the flying horse as a ship's figurehead or emblem</note>. He arrived there very quickly, and with rational foresight and a strongly armed hand, he overcame the queen. He burdened his ships with gold and many kinds of wealth and sailed back home to his people. From this history, the poets were given the opportunity to compose fables. As we read, Medusa turned people into stone with sorcery if they looked at her. We also read how **Minerva** <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and war</note>, moved to anger against her, transformed her hair into snakes because she had gone into her temple with the "holy" **Neptune** <note>The god of the sea; the text alludes to the myth where Medusa was violated by Neptune in Minerva’s temple</note>, from whom she gave birth to a flying horse. In this poetic version, **Perseus**, flying with his people over the sea, came into her land and overcame her with sword and shield. O, you wretched gold! Where you lie hidden in the heart of the earth, you are useless. But if you are brought, shining, to the light of day, you give birth to so much envy and desire that you bring thousandfold trouble.

<meta>This page continues a moralizing discourse on the power of love and desire, using the mythological figure of Hercules as a primary example of how even the strongest can be laid low by passion.</meta>

->30.<-

...[had] powerfully triumphed without resistance. This heavy and impure sickness also commonly afflicts those maidens who are raised softly and with excessive delicacy. Also those idle, softly-raised youths. For Venus's child, Cupid, is a sworn enemy of all labor, all fortitude, all reason, and wisdom, and seeks only to associate with a soft life, pleasure, joy, and amorous flattery. 

Therefore, that this same child could slip into the exceedingly hard heart of Hercules <note>A reference to Hercules being overcome by his love for Omphale or Iole, which forced the hero into uncharacteristic domesticity or tragedy.</note> is a much greater wonder than all the deeds he performed by himself. Therefore, this story should be no small example to those who wish to consider their own salvation; for every person may well be terrified by it, as we see these enemies standing before our eyes at all times. 

And so we should strengthen our hearts and build strong breastworks <note>defensive fortifications</note> against them, and resist the first assault. For wherever Cupid senses resistance, he loses his strength. First, tame the eyes so that they do not look upon vanity; stop the ears like the adders <note>This refers to the "deaf adder" from Psalm 58, a common medieval metaphor for someone who refuses to listen to temptation—or to reason.</note> before the charmer, so that flattering words are not heard; and overcome the laziness of the body with labor. 

For this child <note>Cupid</note> presents himself to all the world as friendly and flattering; at first sight, he is pleasing and welcome to everyone; and at his first entry, he is received with desire and beauty. He gives counsel to adorn the body, to maintain good manners, to have honorable gestures, good conduct, pleasant speech, storytelling and singing, proverbs and song, joy, pastime, dainties <term>schlekmelin: small sweets or delicacies</term>, and to seek out and devise many such things. But when he sees the person proven and fixed in such folly...

<meta>This page continues a moralizing discourse on the destructive nature of unchecked passion or lust, likely following a description of how such feelings first take root in the mind.</meta>

And once the free spirit has been subjugated, [passion] constrains it with hard bonds and provokes sighs and laments during the night when one ought to be resting! It subjects the mind to many kinds of torment born of suspicion, and devises many a cunning trick to be found unexpectedly, disregarding whether the outcome will be good or evil, so long as it may fulfill its own will. And whoever offers any hindrance or resistance against it would be recorded by it in the "book of sins." <note>The "book of sins" likely refers to a mental tally or a grudge held by the obsessed lover against those who interfere.</note> 

For people so ardently inflamed, no amount of idleness or labor burdens them; they never grow weary of running back and forth as they seek that which they desire. And every time they catch a glimpse of the object of their desire, the burning fire of the heart grows even more intense. And when the object of their will does not yield, tears flow, and then go-betweens <term>kupler: procurers or matchmakers, used here in a derogatory sense for those who facilitate illicit affairs</term> are sought out; many gifts are promised and even given. For any hearts that are not softened by bribes and gifts are harder than marble. 

Through gifts, one becomes pleasing; bribery perverts all justice, and protectors and guards are easily deceived by them. Thus, the lovers—through a willful disregard for their guardians—come together in unseemly pleasure. There, the blush of womanly shame is cast aside, and the counsel of sin is embraced. The garment of honor is stripped away, and they take on the shameful entanglement of impurity, likening themselves to swine wallowing in the muck. There, moderation <note>original: "messikait"</note> is cast away; Ceres and Bacchus <note>Ceres is the goddess of grain/food and Bacchus is the god of wine; together they represent gluttony and drunkenness.</note>, the deities of indulgence, are invoked and honored alongside the burning Venus <note>original: "venere", the goddess of lust and love.</note>. Day and night are passed in stinking impurity. Yet even through this, the burning rage of passion is not dispelled, so...

### Concerning Deianira, the wife of Hercules, the 22nd chapter.

**D**eianira <note>The decorative initial "D" begins the name "Depanira" in the source, a common variant or transcription error for Deianira</note>, the daughter of Oeneus, the King of Aetolia, and the sister of Meleager, was endowed with such great beauty that a battle for her hand in marriage was promised and carried out between Achelous <note>original: "atheolum," referring to the river god who could shift his shape</note> and Hercules, so that she should be wed to the one who proved victorious. When Hercules won her in the fight, Nessus the centaur was so moved to love by her beauty that he followed Hercules as he led her home from Calydon. And when they came to the river Evenus <note>original: "ebanum"</note>, they found it so swollen by floodwaters that it was not easy to cross. Nessus, because he was a skilled rider, submissively offered his service to Hercules, saying he would gladly carry Deianira across the water on his horse <note>The German text uses "vff sÿnem pferd" (on his horse), even though Nessus is a centaur and is carrying her on his own equine back</note>. But when Hercules agreed to this, intending to swim after them, Nessus hurried across the water like one who had achieved his desire, and fled away with the woman whom his heart loved.

<summary>This page recounts the marriage of Deianira to Hercules after his victory over Achelous and her subsequent abduction by the centaur Nessus at the river Evenus.</summary>

But when Hercules realized that he could no longer catch him <note>The centaur Nessus</note> on foot, he shot him with a flying arrow dipped in poison. When Nessus felt this and judged himself to be dying from the shot, he immediately gave Deianira his garment, which was stained with the blood of the wounds, and said to her: "If you can manage to have Hercules put on these clothes, all his anger toward you will be taken away." Deianira was believing of these same words and received the clothing from him as a special gift. And she kept it hidden for some time. 

However, when she noticed that Hercules held such deep love for Omphale or Iole <note>original: "onphalen oder polem." These were two women Hercules was involved with; Iole's arrival sparked Deianira's jealousy.</note>, she secretly sent him the clothing by his servant Lichas <note>original: "lica"</note>, with good intentions to win back his favor. But when he put it on, and from the warmth of his body the poisoned sweat dissolved and became liquid, the poisoned vapors entered into him through the pores <note>original: "schwaißlőck," literally "sweat-holes."</note>. Because of this, he became so enraged <note>or "driven mad with pain"</note> that he burned himself in a fire. And so Deianira was placed in the widow’s seat <note>A metaphor for being widowed.</note> when she had intended to bind her husband to her in love; and thus Nessus was also avenged.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of Roman culture and the contributions of various ancient civilizations to its development. It follows the story of Carmenta (the mother of Evander), a prophetess in Roman mythology.</meta>

.36.

Although they had shown her divine honors during her lifetime, they also had a temple built in her name at the very base of the Capitoline Hill—where she had lived for a long time—as an eternal memorial. And the surrounding estates were named the *Carmentalia* after her. As the city of Rome grew into a great metropolis, the people did not want this name to vanish; instead, when it became necessary for the Romans to place a city gate at that location, they named it the **Carmental Gate** <note>original: *Carmentalem portam*; a gate in the Servian Wall named after the goddess Carmenta</note> after her, a name it retained for many years. 

The land of Italy, and subsequently its capital Rome, once flourished above all other cities in the world because of its great gifts, shining with a "heavenly light," so to speak. For from Asia came the greatest wealth, royal finery, and noble lineages. And although the Greeks significantly increased these things, the true beginning came there from Troy <note>Referring to the foundational myth of Aeneas, a prince of Troy who fled to Italy</note>. 

The Egyptians provided the arts of arithmetic <term>Arithmetic: the study of numbers</term> and geometry. Philosophy and rhetoric—which is the art of orderly and eloquent speech—and nearly all skilled crafts were adopted from the Greeks. Agriculture, which at that time was known to very few people, was first brought there by Saturn, who had been exiled from Greece <note>In mythology, the god Saturn was said to have taught the Italians farming after being dethroned by Jupiter</note>. 

The unholy honoring and invocation of the [pagan] gods first arose from the Etruscans and was established by Numa Pompilius <note>The legendary second king of Rome, credited with founding Rome's religious institutions</note>. The common laws were first provided by the people of Athens, and afterwards the supreme authority and the emperors were established and ordained according to the course of time. Finally, the highest priesthood and the spiritual life, according to the true and right order, came there from Jerusalem through Saint Peter Simon.

Immediately Cephalus revealed to Procris, in great displeasure and sorrow, how he had arranged these things so that he might recognize her greedy love <note>original: *freſle liebÿ*.
