Therefore she was cast into such shame and terror, and her face was covered with redness; and by the conscience of her guilt, her mind was so moved that she fled without delay from the company of men into the solitude of the woods and wilderness, to live there alone. And Cephalus followed her diligently with a hunter <note>In the original myths, Cephalus is often the hunter himself; here he is described as pursuing her with the aid of a hunting party or guide.</note> to bring her back to him. And it happened, by chance, that Procris had hidden herself by a marsh in a wild thicket of reeds; and when Cephalus saw from the front that the reeds were moving, he thought a wild animal had hidden itself there, and with the intention of hitting the same, he shot <note>Likely with a javelin or arrow.</note> Procris to death: thus she perished. What shall we say? I do not know if anything on earth might be more powerful than gold. I also cannot imagine that greater folly can be found than when a person strives after that which, once found and acquired, may perhaps provide some rest <note>The author is being cynical here: the "rest" found through greed is often the "rest" of the grave or a troubled mind.</note>. Both of these <note>The power of gold and the folly of man.</note> that frivolous woman <note>original: "lÿchtfertig wyb". A harsh moral judgment on Procris for being tempted by the gold Cephalus offered while in disguise.</note> has well proven: therefore she defiled herself with an indelible stain for all eternity, and also sought death, which she found: and yet desired it little. But that I may leave aside the immeasurable desire for gold, to which all fools are drawn: I ask those who are choked by such desire to tell me what use, what ornament, what praise, what glory they receive from it? Truly, according to my knowledge, this desire is nothing other than a shameful sickness of the soul, sprung from frivolity: and it is found only among those who can easily suffer that all other people—whatever conflicts <note>original: "spen". This likely refers to "Späne" or "Spän," meaning disputes, splinters of contention, or distractions that draw one away from virtue.</note>—are set before them.

<summary>This page concludes the tragic myth of Cephalus and Procris, where Procris is accidentally killed by her husband after fleeing his test of her virtue. The author uses her death to launch a moralizing critique against the corrupting power of gold and the "sickness" of human greed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a biographical account of a woman, clearly the prophetess Manto, daughter of Tiresias, following the fall of Thebes. It discusses her various origins and her journey to Italy.</meta>

...learned so completely from her father (as some wish to say, others believe it was found by the Chaldeans <note>The Chaldeans were an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their astrology and occult knowledge.</note> from King Nimrod) that in her time no one was found who could better tell of future things from the movement of flames, from their color, from their crackling, and many other things (I know not with what devilish help). Furthermore, the entrails of sheep and bulls, and of all other animals, were so well known to her that through them—and with other help and art (as is said of her)—she often compelled unclean spirits to come to her and provide speech and answers to her questions. 

And after the Greek kings who lay before the city to conquer it were slain, and King Creon <note>In Greek myth, Creon took the throne of Thebes after the disastrous war of the "Seven Against Thebes."</note> undertook to rule the city with power, she fled the new king and came to the part of the world called Asia. ## Concerning the wives of the youths called the Minyae, the twenty-ninth chapter.

**T**he names and the number of these same women have been worn away by age, so that they are no longer named by the scribes. <meta>This page continues a narrative likely drawn from historical or moral anecdotes (such as those by Valerius Maximus), specifically the story of the Minyan women who saved their husbands. It transitions from the narrative of the prison escape to a philosophical reflection on the nature of marriage.</meta>

.42.

And when the sun had set, they came to the
dungeon, desiring to see their dying husbands. And because
they were the most noble of the city, this was easily
granted to them by the guards. But when they
came inside to them, they did not spend the time with mourning and
lamenting, but instead revealed their plan to them, and
exchanged their clothes with the men, veiling their
faces according to the custom of women; thus the young
men went out from the prison in the women's clothes, weeping,
in sad gestures, with eyes cast down toward the earth.
To this also, the darkness of the night was helpful
to them, as was the dignity of the women, through which the
guards might easily be deceived, so that they let the
condemned out to their death <note>The guards believed they were letting the wives depart, but they were actually letting the condemned husbands escape in disguise.</note> and kept the women
in their stead. This event was also not revealed
until the executioners of the sentence came to kill
those who were condemned; only then did they find the women
lying in the prison in the men's place. That is, without doubt,
the great loyalty of women, and exceedingly fervent love. And
to say nothing of how the guards of the condemned were made into
a laughingstock, how the prisoners were liberated, what the
authorities <note>original: "die gewaltigen" refers to those in power or the ruling officials.</note> decided about it, and what resulted from it,
I will only say a little about the powers
of true love in holy matrimony.
And some hold that from the ancient law of nature (which
cannot be changed) no greater bitterness <note>original: "nẙd" usually means envy, but here implies the discord or malice arising from a broken relationship.</note> may arise
than from the discord of spouses; and conversely,
no greater love can be found than in a
friendly, harmonious marriage. For the fire
of reason does not burn with madness, but rather warms
with mutual pleasure, and binds the hearts together so
that they always, in like manner, will and nill <note>original: "wellend und enwellen." This is a vernacular translation of the Latin legal and philosophical phrase "idem velle et idem nolle" (to desire the same and to reject the same), signifying total unity of mind.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts a famous historical anecdote where noblewomen swapped clothes with their imprisoned husbands to help them escape execution, using this as a springboard to discuss the profound unity and "fire of reason" found in a harmonious marriage.</summary>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the virtues of love and loyalty, specifically focusing on the historical or legendary account of the wives of the Minyans.</meta>

And when lovely habit comes between them, it leaves nothing undone by which it may remain whole and be strengthened; it does nothing sluggishly, but is always fast and tireless. And if adversity should ever occur, it offers itself unbidden to endure toil, labor, and anxiety in order to suffer for helpful counsel.

**The** same pleasing, sweet, and proven love between the spouses of the Minyans <note>original: *meniarum* - the Minyans were a group in Greek myth whose wives famously swapped clothes with them to help them escape a Spartan prison</note> compelled their minds so that they devised such a ruse and high cunning at a time when their husbands were in distress, whom they otherwise might not have seen. Through the arrangement of clothing, by choosing the right time, and with all foresight, they sought to blind the watching guards.

**Therefore** they cast aside all pleasure and joy and used their high reason, resolving from the depths of their hearts to leave nothing untried for the salvation of their loved ones. For this reason, goodness rose up in their spirits, through which they boldly sought to liberate their husbands, so that they—through faithful, complete marital union <term>marital union: "gemahellichafft," referring to the bond and legal status of marriage</term>—might be set free and gifted again with life, though they had been condemned by the highest court. They lay hard in the dungeon, they were abandoned, and they were now handed over to the hands of the executioners. And the greatest thing of all was that the supreme power was thereby mocked, all laws despised, and the will of the entire community hindered and nullified through them. In order to release their husbands, they placed themselves in mortal peril, into the power of those they had mocked and deceived.

**I** cannot marvel enough at such great loyalty and so complete a love. I recognize by this that if the love within them had been small and cold, they might well have remained at home unpunished and with a good reputation.

### On Penthesilea, Queen
### of the Amazons, the 30th chapter.

Penthesilea the maiden was a queen of the Amazons and reigned after Orithyia and Antiope. From what lineage she was born, I have never read. <meta>This page continues the biography of Penthesilea, the Amazon Queen, focusing on her martial inventions, her unrequited love for Hector of Troy, and her eventual death on the battlefield. The text reflects medieval perceptions of gender roles and the classical tradition.</meta>

and she practiced not womanly, but strict, manly **chivalry**, with the war-chariot and on horseback: she also distinguished herself remarkably above other queens in strength and the arts of war. She must also have been ingenious, for one reads of her that she was the first who invented the <term>battle-axes</term> <note>original: *wurfböchel*. This refers to the *bipennis*, a double-headed axe traditionally associated with the Amazons in classical literature.</note> which before her time were unknown to the world. Of this, Horace <note>original: *Oratius*</note> writes in his *Odes*. 

This same Penthesilea—as some claim—when she heard of the unheard-of strength of Hector of Troy, began to love him (though she had never seen him) so passionately and so eagerly desired to do so, that she might receive an heir for her kingdom from him; therefore, she marched with a great army, well-equipped, to Troy to help him against the Greeks. And although the Greeks were widely and highly renowned for their strength and arts of war, nevertheless, so that she might please Hector with her power and skill in war and her beauty, she was diligent in fighting the enemy fearlessly. She often scattered them from their horses to the earth with her lance; often wounded them mortally with the sword; and often pursued the fleeing ones with her projectiles. She displayed so many manly deeds that Hector himself was greatly amazed by her. 

**Finally**, <note>The letter 'Z' in the original *Zeletst* (Finally) is rubricated in red to mark a new section.</note> when she fought manfully and for a long time against the strongest enemies—and more than enough so that she might make herself worthy of such a strong <term>lover</term> <note>original: *bůlen*. In this context, it refers to a romantic interest or paramour, though the two never actually met in many versions of the myth.</note>—many of her people were slain. She herself was so severely and piteously wounded that she was seen lying dead in the midst of the enemies who had been killed by her hand. 

Yet some say she only arrived at Troy after the death of Hector and remained there. Some might think it impossible that women should attain such <term>valor</term> <note>original: *manhait*, literally 'manliness'. In the medieval mindset, martial courage was a masculine trait, so a woman possessing it was seen as "becoming manly."</note>; but that doubt may easily be removed if they consider that habit is turned into nature <note>This is a reference to the philosophical proverb "Habit is second nature," suggesting that through constant practice, the Amazons overcame their "natural" female frailty.</note>.

From this, the poets have derived the fable <note>In this context, "fable" refers to a mythological story or classical literary tradition.</note> of how she was wooed by Apollo, and she promised to comply with his wishes provided that he would endow her with the art of prophecy. Since he could not take the gift back from her, he added a condition: that she might indeed prophesy, but no one would ever believe her prophecies, treating them as if a fool had spoken them. Others say that as he sat at the table, he was clothed in the garment that he had won through victorious war, in order that he might show the Greeks the festive days more clearly. To him, his wife, the adulteress, began to speak with flattering words and begged him to put on other clothes according to the custom of the land, which she had caused to be made in his absence. She offered him the tunic with sleeves, but without a hole for the head <note>This "headless" garment is a famous element of the Agamemnon myth, intended to trap him</note>, which she had prepared with evil intent. 

He went to a place where the woman brought the tunic to him; he thrust his arms into the sleeves and threw the tunic over his head, searching for the neck-hole. While he thus struggled back and forth, searching for the opening, she signaled to the adulterer Aegisthus to kill him while he was entangled, which was easily accomplished. Thus he was secretly murdered by Aegisthus, who also after his death ruled the whole kingdom with Clytemnestra defiantly and by force for seven years. 

But at that same time, Orestes, the son of Agamemnon and Clytemnestra, had grown up; his friends had protected him from the terrifying rage of his mother. He was encouraged to take revenge for his father’s death...

Although both are great evils, I do not know which is the greater: whether the sin of adultery is greater, or the extreme audacity of killing the illustrious king <note>Agamemnon, the leader of the Greeks in the Trojan War</note> without cause. He sought to ensure his father’s innocent death did not go unavenged upon his guilty, raging mother and the adulterous priest <note>Aegisthus, who helped Clytemnestra kill Agamemnon. <meta>This page begins the biography of Helen of Troy, likely from a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). It establishes her lineage and the difficulty artists and poets face when attempting to describe her legendary beauty.</meta>

.48.

**H**elen (as many of the ancients believe) has been well known throughout the whole world, both for her servitude to unchastity <note>original: "vnkůſchp"; referring here to her reputation for adultery or lust</note> and because of the long and great war raised on her account. She was the daughter of Tyndareus, the King of Oebalia <note>original: "Cibalie," a classical name for the region around Sparta</note>, born of the most beautiful woman, Leda, and wed to Menelaus, the King of the Lacedaemonians <note>The people of Sparta</note>. The beauty of this same Helen (as all the ancient Greek masters, and after them the Latin authors, say) was so elegant and exceedingly radiant that she might easily be placed above the beauty of all other women. For even Homer exhausted his own mind—and he possessed an angelic <note>original: "engliſche vernunfft." In this context, "engliſche" means angelic or heavenly, rather than the English language</note> intellect—to say nothing of others, before he could describe her beauty in his poems (based on as much as he had heard of it). Beyond that, all the best painters and sculptors have endeavored, with great diligence, effort, and labor, to achieve the highest level of adornment, a peak <note>original: "ain gipch," likely an abbreviation for "gipfel" (summit) or the start of "gipilt" (image/statue)</note>

<summary>This page introduces Helen of Troy, describing her parentage and marriage while emphasizing that her beauty was so great it nearly defied the descriptive powers of Homer and the skills of the greatest artists.</summary>

<keywords>Helen of Troy, Homer, Leda, Menelaus, Greek mythology, beauty, Trojan War, Tyndareus</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the legendary beauty of Helen of Troy, specifically discussing the artist Zeuxis's attempt to capture her likeness and the mythological origins of her perfection.</meta>

five of the most beautiful women, best formed in gesture and limb, were selected from all those who could be found. From all of them, he <note>The artist Zeuxis of Heraclea</note> assembled a single figure, taking from each woman her best and most beautiful features. Yet even then, with all his art and power, he could not sufficiently compose an image that might equal her, according to the universal praise of her beauty. 

I do not wonder at this. For who could—with the brush, with colors, or with the <term>engraving tool</term><note>original: "grabmesser"; refers to a burin or chisel used for fine detail</note>—depict those joyful eyes, that lovely face, and her pleasing smile? Or the many types of graceful gestures that change according to her words and deeds? For these are the works of nature alone, and not of a master artist. Therefore, he created what he was able to; and that which was made by him, he left to posterity as a heavenly image.

From this, ingenious masters <note>The poets and storytellers of antiquity</note> have derived such a poem about her: that because of her burning eyes, she shines like the morning star, possessing such light as was never before seen by any mortal man. They write of the remarkable beauty of her entire face; her long, gold-colored hair flying over her shoulders, adorned with sweet-sounding <term>cymbals</term><note>A poetic or perhaps mistranslated reference to hair ornaments that chime</note>; her sweet voice; her praiseworthy conduct and noble gestures; her rose-colored, sweet-smelling mouth; her shining forehead; and her white throat, like ivory. 

They speak also of the wondrous delight of her breasts, and how she was born to the god Jupiter by Leda. In this tale, Jupiter was transformed into a swan, and Leda was changed into an eagle <note>This is a curious variation in the 15th-century German text; traditionally Leda remains human or is associated with the swan, while the eagle is usually Jupiter's own symbol</note>, laying two eggs. From one egg came Helen, and from the other, Castor; both are described as immortal. By this story, the poets wish to show that the beauty of Helen cannot be captured by any man with brushes and colors, as the highest god Jupiter himself shaped her.

<summary>This page describes how the artist Zeuxis synthesized the best features of five women to paint Helen of Troy, yet still failed to capture her divine essence. It recounts the myth of Helen's birth from Jupiter and Leda to explain why her beauty transcends human art.</summary>
<keywords>Helen of Troy, Zeuxis, Leda, Jupiter, Castor, artistic perfection, Greek mythology, Renaissance translation</keywords>

...with every beauty and without flaw. Because of this wonderful beauty, Theseus was moved to travel from Athens into Laconia. During a time of the greatest celebration among the royal knighthood, he boldly and by force abducted the tender young maiden and took her home. And although he could obtain nothing from her beyond kissing, her chastity was nevertheless somewhat compromised <note>The original German "mit maſen vermerket" implies her reputation or purity was affected to some degree by the kidnapping.</note>. Shortly thereafter, while Theseus was away from home, her brothers demanded her return. This was granted without objection by Theseus's mother, named Alathia <note>This likely refers to Aethra, the mother of Theseus in Greek mythology.</note>. However, some say that Proteus, the King of Egypt, was the one who returned her to her country. Finally, when she reached womanhood, she was given in marriage to Menelaus, the King of the Lacedaemonians <note>original: "lacedemonū"</note>, to whom she bore an only daughter named Hermione.

Some years after these events, Paris returned to the city of Ilium <note>The city of Troy</note>. He was the same son who—because of a dream his mother Hecuba had while pregnant—had been cast out into the woods of Mount Ida to be eaten by wild animals at the command of his father, King Priam. Later, after he defeated his then-unrecognized brother Hector in wrestling and combat, he was identified by Hecuba as her own son through the evidence of his cradle and other tokens, and was welcomed back into the family. He then recalled the promise made by Venus <note>The Roman name for Aphrodite</note> to give him the most beautiful woman in the world, a reward for his judgment of beauty when he awarded her the golden apple <note>A reference to the "Judgment of Paris," where he chose Venus over Juno and Pallas Athena.</note>.

Consequently, after ordering a great fleet to be built in the forest of Mount Ida, he sailed across the sea accompanied by a royal host to the land of Greece. His pretext was to bring back his sister Hesione, who had been carried off by Telamon during the first conquest of the city. There he was received with great honor and hospitality by Menelaus. But when he set eyes on Helen...

## Of Circe, the daughter of the Sun, the 36th chapter.
**C**irce, on account of her sorcery <note>or "magic arts"</note>, is to this
very day a widely renowned woman, according to
the tales of the poets. She was the daughter of the
Sun and Perse, the poetess <note>Perse was a daughter of Oceanus; the text calls her a "tichterin" or poetess/maker</note>, whose father was

...called the daughter of the sun, because she was illuminated with a wonderful and delicate beauty, and because her knowledge of the power of herbs <note>In ancient mythology, Circe was a master of *pharmakeia* (the use of herbs, drugs, and spells).</note> was superior to others. It is said of her that all sailors who arrived at that same mountain—which was once an island but is now a coastal shore—whether driven by storms or arriving by design, were transformed by her sorcery <note>original: "zoberÿ"</note> through spells and potions into wild, terrifying beasts.

->CAMILLA<-
->METABVS<-

**C**amilla, the highly renowned maiden of worthy memory, was the daughter of Metabus, the most ancient king of the Volscians <note>The Volscians were an Italic tribe of the first millennium BCE, often depicted as fierce rivals of early Rome</note>, and Camilla, his wife. The mother ended her life at the birth of this same child. In her memory, the father removed the letter "s" from her name <note>Original: "tett er den buchstaben .s. von ierem namē." Boccaccio explains that her mother was named Casmilla; by removing the 's', the name became Camilla</note> so that she would be called **Camilla**.

From the very day of her birth, fortune was harsh and ill-intentioned toward this maiden. Not long after the mother’s death, Metabus was suddenly driven from his kingdom by an uprising <note>The original term "vfflöff" refers to a sudden revolt or civil disturbance</note> among his most powerful lords and citizens. He fled, taking nothing with him except his small daughter, who was dearer to him than all other wealth. Because of her, he lamented his misfortune the less, since he was able to take her with him into exile <term>exile</term> <note>The original German uses the word "ellend," which in the medieval and early modern period specifically meant being "out of the land" or in a foreign country, rather than just being unhappy</note>. 

As the poor man escaped on foot, carrying his only daughter in his arms, he came to a flowing river named the Amasenus <note>The transcription says "Damascenus," likely a printer's error for the Amasenus, a river in the Latium region of Italy where the story takes place</note>.

With all the strength of his body, he threw the pole with the child as far as he could into the water, and then he quickly swam across after her.

# Regarding the Maiden Camilla

->**The Warrior Queen**<-

When the **prince** <note>Likely referring to Turnus, the primary antagonist of Aeneas in Italy.</note> **wanted** and **sought** for **help** in his great necessity, **Camilla** also arrived at the camp. She had **sought** out the conflict, driven by a courageous **spirit** rather than the **softheartedness** <term>softheartedness<note>The original German "waichmůtikait" suggests a delicacy or emotional vulnerability that medieval authors often contrasted with the "manly" virtue of female warriors like Amazons.</note></term> typically expected of women. 

She was **outfitted** <note>original: "geschalmůczet"; likely referring to her being armored or wearing a specific style of helmet or hood.</note> for the rigors of the field. In her **spirit**, she was determined **to do** whatever was required for victory. The soldiers were filled with **joys** at the sight of her, for it was a marvel **to hear** her speak and to see her bearing.

<margin>Camilla joins the prince Turnus.</margin>

All **eyes** were fixed upon her, and from her **mouth** came words that inspired the troops. There was **overwhelming joy** among the men to have such a powerful ally, and she herself appeared quite **joyful** to lead her cavalry into the fray.

<summary>This page describes the arrival of the warrior queen Camilla to assist Turnus in his war, highlighting her rejection of traditional feminine "softness" in favor of military resolve.</summary>

## On Lavinia, the Queen of Laurentum, the 39th Chapter.

**Lavinia**, the Queen of Laurentum, was descended from King Saturn of Crete (which is now called **Candia** <note>Candia was the name used for Crete during the Middle Ages and the Renaissance, particularly by the Venetians.</note>). She was the only daughter of King Latinus and his wife, Amata. Eventually, she became the wife of the most highly renowned prince, **Aeneas of Troy**. She has remained prominent in the memory of mankind more because of the wars and battles Aeneas fought against **Turnus** on her account than for any other praiseworthy deeds performed by her. 

This same Lavinia—owing to the exceptional beauty of her appearance and also because she was the designated heir to her father’s kingdom—was passionately desired by Turnus, the fierce King of the **Rutulians**, to be taken as his lawful wife. In this matter, Amata, the young woman’s mother, gave him great hope and favor, because he was the son of her daughter <note>The text reads "tochter sun" (grandson), though traditionally Turnus is identified as the son of Amata's sister; the translator may be following a specific variant or using the term for a close relative.</note> by another man. But Latinus, through the practice of...

## Chapter 40: Concerning Dido

**D**ido, who was previously called Elissa, was a queen and the founder of the city of Carthage. I must expand upon her praise somewhat in my writing, so that I might erase some of the stains that have been unfairly cast upon her widowhood by certain writers <note>The author likely refers to the "stains" created by Virgil, who depicted Dido as breaking her vow of chastity for Aeneas, whereas older historical traditions maintained she remained faithful to her dead husband.</note>. To speak of the beginning: the Phoenicians, a highly intelligent and industrious <term>spůͦrich: literally "scent-following," implying a people who are diligent and skillful at seeking out opportunities</term> people, traveled across the sea from furthest Egypt in ancient times into Syria, where they built many magnificent cities. Among them, the supreme ruler was King Agenor, who not only in his own time, but even up to our...

<summary>This page introduces the history of Queen Dido (Elissa), the founder of Carthage, and the origins of the Phoenician people. The author expresses a desire to defend Dido's reputation against historical slanders regarding her widowhood.</summary>

<keywords>Dido, Elissa, Carthage, Phoenicians, King Agenor, Virgil, Aeneid, founding of cities, widowhood</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy and history of the Tyrian royalty, focusing on the origins of Queen Dido, the legendary founder of Carthage.</meta>

...was illustrious and widely renowned! From this lineage, Dido took her origin. And when her father Belus <note>A legendary King of Tyre in Phoenician mythology</note> had conquered the island of Cyprus, he ended his days and entrusted the young lady and her brother Pygmalion (who was the elder) to the lords and citizens of the land. They, immediately thereafter, placed him upon the royal throne of his father to rule the kingdom.

They gave Elissa, his sister, as a wife to the highest-ranking man after the king in Tyre, named Aterbas, or Sychaeus, or Sicharius <note>original: "aterbas oder ſicheus oder ſicharius"; these are various historical and literary names for Dido’s husband</note>, who was the administrator of the highest office of the priesthood of Hercules! They also loved one another holily in marriage.

Pygmalion was, above all other mortal men, so possessed by greed <term>greed: the original Early New High German 'gytikait' implies an obsessive, almost demonic avarice</term> that he could never be truly satisfied by gold. Now, Aterbas was exceedingly rich in gold! But when he recognized his brother-in-law's greed, he kept it as hidden as he could. Yet he could not suppress the common rumors of his wealth; because of this, Pygmalion sought ways to obtain the gold and had him secretly and unexpectedly murdered.

When Elissa became aware of this, she bore the death of her husband so unbearably that it lacked little but that she would have died with him. After she had spent much time with her tears, and had often fruitlessly called upon her dearest husband Aterbas, and had severely cursed her brother at all times, desiring revenge, she was warned in sleep (as she had also often wisely considered while awake) to flee from her brother, so that she would not also be killed because of his great greed.

Therefore she cast off womanly frailty and strengthened her heart with a manly spirit; for which reason her name Elissa was abandoned and she was called Dido. In her language <note>The author refers to Phoenician or Punic</note>, this means as much as "a manly woman" <note>This reflects a traditional folk etymology where 'Dido' is associated with the Latin 'virago', meaning a woman who possesses male strengths or courage</note>. And she began to consider above all things how...

<summary>This page narrates the tragic backstory of Elissa (Dido), whose husband Aterbas is murdered by her greedy brother, King Pygmalion. Distraught but warned in a dream, Elissa resolves to flee Tyre, adopting the name Dido to symbolize her newfound "manly" strength and resolve.</summary>
<keywords>Dido, Elissa, Pygmalion, Tyre, Aterbas, Sychaeus, Carthage foundation, greed, Phoenician history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Dido's flight from Tyre following the murder of her husband, Sychaeus, by her brother King Pygmalion. The previous pages likely detailed the murder and Dido's initial grief.</meta>

->57<-

She drew to herself the princes of the cities, who had been burdened and harmed in many ways by her brother, so that they might agree to her plan. When this was done, Dido had all the treasures of her husband—and as much as she could take from her brother—secretly carried into the ships which he <note>her brother, Pygmalion</note> had prepared to send her away. And with well-considered cunning, she had many sacks and small chests filled with sand and heavily weighted, to pretend that her treasures were inside, and had them also carried openly into the ships. Thus she sailed away from there with all the people who were allied with her, the greater part of whom were unaware of what her intention was.

But as soon as they reached the high seas, she ordered those same weighted sacks and chests to be thrown overboard, at which everyone who had not been informed of her secret was filled with wonder. And upon this, she spoke to her fellow voyagers thus: 

"Now I will gladly suffer the death I have often desired, since I have found the opportunity to sink the treasure into the sea—that treasure for which my dearest husband was killed by the greedy Pygmalion. But I have compassion and mercy for you, for I know well that if we return to my greedy brother, he will not leave you and me unpunished with a grim death once he realizes the treasure is sunk. To prevent this, I have resolved in my heart never to return to my brother, and I offer myself, according to my power, to be honorable and helpful to all who wish to stay with me, wherever we may go, and never to abandon you." 

When the sailors heard this—although they were loath to leave their ancestral homes—nevertheless, out of fear of the death they expected from Pygmalion, they readily agreed to travel with her into exile <note>original: "ellend," which in Early New High German refers to being in a foreign land or exile, carrying a sense of both physical distance and the resulting misery</note>.

<summary>Dido uses a clever ruse involving weighted sacks of sand to trick her followers into believing she has lost her husband's treasure at sea, thereby forcing them to choose exile over facing King Pygmalion's certain wrath. This strategic move secures the loyalty of her fleet as they begin their journey to found a new city.</summary>

<keywords>Dido, Pygmalion, Sychaeus, Tyre, treasure, cunning, exile, Phoenician history, maritime escape</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Elissa (better known as Dido), the legendary founder of Carthage, as she flees her brother Pygmalion after the murder of her husband. It follows her journey from Tyre through the Mediterranean.</meta>

And they turned their ships and came, by the lady's instruction, to the island of Cyprus. There they found young women sitting on the shore, according to their custom, to provide physical pleasure and amusement to guests in honor of the goddess Venus <note>original: "veneri"</note> and as a special sacrifice, so that they might preserve the human race <note>This refers to the practice of sacred prostitution attributed to Cyprus in antiquity.</note>. She took them all with her, and also the high priest with all his household, from whom she learned many future things that would arise from her flight. 

Sailing thus, they left Candia <note>The medieval name for Crete.</note> behind them and Sicily to the right hand, and arrived at the shore of Africa. There they intended to find rest from their great labor and safety for their ships, and they landed there. Immediately, there was a great gathering of the nearby people and a desire to see the foreign guests; and some brought food, others various merchandise, as is customary. Through this, they gained mutual goodwill and favor on both sides, and through friendly conversation, they recognized that it was the inhabitants' good will that the guests remain with them. To this also the people of Utica <note>An ancient Phoenician city in North Africa, near the site of Carthage.</note> advised, who themselves had once traveled there from Tyre. 

And although she heard that her brother was preparing to wage war against her, she nevertheless remained undaunted and decided to stay in those parts. So that she would not be suspected of attempting great things, she requested only as much land to establish her seat of government as she could encompass with an ox hide. And when that much ground was sold to her, she had the hide cut into such small strips that she enclosed much more land with it than the sellers had ever imagined <note>This is the famous "Byrsa" (hide) trick; the word 'Byrsa' in Greek means 'hide,' explaining the legend of how the citadel of Carthage was named.</note>. And she began to dig there and found at the beginning a horse's head, which from the interpretation <unclear>of the omen...</unclear>

<summary>Dido arrives in Africa after stopping in Cyprus and uses a clever trick involving an ox hide to secure enough land to found the city of Carthage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the founding of Carthage, following the clever acquisition of land using an ox-hide.</meta>

->.59.<-

The soothsayer indicated that a warlike city would be built there, and he named the city Carthage, and the citadel within it "Byrsa," <note>original: "birsam"; the name is a pun on the Greek word *byrsa*, meaning "ox-hide," referencing the legend that Dido enclosed the land for the city within the strips of a single hide.</note> so named after the ox-hide. Then, for the first time, she allowed her companions to see the treasure which she had previously hidden, and she encouraged them toward joy and good hope for future fortune. Immediately, through the willing labor of every person, the temple, the walls, the public buildings of the city, and the others were quickly erected. Laws were established for the common people, and order was given so that they might live civilly and well in unity. 

Because of this, and also due to her exceeding beauty, noble bearing, high virtue, and supreme constancy in her honor, she was so exalted that her praise was spread throughout all of Africa. Because of this fame (since all men of Africa are said to be inclined toward a quick weakness for bodily lust), <note>This reflects a common medieval stereotype regarding the "hot-blooded" temperament of people from southern climates.</note> the King of the Musicani <note>original: "musicanorum"; likely referring to the Maxitani, a North African tribe.</note> was fervently moved to seek her friendship, and he intended to win her through threats. He sent word to the leaders of the city: unless they gave him the queen as his wife, he would destroy the city. When they heard this, they were deeply troubled in their hearts, for they knew the holy and immovable resolution of their lady's chastity; on the other hand, they feared the king, that if his will were not fulfilled, they would be entirely [destroyed] <note>The text cuts off at "vert-", likely "vertilgt" (destroyed) or "vertrieben" (driven out).</note> by him.

<summary>This page describes the rapid construction and legal organization of Carthage under Queen Dido's leadership, as well as the beginning of a diplomatic crisis caused by a neighboring king's demand for her hand in marriage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Dido, the Queen of Carthage. In the preceding text, her citizens likely presented her with the demand of a neighboring king (Iarbas) for her hand in marriage, under threat of war.</meta>

For none among them wanted to leave their home and go to the terrible tyrant <note>original: "fraiſſamen wůtrich" — this refers to Iarbas, the King of the Gaetulians, who threatened Carthage with war if Dido refused to marry him</note> to instruct him. The noble woman <note>original: "Das edel blůt" — literally "the noble blood," a poetic way of referring to the Queen herself</note> did not notice what treachery lay hidden in their words, and she turned boldly toward them and spoke thus: "O you manly citizens, what fickleness is in your minds! O, what laziness of body! Do you not know that we are born for our father and for the fatherland? And that no one is worthy of the name of 'citizen' unless he dares to suffer not only hardship but even death for the common good <term>common good: "gemainen nucz" — a central civic virtue in medieval and Renaissance political thought</term>, should it happen so? Therefore, go forth happily and do not let yourselves be overcome by small worries, so that you may prevent the destruction of the land."

By this speech, they <note>the citizens/counselors</note> believed they had the queen sufficiently trapped. For if it was her own advice to suffer worry for the sake of the common good, then it would be a very small harm if she also, to maintain that peace, took a husband! And so they revealed to her the king’s desire. 

But when she realized how she had ensnared herself through her own advice, she lamented such treachery, which she could not resist. She sighed deeply in her soul and thought how she might strengthen her resolve of chastity <note>In this version of the legend, Dido is celebrated for her absolute devotion to her deceased husband, Sychaeus, rather than her tragic love for Aeneas</note> to remain faithful to her husband. And she found what she believed to be the most fitting way. She gave them this answer: that if it were their will for her to marry, they should give her a set period of time <note>a "zil," or a delay/deadline</note>, after which she would enter into marriage again. This request was granted to her.

In the meantime, Aeneas came from destroyed Troy, a fugitive by chance, wandering to her shores; he was well received by her. But to keep eternal purity for her husband, as was her steady intention, and to choose death rather than break her vow, she had a pyre of dry wood carried to the highest point of her dwelling, according to her custom, to show as if she [wanted to offer a sacrifice] to her husband Sychaeus... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the word "Sicheo" refers to Sychaeus, Dido's late husband.</note>

<summary>Dido’s citizens trick her into agreeing to remarry by appealing to her own sense of civic duty, but she secretly plans to maintain her vow of chastity through a funeral pyre ritual as Aeneas arrives.</summary>

I was completely forsaken by everyone: I had neither counsel nor help from anyone. <meta>This page continues a moralizing discourse comparing the virtues of figures from antiquity with the expectations for Christian women. It specifically focuses on the "Chaste Dido" tradition, a version of the myth common in the Middle Ages where Dido commits suicide to remain faithful to her late husband, Sychaeus, rather than for the love of Aeneas.</meta>

If the noblewoman Dido was able, for the sake of earthly honor, to withstand and conquer her <term>raging blood</term> <note>This refers to physical passions or carnal desires.</note>, why then should a Christian woman not be capable of the same in order to earn eternal honor and glory? 

But unfortunately, while we seek ways to hide these misdeeds from God—to whom, nonetheless, all things are revealed—we deprive ourselves of earthly honor and eternal joy. We force ourselves to fall into the depths from which no one can ascend. 

Therefore, all those who look upon the dead body of Dido <note>The author invokes the "historical" or "chaste" Dido. In this tradition, Dido died to avoid a forced marriage and stay true to her first husband, making her a model of widowhood.</note> should rightly feel ashamed. This is especially true when they consider the cause of her death: that they are being surpassed in praiseworthy chastity by a pagan woman. 

Furthermore, no widow should think that she has done enough for her husband simply by weeping for him and wearing black clothing and a <term>broad veil</term> <note>The original term "sturcz" refers to a specific type of stiffened head-covering or hood worn as a sign of mourning in the 15th and 16th centuries.</note>...

<summary>This passage uses the legendary Queen Dido as a moral exemplar, arguing that if a pagan woman could sacrifice her life for chastity, Christian women have no excuse for failing to uphold the same virtue for eternal reward.</summary>

### Concerning Nicaula: The 41st Chapter

Nicaula was born in the most distant land of the Moors. Because she emerged from a wild, unrefined, and <term>uncivilized</term> <note>The original German "vnmenschlichen" literally means "inhuman," but in this historical context, it refers to a perceived lack of social refinement or "civilized" customs.</note> land—in terms of the customs and character of the people—yet attained such great wisdom, skill, and high dignity, she is rightly praised far more than others. 

She lived during the era when the <term>Pharaohs</term> <note>The hereditary rulers of ancient Egypt.</note> were passing away in Egypt, and she was herself descended from their royal lineage. It is also said of her that she was a queen in Ethiopia (which is the land of the Moors), in Egypt, and in Arabia. Furthermore, she possessed great wealth on the island of <term>Meroë</term> <note>An ancient city-state on the east bank of the Nile in modern-day Sudan, famous in antiquity for its wealth and its "candaces" or queen-regents.</note>, where the waters of the Nile flow. Because of this, she possessed such a great abundance of goods, including gold and silver, that she was estimated to surpass all the world...

<meta>This page continues a biographical sketch of the Queen of Sheba (here called Sabba) and begins the entry for Pamphile. The text is a vernacular translation, likely from Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women).</meta>

...never found idle nor in womanly weakness, but always diligent and tireless in the practice of wisdom and learning the arts! So much so that she is praised in the Holy Scriptures; she is the one called **Sheba** <note>original: "Sabba"</note>. When she heard through the far-reaching fame of the world of the great wisdom of King Solomon, she felt such great wonder that she journeyed from nearly the ends of the earth, leaving behind her kingdom of Egypt and the Red Sea, traveling through the desert in Arabia with a royal army at great expense to Jerusalem to see him. 

Even the most wealthy King Solomon was struck with wonder at this woman’s great power. After she was received with great dignity and royal honors, and her many questions were skillfully answered and explained by him, she openly acknowledged that his wisdom far exceeded all human skill, intellect, and knowledge, and that it undoubtedly sprang not from human study, but from divine grace. 

Afterward, she offered the king many kinds of magnificent gifts, among which were balsam saplings <note>Balsam was a highly prized aromatic resin used for medicine and perfume in the ancient world.</note>, which Solomon later had planted by the <term>Lake of Asphalt<note>original: "vper aſaltidis"; a historical name for the Dead Sea, referring to the bitumen or asphalt that surfaces there.</note></term>. She also received gifts in return from the king and began her journey back to her kingdom. Some also say she was the Queen of Meroë, who was later called **Candace**. Because of her, all the rulers <note>The text uses the masculine "kings," though the Candaces of Meroë were famously a dynasty of warrior queens.</note> in that region were called "Candace" for a long time, just as they had previously been called "Pharaoh."

### Of Pamphile, daughter of Platea, the 42nd Chapter

**P**amphile was a Greek woman, but from which region she came has been lost to time <note>original: "hat das alter hin gefůzet"—literally "age/antiquity has footed it away."</note>. Although she is not highly praised for her noble lineage, she nevertheless increased the common good; for this, she deserves to receive some praise. For nothing new is ever discovered, however small, that is not a sign of a subtle mind, for which a person should be honored.

<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the family of Numitor and the rise of the tyrant Amulius, following the usurpation of the kingdom of Alba Longa.</meta>

by force, he drove out his brother <note>Numitor</note>. And yet, so that he would not be seen as too cruel toward his brother or stain himself with his brother’s blood, he allowed him to live in leisure, dwelling in the countryside.
But his son, Lausus, he ordered to be killed immediately, so that he would be without worry that Lausus might later drive him out and return the kingdom to his father. The young maiden, Lausus’s sister <note>Rhea Silvia</note>, he let live, but to ensure he was without fear that any offspring might be born from her—who might cause him harm in the future—he gave her to the goddess Vesta <note>The Roman goddess of the hearth; her priestesses, the Vestals, were sworn to celibacy</note> and placed her with other young women in her cloister, where she had to vow eternal chastity.

However, that she came to a man’s embrace was proven by her large belly <note>pregnancy</note>, for she gave birth at once to Remus and Romulus, the beginners, founders, and fathers of the mighty city of Rome. And because of this fault (though she was a royal woman), the children were cast out into the wilderness of beasts, and according to the ancient laws, the mother was buried alive. 

And although her body was buried, her name is highly honored in eternal memory through the great deeds of her children—a name which the tyrant <note>Amulius</note> would have gladly extinguished. When I consider this Rhea and see how many "works of Venus" <note>original: "werk veneris"; sexual acts</note> those holy clothes cover, I cannot help but mock her folly. There are some greedy people who, in order to save some inheritance, force their children into cloisters under the guise of devotion. The parents treat the young ones so harshly in their childhood days that afterwards, through flattery and the hope of an easier life, the children enter more willingly, not knowing what they are doing. And then the parents say they have given a child to Almighty God

<meta>This page continues a moral discourse on the origin of sins, transitioning from internal thoughts to external physical acts. The text is written in Early New High German (Alemannic dialect).</meta>

In the same way, the scoffers <note>original: "spēchÿ"; likely referring to those who mock or speak idly</note> who, out of ignorance of external things, allow the impurity of the kidneys <note>original: "nieren"; in medieval thought, the kidneys or "reins" were considered the seat of deep-seated emotions and secret desires</note> and the heart, unbelief, and malice; from which grow impure curses, insults, and idle words. From these grow impure deeds, which are called the **sins of the hand**, such as the impurity that leaves a person when they commit a sin. Or when one takes people by stealth <note>abduction or kidnapping</note> and throws them into prison; from which comes murder, impure limbs, and fire <note>perhaps referring to the "fire" of passion or literal arson</note>; from which many kinds of impurity grow in the heart. 

From this also grow impure smells, impure food and drink; from which grow impure embraces, from which grows impure cohabitation <note>sexual relations outside of marriage</note>; from which grows pride and those wretched, impure deeds and constant malice. From these, many impurities spring forth in the heart and the soul, even where you might have hoped for something good. From you often grow impure, public deeds of unchastity <term>unchastity: sexual behavior considered immoral or outside of religious law</term>: illegitimate births, abandoned children—not to mention those children who are secretly killed <note>a reference to the historical reality of infanticide to hide "shameful" pregnancies</note> and through...

<summary>This page describes a "genealogy of sin," illustrating how internal malice and "impurity of the heart" manifest into external crimes like kidnapping, murder, and sexual immorality. It concludes with a grim reflection on the social consequences of unchastity, including abandoned infants and infanticide.</summary>

<keywords>morality, kidneys as seat of emotion, sins of the hand, unchastity, infanticide, medieval ethics, social history</keywords>

...driven away by wickedness, of which I will not speak. And often they flee shamefully from the cloisters <note>convents</note>, those who did not wish to guard themselves with holy marriage. Therefore, the unwise should take note, from the evils of others, to prevent future harm, if they have any discernment. For no child should be pushed into a cloister unknowingly, nor while too young, nor by force: for such sacrifices of such chastity are unacceptable to God. They should be raised honorably with good morals in their father's house from childhood, until they reach the point where they clearly understand in their minds what they are doing. And they should take upon themselves the yoke of eternal chastity, poverty, and obedience with a well-considered will. And if such [willing women] are found only rarely, it would still be far better to have a small number of such virgins in the cloisters than a large number of those by whom the holy places are insulted and dishonored.

Regarding this, see Titus Livius <note>the Roman historian Livy</note>, first decade, first book, leaf 10, in the books made with printed characters <note>original Latin: "De hoc vide titum liuiū decade pma li° p° fol° x° in libris caracteribus factis." This likely refers to an early printed edition of Livy's history of Rome.</note>.

## Of Gaia Cyrilla, the 47th Chapter.

**G**aya Cyrilla <note>also known as Tanaquil</note> was the beloved consort of the Roman king, Tarquinius Priscus. It is said (although she was the wife of a Roman king) that she was never found idling in her royal hall. Instead, since she had given herself to the labor of wool-working (which in those times was honorable for noble Roman women), she showed herself so diligent in it that her name is honored even to the present day. And for her domestic diligence, she was also so esteemed during her lifetime that all the Roman people...

They established a public decree that whenever a new bride was led into her husband's house for the first time, she should be asked at the threshold what her name was, and she should immediately answer that her name was Gaia <note>This refers to the ancient Roman wedding formula *Ubi tu Gaius, ego Gaia* ("Where you are Gaius, I am Gaia"). The tradition invoked Gaia Caecilia as a patron of domestic harmony and industry.</note>, as a reminder that she should also follow the example of that same Gaia’s life. Although this custom is little valued by the ignorant, it is to the wise a great sign of the high wisdom and reason of that praiseworthy lady, which is why she is rightly not forgotten.

**T**ullia—who through her grave and evil deeds gave cause for the Roman Empire to be moved from a state of bondage <term>bondage: the original 'aigenschafft' implies a state of being subject to a tyrant or monarch</term> into a state of freedom—was born a daughter of Servius Tullius, the sixth king after Romulus, by his wife, who was herself the daughter of the fifth king, Tarquinius Priscus. She was of a high spirit and would have been worthy of praise for her sharp intellect, had she not turned those qualities toward murderous evil. 

For at that time—when her father Servius Tullius was chosen as king through the common favor of the Roman people and the decree of King Tarquinius and his wife Tanaquil—it had been foretold to them previously by the burning head of the child Servius while he lay in his cradle <note>According to Roman legend, flames appeared around the head of the sleeping boy Servius Tullius without harming him, an omen interpreted by Queen Tanaquil as a sign of his future greatness.</note>. [They] deliberated whether it would be better to kill Servius or the king; they thought it better for their purpose to execute the king. For if Servius <note>Servius Tullius, the king's son-in-law and protégé</note> died, the king would simply find another son-in-law to whom he would bequeath the kingdom, thus alienating it from them. 

And they appointed two shepherds <term>hüter|In the original Latin source by Livy, these are "pastores" or shepherds</term> who were well-practiced in throwing the axe <term>byhel|A hatchet or small axe</term> to go before the king under the guise of a legal dispute, seeking justice. And as the king turned to one to hear his case, the other threw his axe into the king's head and left it sticking in him. 

Queen Tanaquil immediately ordered all those who did not belong there to leave and closed the royal hall. With great diligence, she examined how the wounds of her dear husband might be tended, just as if there were great hope for his life. 

The murderers were caught; because of this, Aruns and Lucius <note>Aruns and Lucius were the sons of the former King Ancus Marcius, who believed the throne had been stolen from them</note> feared their evil deed would be revealed and avenged, and they fled Rome for the city of Pometia to avoid retribution. 

The queen also considered other help, should she lose hope of his recovery. She called Servius and spoke to him, lifting his right hand: "You see my husband is nearly dead; I ask you not to let the death of your father-in-law <note>original: "schwehers"</note> go unavenged, and do not let your mother-in-law <note>original: "schwiger"</note> become a laughingstock to her enemies. For the kingdom is yours, if you are a man, and not theirs who committed this murderous evil with foreign hands <note>Referring to the use of hired assassins rather than the conspirators doing the deed themselves</note>. Therefore, rise up and follow the gods who designated you king, when the heavenly fire surrounded your head in the cradle <note>A famous Roman legend where the infant Servius's head caught fire while he slept, signifying his future greatness</note>. And consider who you are, not your birth or from where you came <note>Servius was rumored to have been born to an enslaved woman, a fact his enemies used against him</note>."

### Concerning Lucretia, the 47th Chapter.

**L**ucretia, a leader of Roman womanly <term>virtue</term><note>The original German "eberkait" refers to honor, integrity, or a sense of moral duty.</note> and a holy, sublime ornament of all purity, was the daughter of a noble Roman named Lucretius Spurius Tricipitinus, and the wife of Collatinus. It is a matter of debate whether she should be praised above all other honorable Roman women more for the beauty of her body or for that of her character. 

During the time when Tarquinius the Proud <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the legendary seventh and final king of Rome.</note> lay before the city of Ardea to conquer it, she moved from the hamlet of Collatia to the city, into her husband’s house. Now, as the siege dragged on, the royal youths would gather together in company, among whom was also Collatinus. One day, after they had feasted well and perhaps the wine had gone to their heads...

<summary>This page introduces the story of Lucretia, presenting her as a paragon of Roman womanly honor and purity while setting the scene during the siege of Ardea.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legendary history of early Rome, specifically the events leading to the fall of the Roman monarchy. The previous context likely described the siege of Ardea, where the Roman princes, including Sextus Tarquinius and Collatinus, were idling in camp.</meta>

.lxx.

... fell into a conversation, speaking of their wives. And as is customary, where each man prizes his own wife as the highest in virtue and honor, they unanimously decided to send messengers riding swiftly to Rome to discover, while the wives were unawares, how they all behaved in the absence of their husbands.

When this was done, the wives—especially the most beautiful and youngest—were all found in finery among other young women, living in luxury with dancing and other pastimes. 

Immediately they turned their horses and rode to the estate of Collatia <note>original: "wÿler Collaciun"</note> to see the conduct of Lucretia. They found her adorned with no festive clothes, but in simple attire, sitting among her handmaidens preparing wool. And for this, she was esteemed by everyone (and rightly so) as the most honorable. 

Collatinus, seeing how he was honored through the reputation of his highly-praised wife, intended to honor the guests in return and invited all the royal youths into his house. And while they were well and honorably sated with ample food after a kind reception, Sextus—the son of Tarquin the Proud <term>Tarquinius Superbus; the historical last king of Rome, though the text here uses the title "Kaiser" (emperor)</term>—cast his unchaste eyes enticingly upon the honorable beauty of the chaste lady. 

Ignited by an evil-burning, foul fire, he resolved in his mind to possess her body and defile her lovely beauty. He decided that if this could not happen by her will, it must be accomplished by force. 

A few days later, driven by his mad and burning lust, he set out secretly by night and rode from the army camp to Collatia, to the house of Lucretia. She received him openly and kindly, without any suspicion of evil.

But when Sextus <note>Sextus Tarquinius, the son of the last King of Rome</note> noticed that all the household was quiet and now weighed down with sleep, he went into the bedchamber of Lucretia with a drawn sword and spoke thus: "First and foremost, Lucretia, I tell you this: if you let a single cry escape your mouth, you will be stabbed to death. Furthermore, know that I am immoderately inflamed by your beauty; therefore, I desire that you fulfill my will willingly. If that may not happen peaceably, you shall be forced to it with the sword."

But as he could not obtain this through threats—for she did not fear death if she might thereby protect her womanly honor—he devised a damnable, evil trick to stain her purity. And he said, "If you will not do as I wish, then I will kill you and a servant <term>kneckt: a male servant or stable-hand, whose low status would make the scandal even more shameful</term> with you, and I will say that I found you both sinfully together and that, out of my duty as a kinsman, I have punished the evil adultery of you both."

By such words, the chaste woman was distressed and thought in her sorrowful mind: "If I were killed in this way, there would be no one to clear me of such an accusation; therefore, it is better to yield my body to the adulterer so that I may afterwards prove my innocence through my own punishment." When he had satisfied his filthy lust, he departed from there with joy, as if he believed he had won a battle. But Lucretia was sad and heavily burdened by such a sinful evil. And as soon as day dawned, she sent for her father, Tricipitinus <note>Spurius Lucretius Tricipitinus</note>, and for Brutus, her husband Collatinus's <note>Lucius Tarquinius Collatinus</note> closest friend, who had always held her dear. She also had her other friends summoned quickly, as well as her husband. When they arrived, she told them all...

...sad and weeping over what Sextus <note>Sextus Tarquinius, the son of the last king of Rome, whose assault on the noblewoman Lucretia triggered the revolution that established the Roman Republic.</note> had committed against her the previous night. But when her friends, each and all together, began to comfort the weeping lady, she drew forth her sharp knife—prepared for this very end—which she had previously hidden beneath her garments. And she spoke: "Though I have acquitted myself of the sin, I am yet not released from the punishment. Therefore, let no woman ever take an example <term>example: the original "bůspel" refers to a moralizing precedent or warning</term> from me to live in shame after an evil deed, as I have." With these words, she plunged the knife into her innocent heart. And falling down upon it in the presence of her father and her husband, she ended her life. O, the tragic beauty of this woman! O, the pure clarity of your soul; who may praise you enough, or exalt you as highly as you deserve? No one, save he who can contemplate what you have done: how greatly you esteemed womanly honor, how strongly you valued the keeping of marriage in holiness, and what punishment you inflicted upon your own body for its defilement <note>original: "vermalgung"</note>—and yet your pure spirit never wavered.

# Of Tomyris, the Forty-Seventh Chapter

**Tomyris** was an illustrious queen of **Scythia** <note>Scythia refers to the vast region of Central Eurasia inhabited by nomadic peoples in antiquity.</note>. And because these same people lived in an uncultivated, unfruitful, and cold land situated near the mountains called the **Riphean** and **Hyperborean** <note>Legendary mountains in ancient geography, often thought to be the Urals or located in the far north</note>, and because they lived only among themselves and were almost unknown to all other races, the origins of this queen have been described all the less. 

But her name spread because she was able to rule such a rough, untamed, and wild people with such powerful authority. At the time when the mighty King of the Persians, **Cyrus**, held the kingdoms of Asia, he fell into a great desire to also conquer the kingdom of Scythia. This was more for the sake of worldly fame than for the expansion of his empire, for he had heard that the Scythians were poor and lived like cattle-herders <note>original: "vihilch," implying a nomadic lifestyle centered on livestock</note>, and yet they could never be overcome by even the greatest kings. 

Because of such desire, he gathered an army for an expedition against the royal widow, Tomyris. When she learned of his arrival—knowing well that he was a fearsome emperor of all Asia and nearly the whole world due to his great victorious battles and strong rule—she nevertheless did not flee into caves as frightened women do. Nor did she seek any mediator to ask for peace; instead, she gathered all her people, ordering them to arm themselves to resist the enemies according to their ability. 

And although she could have easily prevented his entry into her land by ship, she nevertheless permitted him to come with all his people into her land across the **Araxes** <note>The Aras River, which formed a boundary between the Persian Empire and the Scythian territories</note>. She did this because she believed he would be much more easily defeated in battle within her own land, which was known to her army, than outside of it. When she learned through certain intelligence that he had entered her land, she appointed a young man, her <insert>son</insert>... <note>The text cuts off as she prepares her response, historically involving her son Spargapises.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Tomyris, the Queen of Scythia, and describes the beginning of her legendary conflict with Cyrus the Great of Persia, who invaded her lands for glory.</summary>
<keywords>Tomyris, Scythia, Cyrus the Great, Persia, Araxes River, Riphean Mountains, ancient warfare, women in history</keywords>

[her] **only son** <note>This refers to Spargapises, the son of Queen Tomyris of the Massagetae.</note> with a third of her people to go out against him and do battle. **Cyrus**, when he learned of his arrival, considered the nature of the people and the land; and he realized that he could better overcome them through **cunning treachery** than with the sword.

And he had all the campsites sufficiently prepared with the best fare and foods, and especially with the **best wine**, which was previously unknown to those people! <note>The Massagetae were nomadic herders who typically drank milk or water; they had no natural tolerance for fermented grape wine.</note> After this, they **feigned a retreat**. And when the young man arrived at the camp with his people, he found no enemy!

Because of this, he was heartily rejoiced, as one who had turned the enemy to flight with a powerful victory. And they abandoned their battle formation and gave themselves to food, as if they had been invited to a **banquet** <note>original: "mal"</note> rather than a battle. And they filled themselves so with the unaccustomed wine that they were moved into a **heavy sleep** by it...

[...blinded by] excessive glory, she lured him into the rough, wild, uncultivated mountains and wilderness, well-known to her people but completely unknown to Cyrus and his men. There, she completely surrounded him! And by this scheme of hers, all of Cyrus’s people were slain by them. Cyrus himself could not escape; instead, the royal widow <note>Tomyris, Queen of the Massagetae, who sought revenge for the death of her son</note> sated her fierce anger with his blood. She ordered Cyrus’s dead body to be sought out from among all the corpses, and his head to be struck off and bound inside a skin <note>original: *schluch*; a leather bag or wineskin</note> full of human blood, so that he might have his fill of the blood for which he had thirsted. Because of such deeds, this widow Tomyris is rightly to be placed in human memory—and all the more prominently, as the empire of Cyrus <note>The Achaemenid Empire, which was the largest the world had seen at that time</note> was so vast.

->77<-

than others have committed. For many notable stories have often been accomplished by both evil and good men. Therefore, although she practiced shameful works <note>original: "schantlicher werk"; a reference to her life as a courtesan or prostitute</note>, her **manly** <note>In the 15th century, "manly" (manlicher) was a high compliment for a woman, signifying courage, strength, and virtue—traits typically associated with men in that era.</note> deeds should nevertheless not be forgotten. 

For in the time when Amyntas reigned in Macedonia <note>The text mentions Amyntas of Macedonia, though historically the story of Leaena is set in Athens during the reign of the tyrant Hippias.</note>, the noble youths **Harmodius** and **Aristogeiton** rose up to deliver the land from the oppression of the tyrant **Hipparchus**. And after they had killed him, an inquiry and questioning were conducted by his successor to discover who had done the deed. Because of this, many were captured, and among others, this same **Leaena**. Since many of the youths frequented her house for pleasure, she was suspected of knowing about such plots. 

And as she was harshly tortured with many kinds of torment to make her surrender those she knew to be guilty of the deed, she firmly intended to die rather than betray these youths. Nevertheless, out of fear that she might be made weak-spirited by the pain and driven to confess—and also considering how honorable and holy the name of true friendship was—after strong denial, the increase of her torments, and the growing weakness of her body, she fell into a stronger, more **manly** spirit. And therefore, to make her resolve firmer, she bit off her own tongue and spat it out. 

By this single, highly praised act, she deprived herself of all speech, whereby she knew everyone would remain un-betrayed by her. Who would say that **Leaena**, who was of such strong spirit, could be placed in such a shameful life except through the great misfortune of evil luck? Without doubt, whoever says that women can only keep secret what they do not know has not known this woman. And truly, this woman fell into loose living not out of her nature—for that was good—but out of idleness and laziness...

<meta>This page continues the historical and moral narrative regarding the downfall of a female tyrant, identified by the context as the biblical Queen Athaliah, and the rise of the rightful heir under the guidance of the High Priest Jehoiada.</meta>

...prince Jehoiada <note>original: "joadam"; referring to the High Priest who led the revolt against Queen Athaliah</note> raised up a king and dragged her from the royal throne. And through the outcry of all the people calling out against her—after she had reigned for seven years—she was executed. Thus was the evildoer sent to hell by the same path she had sent many innocents. These are the works of God’s justice. 

And although the punishment for sin may be delayed for a while, it strikes all the more harshly afterward, especially among those who, despite long pleading, refuse to turn toward reform. And if we do not consider this, if we do not wish to believe it, if we disregard all punishment but instead wish to burden ourselves with even greater guilt, then—when we least expect it—we shall be swallowed by the raging waves of the bottomless sea, where neither weeping nor crying nor our sin will be of any help. 

Shameful and utterly contemptible is all desire to rule without a lawful entry <note>meaning a legitimate or hereditary claim to the throne</note>. For without right, there are two ways to enter power, both of which are wicked and burdensome: through treachery or by force. For treachery, you must employ wicked cunning, sinful alliances, perjury, betrayal, and all manner of malice. By force, you will be constantly tormented by arrogance, insurrection, terror <term>fraiſſami: a state of extreme fear, danger, or cruelty</term>, tyranny, and the like. 

And if you wish to use such force, you will need the service of wicked men; you must then be a servant to those very people if you wish to attain the kingdom you desire. Once you have won the land, it will then be necessary for you to stop up your ears and cast down your eyes, so that you neither hear nor see the weeping and lamentation—and the thousandfold evils: killings, murder, looting, theft, the defiling of virgins, the shaming of women, and countless similar things that happen because of you.

<summary>This page describes the violent end of a seven-year tyranny and offers a moral warning against usurping power through treachery or force, detailing the spiritual and social horrors that follow illegitimate rule.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing critique of courtly life and the corrupting nature of power obtained through violence, likely following a discussion of the duties of a ruler.</meta>

[Metal clip at top center]
.75.

All human praise and honor would be turned to evil for you; out of necessity your heart must become stony to bear all this evil, with goodness shut out from it and cruelty let in. Injustice is sought and reason is driven out. All the power of justice is taken away and given over to self-will <term>aigēmůtikait: acting according to one's own whims rather than the law</term>. Simple piety is mocked and wickedness is honored—which, however, by the grace of God, is rare in the courts of our German Princes. 

**Robbery**, gluttony, unchastity, and blaspheming God are now the ornaments of the nobility. I do not mean here, but rather "beyond the sea." <note>original: "enhalb meres." The author repeatedly uses this phrase as a rhetorical shield to avoid being accused of treason by claiming he is describing foreign courts rather than the German ones he is actually criticizing.</note> Mercy is suppressed; praise comes through the shedding of the blood of the most pious, and the exaltation of the wicked—those who violate virgins, shove virtue aside, and can devise "new inventions" <note>likely referring to new forms of taxation or legal loopholes</note> to utterly ruin the poor; for these same people are now the highest at the lords' courts. 

**I** mean all this as being "beyond the sea." And generally, those who know well how to drive away peace and instigate rebellion are highly praised. 

**O** you princely entrance into power through murderous bloodshed! **Consider** what arises from that: immediately after such an entrance, the highest officials are killed or sent into exile because of suspicion. The rich are ruined, and old friends are driven away. Brothers, sisters, children, and grandchildren are all oppressed. No loyalty is found there, no justice, and no holiness; only constant concealment at all times, restless sleep, and heavy dreams. You cannot eat a single mouthful without worry, for you have cowed and driven away the pious and must now entrust your life to the wicked. 

**After** such "honors" you strive. Consider whether it would not be better for you to be content with what is yours, which you can possess peacefully, honorably, and godly, without dispute and without worry for your life. <note>This final section (lines 21-22) is highlighted in yellow in the original manuscript, marking it as the core moral lesson of the page.</note>

<summary>A scathing moral critique of the corruption, violence, and paranoia found in royal courts, contrasting the hollow "honors" of a tyrant with the peaceful life of a content man.</summary>

# Of Hippo, the 51st Chapter

[Decorative initial H]ippo was a Greek woman, but from which ancestors she descended has not been passed down to us. However, from the one powerful deed of hers that we can read about, we may reflect upon how great and manifold her praiseworthy virtues must have been! Even though "a single swallow does not make a summer," <note>A proverb meaning one instance doesn't prove a rule; however, the author argues her single act is so great she deserves lasting fame regardless.</note> she shall rightly not be forgotten. 

Once, when Hippo was captured by pirates and carried away by sea, she noticed how the robbers intended to violate her honor, for she was exceedingly beautiful. But when her graceful chastity could find no way to escape them, she leapt into the raging sea to prevent their shameful violence. By the loss of her life, she preserved her purity. 

Who would not greatly praise the stern resolve of this woman? Though she had <insert>perhaps few</insert> <note>original: "villpēcht wenigen"</note> years of life, she chose to keep her radiance and placed herself in the eternal memory of praiseworthy works, so that her fame would never be erased from the books of remembrance. 

When her body was eventually driven out of the sea by the waves, it came to the land named Erythrae. <note>An ancient Greek city on the coast of Ionia.</note> There, she was at first simply buried by the Erythraeans <note>The OCR reads "pmeonern," likely a corruption of "Erythraeonern" (Erythraeans).</note> like any other drowned corpse. But when the report arrived of the cause of her death, they built her a costly grave with great dignity upon the shore as a long-lasting memorial to her chaste spirit, recognizing that the bright shine of virtue cannot be darkened by any kind of adversity.

Titus Livius, Decade 1, Book 2, Leaf 10. <note>original: "Titus liuius. decas .i. li° .ij. folio. x°." This citation likely refers to the following chapter on Veturia, as Hippo’s story is not found in Livy.</note>
