**Anger against the son.** And what she had planned during her journey—to humbly make requests of him—she turned entirely into harsh words of rebuke when she arrived at the military camp. And as she saw him now coming toward her, she gathered great courage in her weak body and said: "Stand still, you ill-advised youth! I wish to know, before you come to embrace me, whether you approach me to receive me as a captive enemy. I believe that is your intention. Woe is me! Are these the joys for which I desired you to live—that you, sent into exile, would afterwards be seen by me as an enemy of the <term>common good</term> <note>original: "gemainen nuczes," referring to the Roman state or *res publica*</note>?"

"I ask you: do you know upon what seat you sit, thus armed? Do you know what land you have before your eyes? Recognize it, if you do not know: it is the city in which you were conceived, in which you were born, and in which you were nourished and raised with great labor. With what spirit, or by what motivation, could you bring a warlike struggle against your <term>fatherland</term> <note>original: "vatter land," meaning one's native country</note>? You have not considered what more fitting honor was shown to you by your mother during your upbringing. Has the sweetness of your wife not reminded you of your duty? Have you entirely despised kindness toward your children? And would you not accept the offerings of the elders of your fatherland?"

"Your hard heart could not yield to any plea, so that you might let go of the anger which you—though you are the guilty one—have harbored within yourself. When you looked upon this city, did you not think: 'There is my father's house; there are my gods whom I should honor; there is my wife; there are my children; there is my unhappy spirit, troubled by many misfortunes'? The best Romans, the oldest <term>fathers</term> <note>original: "vätter," referring to the Roman Senators</note>, and the highest priesthood have not been able to soften your stony heart, so that you would grant this through prayer..."

<summary>This page depicts the dramatic confrontation between Coriolanus and his mother, Veturia, as she rebukes him for leading an enemy army against his native city of Rome. She appeals to his sense of duty, family, and religious obligation to melt his "stony heart."</summary>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Veturia (mother of Coriolanus) as she pleads with her son to abandon his siege of Rome. It concludes with the withdrawal of the Volscian army and the Roman Senate's decision to honor the women.</meta>

...would do what you are rightfully obligated to do, and should do without being asked. I must lament my misery. For I recognize that through my childbearing, I have brought adversity upon myself and the entire land. Where I thought I had given birth to a son and a good citizen, I have instead acquired a wretched, stone-hearted enemy who will not be moved in any way. It would have been much better if I had never had a child; for through my infertility, Rome might well have remained spared from such strife. And I, a poor old woman, would have died a free woman in a free city. Yet no sorrow can befall me that is not more shameful for you than it is painful for me. Nor can I live much longer in my wretched misery. Consider what the future holds for your children: they must die an untimely death or remain in long bondage <note>original: "aigenschaﬀt", referring to the state of being a subject or enslaved person</note>. 

After these words followed heartfelt tears and the pleas of his wife <note>his "gemachels," traditionally named Volumnia or Virgilia depending on the source</note> and children, with desperate embraces and the great crying, sighing, and prayers of all the noble women. Thus, what could not be achieved by the majesty of the highest ambassadors <term>legates; high-ranking diplomatic messengers of the Roman state</term> or the dignity of the high priests, was accomplished through motherly honor. The great wrath of the fierce prince <note>Gnaeus Marcius Coriolanus</note> was broken and his resolve changed, so that he allowed his army to withdraw, having made peace. 

From this, out of gratitude to the women and by order of the Senate, it was decreed that on the spot where Veturia softened her son, a temple and an altar should be built for the eternal memory of the good deed Veturia performed. It was named the Altar of Women’s Fortune <note>original: "altar deß frowen gelŭks", known in Latin as the Fortuna Muliebris</note>. And although it is very old, it remains even unto our own time.

<summary>Veturia successfully shames and persuades her son Coriolanus to spare Rome, leading to the retreat of his army and the establishment of the Temple of Fortuna Muliebris in honor of the Roman matrons.</summary>

...remained whole and strong. And furthermore, it was granted to them to wear gold and head-ornaments, golden trimmings, purple clothing, necklaces, rings, and brooches <note>The original term "håfftlin" refers to small clasps or hooks used as jewelry</note> made of precious stones and gold, which previously was common only to women in the Orient <note>original: "orient"; referring to the Eastern Mediterranean and Near East</note>. And furthermore, it was granted that women could inherit, which previously had generally been denied. Whether these concessions are pleasing to men today, I will leave in the pen <note>A common medieval phrasing meaning "I will refrain from writing my opinion on this"</note>. For through such finery of women, I have seen many a downfall of men, even into poverty. And much greater and more trouble—about which I will keep silent to maintain the favor of the ladies—might I speak. Indeed, I might cast many words of blame upon Veturia <note>Veturia was the mother of the Roman general Coriolanus; she successfully pleaded with him to spare Rome from his army</note> and the great pride that arose among women from this decree, had the city of Rome not been saved through her and remained upright. Yet I cannot praise the excessive freedom of women and the increase of the same from day to day, for less would have been enough.

### Of Thamiris the painter, the 54th chapter.

**T**hamiris was the best painter in her time. She lived in the ninetieth Olympiad <note>Approximately 420–417 BCE</note>, being a daughter of Micon, the painter of Athens. She abandoned all womanly work <note>Typically referring to spinning, weaving, and domestic chores</note> and gave herself to her father's art, in which she became so skilled that in the time of King Archelaus of Macedonia, she held the highest crown and glory of the arts through a panel on which she had painted Diana <note>The transcription "dpanem" is a scribal error or contraction for "Dianam," the goddess of the hunt</note>.

## Of Artemisia, the Queen of Caria
### Chapter 55

->[Woodcut illustration: Queen Artemisia stands before a grand tomb labeled **MANSOLVS**. She holds a cup, preparing to drink, while a figure nearby tends a fire. A banner at the top identifies her as **ARTHIMESIA**.]<-

**A**rtemisia was a queen in Caria <note>An ancient region in southwestern Asia Minor, located in what is now Turkey.</note> of a high and strong spirit. She possessed a holy and surpassing love for her husband, and such firm chastity in her state of widowhood, that her memory is rightly held up as an eternal example for all widows of the future. Her husband was the most noble king in Caria, Mausolus <note>King Mausolus ruled in the 4th century BCE. The magnificent tomb built for him, the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, became one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World and gave us the word "mausoleum."</note>, whom she loved so much during his life that she could not forget him after his passing. His wonderful tomb bears witness to this devotion. For when her beloved husband had closed his final day, she had the dead body interred with the very highest royal honors. And after the cremation...

<summary>This page introduces Queen Artemisia of Caria as a paragon of widowly devotion and chastity, noting the famous tomb she built for her husband, King Mausolus.</summary>
<keywords>Artemisia of Caria, Mausolus, Widowhood, Chastity, Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, Ancient History, Women in History</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Queen Artemisia II of Caria and her profound grief following the death of her husband, Mausolus.</meta>

->80<-

...of the body, according to her custom, she did not allow the ashes to be buried in a golden urn as other royal ashes are, but gathered them with the highest diligence and kept them beautifully. For she believed no other vessel to be suitable to worthily hold them than her own breast, in which the flames of all love should never be extinguished, even after his death. And for an eternal memory of his past life, she always mixed a little of his ashes into every drink, so much so that nothing of them remained unconsumed. She spent all her remaining time in weeping and mourning, until her <term>natural moisture</term> <note>Refers to the ancient medical theory of 'radical moisture,' a vital fluid or 'humor' believed to be the source of life, which could be dried up by age or intense emotion such as grief.</note> was withered away, and she went happily into death to join her husband, as she intended. 

Yet she accomplished great works during her widowhood <note>original: "witwen ſtůl," literally "widow's chair," a term referring to her status and reign as a widow.</note>. For it was an ancient custom to erect costly tombs for kings after their death, through which one might recognize the love of their spouse during life. Therefore, Artemisia devised a wonderful and costly building, on which no expense was spared. In this, she would not be satisfied with any ordinary or poor workman, but sent for the most highly renowned masters who could be found in all the Greek lands, named Scopas, Bryaxis, Timothy, and Leochares <note>These were the most famous sculptors and architects of the 4th century BCE.</note>, whose equals were not to be found in the world. She commanded them to make a magnificent <term>Mausoleum</term> <note>A grand, monumental tomb. The word itself originates from this very project built for King Mausolus.</note> for her husband according to their highest arts, carved from marble with such labor that the memory of her dear husband would be made eternal through such a wonder. 

And it is not unreasonable—since this building surpasses all others in the entire world in cost and artistry, and is counted as one of the <term>seven wonders of the world</term>—that it should be specifically and diligently reported by us. For through public renown, this work shall flourish again, and the honorable praise of this illustrious woman shall be made clear. Thus, the master builders were summoned to Halicarnassus, the capital of the land of Caria, by the command of the queen...

<summary>This page describes Queen Artemisia II’s legendary grief for King Mausolus, including the consumption of his ashes and the commissioning of the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely introduced the death of Mausolus and the beginning of Queen Artemisia's project to build his monumental tomb, the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus.</meta>

...to form a four-sided tomb. And on the side facing **south** <note>original: "mittag", literally "midday," used to denote the south where the sun is highest</note>, they made it sixty-three **feet** <term>feet<note>original: "schůch," literally "shoes," a traditional unit of measurement roughly equivalent to a modern foot</note></term> long; the other sides were shorter. The height reached a point at one hundred and forty feet and was surrounded by thirty-six marble columns. 

The eastern part with its decoration was entrusted to **Scopas**; from the **north** <note>original: "mitternacht," literally "midnight," used to denote the north</note>, **Bryaxis**; from the **west** <note>original: "occident"</note>, **Leochares**; and the fourth part to **Timotheus**. All four displayed such great skill and applied such diligence—carving narrative histories <note>Likely referring to the famous relief friezes depicting battles between Greeks and Amazons</note>, engraving columns, and other related tasks—that the figures might be esteemed by everyone as if they were **living images**. 

This was done with the greatest earnestness and diligence by the masters, for each wished to demonstrate his art above the others and be praised for these exquisite works. However, **Artemisia** <note>Queen Artemisia II of Caria, who reigned c. 353–351 BCE and commissioned the tomb for her husband Mausolus</note> ended her days before the work was completed. Nevertheless, the masters did not abandon the work, but rather continued for the eternal memory of their singular <note>original: "sůnpchen," likely meaning unique or excellent</note> arts...

For in the works of chivalry and war, she was well-practiced and very capable, and she earned a glorious name for her majesty through the victories she won.

Specifically, on one occasion she protected the safety of her own land; on another, she was summoned to the aid of her allies. desiring her coming from the heart, and they summoned her to come to the city, pretending to surrender themselves along with the city, so that they might reach the marketplace. These things all happened according to her plan, and immediately the queen came with the hidden people out of the smaller gate <note>Artemisia had constructed a secret harbor and hidden passages in Halicarnassus to trap invaders.</note> into the wide sea, quiet and secret, unseen by the enemies. And all the ships of the Rhodians remained there, fully equipped. Immediately, Artemisia, out of her high-mindedness, thought to accomplish a bold plan; she equipped these same ships with her own armed soldiers, well-secured, and had them decorated with laurel branches and other signs of victory, and sailed quickly toward Rhodes. The keepers of the gates and the city were gladdened when they saw their own ships coming with the signs of victory, and they opened all the gates of the harbor and the city, so blinded with rejoicing that they never realized they were receiving enemies instead of friends. This continued until she subdued the unprepared citizens with a strong hand and seized the city. 

There she had the lords of the city executed <note>original: "ertören" - while literally meaning "to make a fool of," in this historical context it refers to the execution or total subjugation of the Rhodian leadership.</note> and, as an eternal sign of victory, she ordered victory columns to be set up in the middle of the marketplace. There, two bronze statues <note>original: "erin sal" - likely referring to bronze figures or monuments</note> were made, which stood for many years afterward: on one, a joyful and victorious image of Artemisia, and on the other, a sad image with a covered face representing Rhodes, signifying the remarkable history with meaningful inscriptions.

Thus she made the city tributary <note>subject to taxes or payments</note> to her and turned her way back to her home. Furthermore, when Xerxes, the mightiest king of the Persians <note>original: *perſarum*</note>, marched by land and sea against the Lacedaemonians <note>the Spartans</note> with the intention of destroying them and all the land of Greece, Artemisia was called upon for help. And she fought so boldly that one might rightly say Xerxes had traded his manhood with the woman. For if he had used his spirit and strong hand as the woman did, he would not have needed to turn his fleet to flight to escape her hand. Thus she returned home, spending the remaining days of her life in love, faithfully honoring her husband’s memory.

# Concerning Megullia, the 53rd chapter.

**M**egullia, as I believe, was a noble Roman woman. And in the holy times of simplicity <note>literally "poverty," referring to a time before Roman decadence</note> in this world's existence—before the knightly class had fallen into proud, ostentatious, and costly decoration of clothing and other luxuries—she was called *Dotata* <note>meaning "the Dowered One"</note> by the ancient Romans. This was because she was endowed for her husband by the goods of her friends with a dowry wonderfully higher than other maidens, according to the custom of that time. For *dotata* is as much as to say "one who is endowed" <note>The author is explaining the Latin root *dos*, meaning dowry</note>. Therefore, it remained a custom for many years that when a maiden was endowed beyond the common rate, she was called Megullia Dotata. And yet her wealth was no more than several thousand copper pennies <note>original: *kupfer pfening*</note>. O good simplicity, O praiseworthy poverty! How this wondrous (and sometimes mocking) course of life has now become so common in these times. For we have everywhere overstepped the right goal so much that now...

<summary>This page concludes the story of Artemisia’s military prowess against Xerxes and introduces Megullia Dotata, using her "modest" dowry to criticize the perceived extravagance of the author's own time.</summary>
<keywords>Artemisia, Xerxes, Spartans, Greece, Megullia Dotata, Rome, dowry, virtue of poverty, ancient customs</keywords>

...it is difficult, whether it be a tanner, shoemaker, carpenter, or tenant farmer, <note>The text lists common medieval trades to emphasize that the desire for wealth and status affects all levels of society.</note> or if a merchant or knight is found, who wishes to lead home a wife with such a gift <note>Referring here to a dowry.</note> as was given to this **Megulia**, who was received with such high endowments. And it is also no wonder, for common women have taken to wearing royal attire; there is not one who does not wish to be dressed in **velvet**, **golden belts**, <term>bodices</term> <note>original: "spenchÿ", referring to a specific style of jacket or ornate bodice popular in the period.</note>, **rings**, **precious stones**, and other things according to princely honors. They wear these things not only without shame, but also decadently in great pride and arrogance. I unfortunately do not know whether such arrogance has arisen because people indulge one another too much, or because of our sins—which I am more inclined to believe. For according to our desires, our haughty minds will never be satisfied.

### Of Virginia the Maiden, Chapter 56

**V**irginia the maiden of Rome, shining in kind memory, was the daughter of an honorable man named Virginius, though he was from the <term>common people</term> <note>original: "bůuel" referring to the plebeians, the non-aristocratic citizens of Rome.</note> of Rome. And although she was highly adorned by the good inclination of nature, yet her name and fame were not spread so widely by that as by the heavy story of her hard father toward her and the evil love of the <term>unbridled suitor</term> <note>original: "vnbrünstigē bülers" implying a shameful or lustful pursuer.</note>. 

For at the time when the <term>ten men</term> <note>The Decemviri, a commission of ten men established to govern Rome and codify its laws around 450 BCE.</note> chosen to rule the city of Rome had ruled for two years, this same maiden was betrothed by her father to a stern youth named Lucius Icilius, a <term>tribune</term> <note>A Roman official (tribunus) elected to protect the interests and rights of the plebeians.</note>. And because of the business of the wars then occurring, Virginius was sent out by the Romans, so that the wedding was delayed. 

While things stood thus, it happened to the misfortune of the maiden that one of the ten men, Appius Claudius, who remained alone in the city to rule with a <term>henchman</term> <note>original: "spuer gesellen," literally a tracking-companion or spy; historically this was Marcus Claudius.</note> named Spurius Appius, was moved by her great beauty into such shameful love that he wished to spare neither effort, labor, nor cost to obtain her. 

But when he noticed that the tender, blessed maidenly spirit could not be softened either by flattering words, prayers, or threats, nor through bribes or great gifts; he also considered

### About Irene, daughter of Cratinus.

**I**rene was a daughter of Cratinus, the painter. And she is praised and honored all the more because she became more skillful than her master; indeed, her name is still celebrated among painters. The great mastery of this same Irene is proven by a painting of a maiden on a panel she painted, located in the city of **Eleusis** in **Greece** <note>original: "Eluſina in krichen"</note>; likewise, a portrait of the elder **Calypso**, and of **Abstireni** the wrestler <note>likely a corruption of "Alcisthene" or "Aristomenes," subjects attributed to Irene in classical sources</note>, all of which were made with such mastery that it is a strange and wondrous thing to hear of coming from a woman. And for many long years, these were held to be works of great art, which is why I did not wish to pass her by without praise.

### About Leontium, Chapter 59.

**L**eontium, if I have considered it correctly, was a Greek woman who lived during the time when **Alexander the Great** reigned. She was highly honored, yet she would have been much more highly esteemed for her sharp mind if she had maintained womanly shame and feminine modesty. <note>Leontium was an Epicurean philosopher, but historical accounts often focused on her reputation as a courtesan to discredit her intellectual work.</note> The ancients write of her that she was so highly learned in all arts that...

--- Page 193 ---
She, through female audacity—or perhaps out of a particular hatred—audaciously dared to write reprehensibly against the greatest master of nature living at those time, **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC) was a Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school.</note>, seeking to wound and weaken him and his art. Since her writings have survived through so many years until our own time, it is well to remember that she must have been a mistress of great arts! Though she was envious! And she was not of low birth, but born of noble blood.

But her dishonorable character darkened her shining blood and her enlightened art. For she was entirely given over to impure unchastity. Oh, foul evil! Her common dwelling was among free-spirited knaves, pimps, rascally boys, open adulterers, and common women, situated in the midst of their shameful houses! Wherein all feminine modesty and physical purity were trodden underfoot and shamefully drowned in secret privies <note>Original: "priuet". These were private latrines or sewers, symbolizing the depth of her moral degradation.</note>. Oh, how much more it is to be lamented and pitied if such great and noble art could be defiled by such filthy hearts! Which, however, is not the case. 

But it would be a great question whether that same **Leuntia** <note>Leontium was a Greek Epicurean philosopher who famously wrote a treatise against Theophrastus; she was often slandered as a prostitute by later historians.</note> is to be judged more powerful because she, through physical temptation, could drag such high natural knowledge into such a sinful state—or whether philosophy is so weak that it would allow itself to be locked within such an unchaste heart.

# About Virginia, wife of Lucius, Chapter 62

**V**irginia was one of the highly honored women among the Romans. Although she was a fellow citizen like the one described previously <note>This refers to the Virginia whose story appeared in the preceding chapter, known for the tragedy involving Appius Claudius.</note>, her father was of the high nobility. Because of the merit of her constancy, she deserves to be exalted above other women living at that same time. Therefore, I wish to describe one of her stories; it is so beautiful and praiseworthy that from this single account, her entire life may be clearly recognized. For this reason, she is rightly counted as one of the highly distinguished women. 

It is well known how in Rome, at the cattle market—now called the Rotunda <note>The translator likely refers to the round Temple of Hercules Victor in the Forum Boarium.</note>—there was a temple built in praise of Hercules. Within it was a special chapel which was dedicated to **Patrician Chastity** <note>original: "patricie pudicie"</note>; that is, the purity and constancy of the noble mothers. 

According to a specific law of the city’s powerful rulers, only the most noble Roman women from the oldest lineages—those who remained within their class and had not lowered themselves through marriage to a commoner—dared to enter there to confess their sins and purify themselves. However, into all other temples, the other women were permitted to go to cleanse their hearts and offer prayers to their goddesses. 

It so happened that Virginia went with the other women to bring offerings into the chapel. For this, she was rebuked by the other women because she was married to a man from the commonality <term>Plebeian|The social class of Roman citizens distinct from the noble Patricians</term>. Even though he had been **Consul**—which is the same as the highest ruler—during the previous year, and was named Lucius Volumnius, a somewhat shameful uproar arose in the temple, which grew even greater afterward.

<summary>This page introduces Virginia, a noblewoman whose marriage to the plebeian Lucius Volumnius caused a scandal in the Temple of Patrician Chastity. It highlights the strict social hierarchies governing religious life in ancient Rome.</summary>

<keywords>Virginia, Lucius Volumnius, Patrician Chastity, Rome, Temple of Hercules, social class, Consul, Plebeian, nobility</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Virginia (Verginia), a Roman noblewoman. In the preceding context, she was likely excluded from the Temple of Patrician Chastity because she married a man of the plebeian (common) class, despite her own noble birth.</meta>

For Virginia, moved in her spirit, went out and spoke: "Now I am constant in honor and from the highest lineage, yet I would be unjustly driven out from this temple because I am wedded to one of the common people <note>The "gemaind" refers to the plebeians, the non-noble citizen class of Rome.</note>, who yet, through his virtue and good deeds, has achieved so much that he has been elected to the highest authority of this city." <note>Virginia was married to Lucius Volumnius Flamma Violens, who was elected Consul, the highest office in the Roman Republic.</note> Following these words, she performed a most praiseworthy work. For as she had many houses lying next to one another in the long street where she lived with her husband, she considered setting aside enough space in one place as would be necessary for such a temple. That same part she separated from the rest of the houses and established an altar therein, and called to her the honorable women of the common people. And she told them of the arrogance of the nobles and complained of the insult shown to her; and she spoke thus about it: 

"O my dearest ones, I beg you earnestly to see how the men of this city strive daily with all their might for virtue and honor; so that you also should diligently pursue the highest adornment of women—that is, womanly honor and constant purity—and apply yourselves and strive bravely <note>The original text uses "manlich" (manfully/stoutly), which in this historical context suggests a strength of character or courage usually attributed to men.</note> for it; so that this temple and altar, which I have built to the goddess <term>Pudicitia</term> <note>original: "pudicicie"; the Roman goddess of modesty and female chastity.</note> for the women of the common people, may be highly praised and lauded for preserving honor and purity, even above the temple of the nobles; so that one may see from your good and pure lives that holy souls are not poured only into noble bodies, but into everyone who wishes to direct their life toward the good according to reason." 

O worthy and holy words of this noble woman! O praiseworthy indignation and good intention, which rightfully should be raised to the heavens with rejoicing! She does not seek to incite rebellion of wives against their husbands; she does not seek the beauty and elegance of the body...

And without doubt, if one drove the disreputable women away from the sacrifice of the altar, many scandalous women would still be found who would withdraw themselves from such wedded frivolity and take up a more honorable life, remaining therein without reproach.

**F**lora was a Roman woman. And as much as shameful gain took away from her feminine adornment <note>meaning her moral reputation</note>, just as much did fortune spread her name further abroad. ### What the marks signify and how one should read accordingly. The [C] chapter.

**F**or <note>The original uses a large decorative initial 'V' for the word 'Um', meaning 'For' or 'In order to'</note> a better understanding of this little book and others that I have translated from Latin into German <note>original: *uß latin gedůtſchet* — literally "Germanized" from Latin</note>, it is to be noted that many different kinds of punctuation marks are used by various writers. Now it is to be known that three kinds of points are commonly used in all speech, providing different levels of meaning. These are: such a **virgule** <term>virgule: a forward-slanted stroke (/), the ancestor of the modern comma, used to mark a brief pause</term>, which hangs slightly; a certain little point or dot with a stroke drawn beside it, thus <note>the text describes what we would recognize as a colon or semicolon</note>; and a little point or dot like this **.** 

The first little stroke is called a **virgula**; it signifies that several words are placed together correctly and in the proper order, but they do not yet conclude a complete, understandable thought. The second little point is called a **comma** <term>comma: from the Greek *komma* (a piece cut off); used here to describe a mark indicating a completed thought that requires further context</term> thus **.** <note>The manuscript likely displayed a specific mark here, such as a point with a tail</note>. It signifies that an understandable thought has been concluded, but more is attached to it which further expands the meaning, and 

<summary>This page introduces a chapter on punctuation, explaining the names and functions of three marks—the virgule, the comma, and the period—to help readers navigate translated texts.</summary>
<keywords>punctuation, grammar, translation, virgule, comma, literacy, book production</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the conclusion or afterword of a work printed in Ulm, likely by the famous early printer Johann Zainer. It focuses on the technical aspects of the book's production, specifically punctuation and orthography.</meta>

.140.

...placed; namely, at the end of the lines, where a word is divided, and one part of the word ends the line and the other part begins the next line. Although some now place a small point in the middle of the line instead of the virgule <term>virgule: a diagonal slash (/) used as a comma in early printing</term> like this: **.** and for the comma they set two small dots like this: **:** and for the period <note>original: "periodum"</note> a little point pulled downwards like this: **;** or like this: **;** yet I have kept to the first system in this little book, insofar as it has not been changed by the printers. 

I have also written some words without distinction to aid the understanding of various dialects; for instance, I use "sie" <note>the word for "she" or "they"</note> as a single syllable. And I use "spe" for two syllables or more. I also use a short *dʒ* <term>dʒ: a common medieval abbreviation for the word "das"</term> where the word is not stressed <note>acting as a conjunction like "that"</note> and a long *das* where it refers to something specific <note>acting as a demonstrative like "this" or "that"</note>. 

An example of this usage is: "I would *that* <note>dʒ</note> you would take *this* <note>das</note> my little book with gratitude; *that* <note>das</note> would bring me the highest joy." The book is full of examples of this instruction; therefore, it is not necessary to write further on the matter, so as to avoid being long-winded. Instead, let us offer praise, thanks, and honor to God, who has shown us the grace to finish this little book.

->Finished blessedly, at Ulm, by
**Johann Zainer of Reutlingen.**<-

...confused and impure works (as is the habit of such harlots <note>original: "dirne"</note>) enticed towards her, so that she became more inflamed in evil love, so strongly that she might easily consume <note>original: "ge:pſſẽ," likely *gepissen* or *genossen*, meaning to use up or enjoy</note> all their goods. And as soon as one fell into poverty, he was abandoned and another taken up; through this she gained such exceedingly great wealth that few could equal her.

The prize was to be a good supper and a beautiful mistress <note>original: "bülen," a term for a lover or paramour</note> to sleep with him that same night. Whatever he threw with the right hand should count for Hercules, and the throw of the left hand for himself. It so happened that Hercules won. 

Therefore, the guardian brought Flora to him and prepared a costly meal for him. But as Flora slept in the temple, Hercules appeared to her in a dream <note>original: "trom"</note>, as if he were practicing physical <note>original: "lÿpplicher," literally "bodily"</note> works with her, and afterward said to her: "The reward for this night you shall receive from the one who first meets you in the morning as you go out." 

When the very powerful youth called Fanucius <note>In classical sources, this figure is usually named Tarutius</note> met her, he loved her dearly in his heart and led her home to his house, where she lived with him in flattering love so long that he made her the heir to all his goods; and he did not live long after that. Thus she was made rich. Although some say that Accia Laurencia <note>Acca Larentia, the legendary foster-mother of Romulus and Remus</note>, who raised Romulus and Remus, was made rich in this way. But that does not matter, as we know that Flora was an exceedingly wealthy harlot <note>original: "hür"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the history of Roman customs and figures, specifically focusing on the origins of the Floralia festival, following the moralizing tone typical of 15th-century German chronicles.</meta>

And when the time of her fatal end arrived, and she left no son behind—desiring through female <term>cunning</term><note>The original German "wÿpplichem list" implies a strategic or deceptive cleverness attributed to women in the literature of this period.</note> to make her name and memory eternal—she appointed the Roman people as heirs of all her current <term>landed and movable property</term><note>In the original, "ligenden vnd farnden güter" refers to real estate and personal possessions.</note>. However, this was done under the condition that every year a solemn festival should be held on the day of her birth, celebrated by the <term>common women</term><note>A historical euphemism for prostitutes, as the subject, Flora, was a legendary wealthy courtesan (meretrix).</note> using the wealth she had bequeathed.

She was also able to gain renown through her riches, and the day was celebrated annually with shameful behavior and the lustful acts of harlots, scoundrels, and other frivolous people. It reached the point where such a festival was observed every year with unchaste pleasure and joy, as if it were the holiest of events. The festival was called the **Floralia** after this same Flora. This continued until the Senate began to feel ashamed of such disgraceful celebrations and sought ways to abolish them through manufactured pretexts—though this was against the will of the rough commoners. How exactly that occurred is not fitting for us Christians to put into writing, and therefore I will leave it out.

# About Romana the young woman, Chapter 64

**R**omana was a young woman who, at the time of her great and virtuous deeds, lived such that she is rightly to be placed among the highly honored women of worthy memory; although the name of her lineage has been lost to antiquity. <note>This story is a famous Roman legend known as "Roman Charity" (Caritas Romana), which served as a primary example of filial piety—the devotion of a child to a parent.</note> She had a mother of noble descent, but she was unfortunate because she was condemned to death before the judge (I know not for what crime). And she was delivered by the chief magistrate to the jailer in prison, so that he might execute her by night, because she was of noble birth. 

But the jailer, when he was supposed to kill her, felt a certain pity for the noblewoman, so that he could not bring himself to kill her with his own hands; and so he locked her alive in a prison cell with the intention of letting her starve to death there. It so happened that Romana, through earnest entreaty, was permitted to enter the prison to see her. However, she was first thoroughly searched to see if she brought her any kind of food against the prohibition. There, the virtuous daughter gave her hungry mother her own milk to suckle, of which she had plenty at that time, for she had recently given birth to a child. 

She did this daily for so long that the jailer began to wonder how the condemned woman could live for such a great length of time. And watching what the daughter did when she was with her mother, he saw how she nourished her mother from her own breasts. Having witnessed the daughter's great kindness toward her mother, the magistrate reported this further to the <term>pretor</term> <note>original: "pretor"; a high-ranking Roman official responsible for the administration of justice.</note>; that same pretor (or mayor) reported it to the entire council. And it was unanimously granted that the guilty, condemned mother should be released to the daughter as a reward for her kindness. 

O, the great goodness of this daughter! For a...

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Romana, a young woman who saved her condemned mother from starvation in prison by secretly nursing her with her own breast milk. Upon discovery, the Roman authorities were so moved by her filial devotion that they pardoned the mother.</summary>
<keywords>Romana, Roman Charity, filial piety, imprisonment, breastfeeding, mercy, Roman law, pretor, motherhood</keywords>

If a citizen rescues another in battle and saves his life, he was laudably 
crowned by the lord with an <term>oak wreath<note>original: "aichim lob"; the *corona civica* or civic crown was the second-highest military decoration to which a Roman citizen could aspire, awarded for saving the life of a fellow citizen.</note></term>. But what praise can we

on any tree. This is not only a holy kindness, but a miraculous one. 
And it is not only to be compared to natural gifts, but placed 
above them. For nature teaches us to nourish children in their 
tender youth because of their frailty; but this woman rescues 
her mother from death. Therefore, she is rightly honored as a 
leader of virtuous children.

...goddess, or Diana, were driven, or came into it through a vow, and thus were required to maintain purity in consecrated garments. This Marcia, with her whole spirit, overcame the sharp sting <note>literally "hook," a metaphor for the enticing nature of temptation</note> of the enticing flesh, against which even the most prominent men often could not struggle; and she kept her body unstained by any blemish until death. 

And although this Marcia is highly praised for such steadfastness, she is nonetheless no less to be lauded for the exercise of her senses and reason, as well as her great art and the use of her hands. For (whether this skill came from masters or from nature, one does not know), because she completely despised typically feminine works of little use <note>likely referring to domestic tasks like spinning or weaving</note>, she gave herself so entirely to sculpting and painting that in painting with the brush and in carving figures from ivory <term>ivory: "helfandbain," literally "elephant bone"</term>, she far surpassed Sophopolis and Dionysius <note>original: "Sopolim vñ Dÿonÿſium," famous Greek painters mentioned by Pliny the Elder</note>, the best painters of that time. 

And although good painters were found from time to time, none was ever seen who was so skillful in that art as she was. As a greater testament to her skill, she produced a portrait that was preserved for a long time after her death, in which she had depicted her own likeness with such proportions <term>proportions: "lidmǎß," meaning the measurement and symmetry of the limbs</term> and colors that no one who had ever seen her could fail to recognize her form. 

And to relate some of her particular customs: she had the habit of painting only female figures or carving them in ivory, and seldom or never male figures. I believe that maidenly shame was the reason for this; for since ancient times it was the custom to make almost naked figures. Her opinion was that if she did not create the naked male figures with the correct proportions, she would be noted for her ignorance; but if she [depicted them accurately]...

### Of Sulpicia, the wife of Fulvius, Chapter 66

**Sulpicia** was once the most honorable woman, by the testimony of all the most noble Roman women. She maintained her **chastity and honor** with no less conviction than **Lucretia** <note>A legendary figure in Roman history who became the ultimate symbol of womanly virtue after taking her own life to preserve her honor.</note>, who killed herself to provide proof of the chastity in her heart. She was the daughter of Semis <note>A variant of the name Spurius Paterculus.</note> and the wife of Fulvius; both were from the most noble Roman families.

This refers to Venus Verticordia, a localized Roman aspect of the goddess tasked with turning women's hearts away from lust and toward marital virtue.</note> according to the decrees of the Sibylline Books. This was done so that maidens and other women might not only turn away from unchastity, but also so that they might more easily [attain] feminine...

# **Of Harmonia, Daughter of Gelon of Sicily, Chapter 67**

**A**rmonia of Sicily was a daughter of Gelon, king of the Syracusans <note>Original: *spracusarum*. Gelon was a famous ruler of Syracuse in the 5th century BCE</note>, whose brother was named Hieron. And although she was born of illustrious nobility, she is remembered much more for her great, faithful goodness. For at that time, the Syracusan people staged an uprising and, raving insanely against the king and all his lineage, blindly raging, they swore to put them all to death. And now that the three royal youths—Hieronymus, Andronodorus, and Themistius <note>The transcription reads *thenscius*, likely a corruption of the name Themistius</note>—had been killed by them, they also sought to kill the daughters of King Hieron, Demarata and Heraclea. They also came for Harmonia, the daughter of Gelon, armed with bare swords for her death. 

But it happened, through the arrangement of her governess <term>governess (zůchtmaisterin): a woman in charge of the education and moral conduct of young noblewomen</term>, that another lady-in-waiting was dressed in all the finery, ribbons, and garments of Queen Harmonia, so that she might die in her place. This girl offered herself willingly to carry out everything: so much so that she did not flee the sharp swords of the killers, nor would she say that she was not the person they sought to kill. Nor did she ever reveal the hidden Harmonia, for whom she was being killed; instead, silent and unmoved, she received the deadly stabs and died blessedly in great loyalty—though she suffered a wretched, evil death. 

But the dear Harmonia, while she was hidden, nevertheless saw the faithful constancy of the innocent maiden and her strong spirit even unto death, as her chaste blood spilled from her wounds. Harmonia was rightly very shocked by this; and although she might have escaped after the departure of the ruffians <note>Original: *wietrich*, meaning tyrants or bloodthirsty, cruel men</note>, she nevertheless began to reflect inwardly through the great goodness of her heart, and with tearful eyes, marveled at the...

<summary>This page tells the story of Harmonia, a Sicilian princess whose life was saved by a loyal lady-in-waiting who disguised herself as the princess to be killed by a mob. Harmonia is moved to tears and internal conflict after witnessing the servant's sacrificial death.</summary>

was greater, or that of the one who followed;

->.lxxxv.<-

This is, without doubt, wonderful to relate, and far more praiseworthy when said of a woman than of a man. For Alexander the Great, the conqueror of the world, is most highly extolled by the ancient writers, particularly for his <term>liberality</term> <note>The original term "miltikait" refers to the medieval virtue of noble generosity, a key trait for a successful ruler.</note>; it is noted that he did not merely endow his friends with small gemstones, money, horses, and the like, but instead honored even those he had conquered with great principalities, mighty kingdoms, and vast empires. 

Oh, how beautiful, how noble this appears in him! Yet, in my opinion, it is not to be compared to the praiseworthy and great deeds of Busa <note>Busa of Canusium was an Apulian noblewoman who provided food and clothing to thousands of Roman soldiers fleeing after their defeat by Hannibal at the Battle of Cannae (216 BCE).</note>. For Alexander was a man, whereas Busa was a woman—and women are generally, and by nature, more <note>original: "zācher," meaning tough or tenacious, here implying a tendency toward stinginess or being tight-fisted.</note> frugal, and more inclined to receive than to give. Furthermore, she was a mistress of her own private property, whereas he [Alexander] took by force from others that which [he bestowed...]

<summary>This page compares the legendary generosity of Alexander the Great with that of the noblewoman Busa, arguing that Busa’s charity is more remarkable because it came from her own resources and defied contemporary gender stereotypes.</summary>

<keywords>Alexander the Great, Busa of Canusium, liberality, generosity, gender roles, virtue, ancient history</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the virtuous use of wealth, likely following a section on the vices of greed or the proper management of a household.</meta>

.xxxvj.

...used well. For nature did not draw and bring forth gold from the earth so that it should return back into it—as the greedy do, who dig it up and then keep it with such great anxiety, as if it were meant to be buried <note>The original "geboren" literally means "born," but the context suggests the gold is being "re-buried" or "re-born" into the earth by those who hoard it.</note> once more—but rather so that it might be serviceable for the common good. It should, in an honorable manner, be helpful to us and our friends for the daily needs of life. And if there should be any surplus, it should be shared with the unfortunate, the ruined, the poor, the unfairly oppressed, prisoners, the sick, and all others in need. This should be done generously from a free spirit, not for fame or worldly honor, but simply to provide help; not for profit, but out of pure goodness. And yet, this giving should be done with such discretion <term>discretion: the quality of being careful in one's actions; here, it refers to the prudence needed to balance charity with one's own financial stability</term> that we do not fall into poverty ourselves by giving too much, which would then force us to rely on the help of others.

.86.

->**On Sophonisba, Queen of the Numidians. Chapter 68.**<-

**S**ophonisba, <note>Original: "Sophonilba." A Carthaginian noblewoman whose life was defined by the conflict between Rome and Carthage.</note> though she was illustrious and born of the royal stock of the Numidians <note>original: "numidarum"</note>, became much more widely renowned through the harsh severity of her death, which she brought upon herself. She was the daughter of Hasdrubal <note>original: "astrubalis"</note>, whose father was named Gisco <note>original: "gisgan"</note>; he was the highest Prince in Carthage during the time when Hannibal was ravaging the Italian lands <note>original: "wälsche land italia," referring to the Romance-speaking lands of the Italian peninsula.</note>.

When she had grown into her blooming years, she was given in marriage by her father to the powerful Syphax, King of the Numidians. This was done not only to create a kinship between the royal houses, but also so that the father might withdraw the King and his aid from the Romans, with whom he had previously been allied. Through the charms of his daughter, the father hoped to make the King an enemy of the Romans. And so it happened: for once the wedding festivities were over, the King was so drawn to and ensnared by the lady’s lovely flattery and great beauty that he was inflamed with love. He believed that nothing could be dear, pleasant, or joyful to him except for his wife alone.

While the unfortunate king was burning with this passion, it happened that the Roman general <term>Cornelius Scipio</term> <note>Scipio Africanus, one of the greatest Roman generals, who eventually defeated Hannibal.</note> intended to cross from Sicily into Africa with his army. Sophonisba was warned of this by her father, and with flattering pleas and caresses, she moved her husband Syphax's mind to her own will and desire. She persuaded him not only to abandon the Romans, to whom he was bound by oaths, and pledge himself to the Carthaginians, but he even became the chosen leader of this foreign war. Thus, having broken the faith and the promises he had recently made to Scipio, he opposed him through letters regarding his crossing into Africa.

<summary>This page recounts how Sophonisba, daughter of the Carthaginian general Hasdrubal, used her beauty and influence to persuade her husband, King Syphax of Numidia, to defect from Rome to Carthage. It portrays her as a powerful political figure who shifted the alliances of the Second Punic War through personal charm.</summary>

<keywords>Sophonisba, Syphax, Numidia, Carthage, Second Punic War, Hasdrubal Gisco, Scipio Africanus, political marriage, betrayal, Roman history</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Second Punic War, specifically the Roman campaign in North Africa led by Scipio Africanus and the downfall of King Syphax.</meta>

### LXXXII.
<note>Chapter 82</note>

But Scipio the Younger, <note>Scipio Africanus, the Roman general who eventually defeated Hannibal.</note> being of a high spirit, condemned the villainy of the unfaithful king <note>Referring to King Syphax of Numidia, who had broken his alliance with Rome to join Carthage.</note> and did not encamp his army far from Carthage. He intended first to drive out the faithless king, which was indeed accomplished by King Masinissa <note>A Numidian prince and Roman ally.</note> and his companion Laelius, who had been sent with him by the Romans. Through them, the king's army was scattered, and he himself was captured, bound, and led away before the city of his royal seat <note>The city of Cirta, Syphax's capital.</note>. There, he was weighed down with chains and displayed to his own citizens, by whom the city was opened and surrendered to the Romans. 

But before Laelius arrived there, amidst the sudden upheaval and great turmoil, Masinissa entered the royal hall in full armor. There he saw Queen Sophonisba <note>The daughter of the Carthaginian general Hasdrubal Gisco and wife of Syphax.</note> coming toward him, contemplating her misfortune. And when she saw him—standing out among the others, of noble stature and well-adorned in weapons and armor—she immediately thought he was the king <note>Sophonisba likely recognized him as a high-ranking leader or kingly figure among the victors.</note>. She fell at his feet, considering how she might move him to mercy through gentle speech.

And she spoke thus: "It is by the providence of God and your own good fortune that you are able to do all things according to your will with us, who were kings only a short time ago. Yet, if it is permitted that a captive might raise a prayerful voice before their victorious conqueror and lord regarding life and death, and touch his knees and victorious hand, then I, your subject, beg your majesty (in which state I myself was shortly before). I implore you by your name, your kingdom, and your entire lineage—and pray that you may enter this realm with a better destiny than that with which Syphax has departed from it—that you would deal with me, an unfortunate woman, in a way that may be deemed good and merciful in your eyes. I ask only this: that I may not be handed over alive to the impetuous and harsh Romans, especially as a Carthaginian. For you can easily understand that I am an enemy of the Romans, being a daughter of Carthage, the child of Hasdrubal, and the wife of Syphax..."

<summary>This page depicts the capture of King Syphax by Masinissa and Laelius, followed by the surrender of his capital city and the desperate plea of Queen Sophonisba to Masinissa to avoid being taken prisoner by the Romans.</summary>

...justifiable care. And if all other ways are closed, then I desire that I may die by your hand, and that I may not be led alive into the power of my enemies. This I ask with inward pleading. 

Masinissa was also from Numidia and quick to amorous desire, as are all others of that same land <note>Ancient Roman and medieval authors often stereotyped North Africans as having particularly passionate temperaments.</note>: he was moved by the great beauty of the lady! She had also made her misfortune appear lovely, through which he received the prayers of the unfortunate lady more kindly and humanely. 

And before Laelius <note>Gaius Laelius, a Roman general and close friend of Scipio Africanus.</note> arrived, Masinissa offered the lady his right hand and lifted her up from the earth, amidst the howling and weeping of the other surrounding women. And immediately he betrothed her to himself as his lawful wife, and held the wedding feast amidst the din and turmoil of weapons! By this he believed he had found a way of mercy for his amorous desire and for the prayers of Sophonisba.

The next day after that, Laelius arrived by command [of Scipio]. Masinissa marched with him, accompanied by all royal finery and other spoils—and also with his new spouse—back into the army camp. There they were first well received by Scipio, for they had well executed what had been commanded of them. 

Afterwards, when Masinissa was rebuked <note>Scipio was displeased because Sophonisba was technically a Roman prisoner of war, and Masinissa had no right to marry her without Roman consent.</note> with kind words because he had betrothed a prisoner of the Romans to himself, he went exceedingly sorrowfully from both of them into his tent. When he was sated with sighing and weeping—so much so that those nearby could well hear his lament—he summoned one of his servants whom he knew to be faithful. He ordered him to prepare a strong poison, according to the fate of the unfortunate Sophonisba. 

And he commanded him that he should mix it into the drink, bring it to her, and say this: the loyalty and promise that he had willingly given her, he would gladly keep for her if he were able; but since his own will... <note>The sentence breaks off here and continues on the next page, as Masinissa explains that he is forced to obey the Romans.</note>

<summary>Masinissa, moved by Sophonisba's beauty and pleas, hurriedly marries her to protect her from the Romans, but is rebuked by Scipio. Realizing he cannot keep her, Masinissa despairingly prepares a cup of poison for her to fulfill his promise that she would not be taken alive.</summary>

...even if it were broken by others: yet he wished to grant her prayer that she would not be led alive into the power of the Romans. As soon as she heard this, she spoke with a manly bearing <note>A common historical trope praising a woman's courage by calling it "manly" or "masculine."</note> to the servant: "Thus I receive the morning gift <note>original: "morgen gäb"; a traditional gift from a husband to his bride on the morning after their wedding night. Yet tell him: death would have been easier for me had I remained unchanged at the time when I was to become a corpse <note>Likely referring to her status before her second, hasty marriage to Masinissa, which led to this tragedy.</note>." She gave no sharper words, but received the drink and without any trembling she drank it all up; and she did not swell for long, but soon fell into the death she had desired. 

In truth, it would be a great and wonderful thing to say even of a man of many years who was weary of life and had no hope but death, if he should go so fearlessly into death. I say nothing of a royal maiden who has only just come to know the goodness of human life and being, and is only beginning to taste its sweetness.

->**Concerning Theoxena, daughter of Prince Herodicus, Chapter 70.**<-

**T**heoxena has, through her sweet goodness on the one hand and through mortal strength <note>original: "mortlicher ſtrenkhait"; can also imply severity or a rigorous resolve unto death.</note> on the other, given great testimony to be remembered. She was the daughter of Herodicus, the prince in Thessaly, at the time when Philip <note>Philip V of Macedon (238–179 BCE).</note>, son of Demetrius, reigned in Macedonia. She had a full sister named Archo, born of the same father, who was also killed through the malice of that same Philip.

->THEOSENA<-

...and henceforth through constantly growing treachery, he <note>Referring to King Philip V of Macedon (r. 221–179 BCE), who became increasingly paranoid and murderous toward his relatives.</note> had both husbands of these sisters miserably stabbed to death; yet each of them left a son with his wife. When they were thus placed in widowhood <note>The original text uses "witwen stůl," literally "widow's chair," a legal term referring to the social and economic status of a widow.</note>, Archo first married a prince of her people named Porridis, and bore many sons by him. 

But Theosena, although she was strongly pursued by many powerful lords, nevertheless maintained her state of widowhood longer with a steadfast spirit. However, when Archo passed away, Theosena was moved by compassion for her sister’s children and feared that they might fall under the power of another stepmother, or be raised by their father with less care. Therefore, so that they might be kept as her own children, she took the same Porridis as her lawful husband; for at that time there was no law against such a marriage. And she began to raise them in the same manner as if they had been born of her own body, so diligently that one could clearly recognize that she had taken this man more to serve the children than for herself.

<meta>This page continues the account of King Philip V of Macedon’s increasing tyranny and his preparations for war against Rome. The previous context likely described his confiscation of property for his own use.</meta>

...if he had taken it for his own benefit. At that same time, it happened that the same King Philip, driven by his restless spirit and mind, again planned to stir up war against the Romans at a time when they were flourishing in fortunate prosperity. And to protect himself from the power of the Romans, he had all his citizens driven out of his cities in Thessaly (that is Macedonia) <note>In the 2nd century BCE, Philip V of Macedon reorganized his kingdom, forcibly relocating populations to strengthen his borders</note> into the land of Emathia. He then garrisoned his cities with people from Thrace, whom he considered to be more warlike and more loyal to him. Because of this, he received no small amount of resentment and words of reproach from those who had been expelled. 

He also turned his thoughts to the young boys whose fathers he had previously put to death. He believed that his land could never be secure as long as they were not all killed. He commanded that they be captured and cast into the dungeon, and not killed all at once, but rather one after another over time, so that he might hold a lingering terror <note>original: "mengen schrekē"</note> before the people. 

Theoxena <note>A noblewoman from Thessaly whose husband and family had been victims of Philip's purges</note> was warned of this terrifying royal command. She reflected on the death of her sister and the wicked murder of her husbands. She also considered what would happen to the children if they fell into the power of the king: that he would not only torture them, but also publicly shame and mock them. To avoid this, she immediately set her mind upon a desperate and terrible deed, and spoke to her husband: she said she would rather boldly kill all the children with her own hand than suffer to see the children led into Philip's power. Poris, her husband, perceived the lady’s murderous intent; to comfort her and to help the children, he promised her that he would flee with her and the children to loyal friends where they would be safe. Immediately, he made it known to all the people that he had to make an annual pilgrimage to Aeneas <note>The mythical Trojan hero and legendary founder of the city of Aeneia</note>, the first founder of the city of Aeneia, to bring an offering with his...

with his wife and children. They had shipping prepared for this purpose and sailed away. But when they had arrived there, and had now completed the day with festive dignity, at midnight—when everyone was overcome with heavy sleep—he rose up with his wife and children, went into the ship with the sailors, and commanded them not to sail back home to Thessaly, but to sail to Eburea <note>Likely Euboea, an island off the coast of Greece</note>, as he had planned before setting out. But when the king’s servants, who were stationed to guard the shore, saw the ship moving quickly from the land, they suspected their flight and intended to detain them. And they set out with a well-equipped and armed ship, with such orders and commands that they should not return without the fleeing ship. When Porris saw them coming toward him, he considered the coming distress and the peril in which he stood; and he urged the mariners to row with all their might, as fast as each one could. Then he prayed to the gods to send him help in his distress.

...we all have neither comfort nor help; thus, no safety is ordained for us, nor can we save ourselves <note>original: "rechen," meaning to extricate or deliver oneself</note> except through death alone. For this, you have these two paths: the swords and the drink <note>poison</note>. Each may choose which he prefers, provided that we may escape the haughty pride of the king! Therefore, dearest youths, show your noble spirit! And let each manfully take up the weapons or drink the poison, while the raging sea still offers us an end to life. While she spoke thus, the king’s servants had now come close to them.

**V**nd <note>The letter V is a large decorative red initial</note> as the youths hastened to fulfill her command, she—the fierce instigator of such a deadly deed—began to force and urge them on. And when this was done, and the children now writhed in the throes of death from the drink and the wounds, Theoxena commanded that they be thrown from the ship into the sea.

**D**o <note>The letter D is a large decorative red initial</note> she, who had raised her children so tenderly, preferred to have killed them rather than have them led into the king’s <term>servitude|Early New High German 'aigenshafft' refers to the state of being a 'bondman' or property/slave of another</term>, so that one might note how greatly and laudably she valued freedom, and how utterly she despised servitude. Then, with a steadfast heart, she embraced her husband (who was then praying) and leaped with him into the raging sea, choosing rather to die in freedom than to languish in base servitude. Thus they left the ship to their enemies and deprived Philip <note>Philip V of Macedon</note> of his deadly designs against them. And so Theoxena created an eternal memory for herself through her noble courage.

# BERONICE

->• 51 •<-

### Of Beronice, the Queen of Cappadocia, the 71st chapter.

**B**eronice <note>Also known as Laodice of Cappadocia; medieval texts often varied the spelling of classical names.</note>, who was also called Laodice, was a daughter of Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI of Pontus, one of Rome's most formidable enemies.</note>, the King of Pontus. She has established her nobility in the eternal memory of mankind through her singular vengeance for the death of her children. 

For after she was married to the king in Cappadocia named Ariarathes and bore him two sons, both were killed by her own brother (also named Mithridates) through treachery disguised as kindness. This occurred just as her father <note>The text here refers to the complex family assassinations typical of the Mithridatic dynasty.</note> before them had ended his life by the brother's command through Gordius. 

Upon this, she did not desist; she set aside her womanly spirit and took up manliness <term>manhait</term> <note>In this context, "manhait" refers to the medieval virtue of "fortitudo" or courage, often framed as a woman adopting male-coded bravery.</note>. She prepared her chariot and marched with an armed hand, in the strength of an army, against the killers of her husband and children. She did not stop until she had sent them to hell with her own hand. 

O good God! O exceedingly great motherly love for children! What greater thing could happen than that a fearless woman with armed hand should overcome those by whom Asia and nearly all of Italy <note>The text likely refers to the vast reach and threat of the Mithridatic Wars which shook the Roman Republic.</note>...

<summary>This page recounts the story of Queen Beronice (Laodice) of Cappadocia, who took up arms to avenge the murder of her husband and sons by her own brother, Mithridates.</summary>
<keywords>Beronice, Laodice, Cappadocia, Mithridates, vengeance, female warriors, Boccaccio, medieval history</keywords>

->CHIOMARA THE WIFE<-
<note>The Latin caption "CHIOMARA VXOR" identifies the subject of the illustration as Chiomara, a noblewoman of Galatia known for her dignity after being captured by Roman forces.</note>

### Of the wife of Orgiagon, Chapter 72

**A**lthough the proper name of this woman—who was a spouse of King Orgiagon <note>The text spells this "drigigontis," a variation of Orgiagon, a chieftain of the Galatian Celts</note> in Galatia <note>original: "gallen kriechen," referring to Gallo-Graecia or Galatia in modern-day Turkey</note>—is unknown to us <note>The author notes the name is unknown in the text, despite the woodcut's caption, reflecting the use of different historical sources</note>, yet her excellence and the great deed and revenge for the violation of her pure spirit should not be unjustly forgotten. For her virtue and strength of spirit may well be described under her husband’s name. 

At the time when the great Antiochus—a king of all Asia and Syria—was defeated by the Romans through Scipio Asiaticus, and the command was then handed over to the Roman leader Gnaeus Manlius <note>Gnaeus Manlius Vulso, a Roman consul who led the campaign against the Galatians in 189 BCE</note>, this same Manlius did not wish to let the great Roman army lie idle. He marched against the fearsome Gallo-Greeks and defeated them so powerfully that they surrendered and acknowledged him as their lord.

<summary>This page introduces the story of Chiomara, a Galatian noblewoman, beginning with the historical context of the Roman conquest of the Gallo-Greeks under Gnaeus Manlius Vulso.</summary>

<keywords>Chiomara, Orgiagon, Galatia, Gallo-Greeks, Gnaeus Manlius Vulso, Antiochus the Great, Roman Republic, female virtue, revenge</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Roman campaign against the Galatians (approx. 189 BC), specifically focusing on the capture and subsequent revenge of Chiomara, the wife of the tetrarch Orgetorix, following the Roman victory at Mount Olympus.</meta>

.91. <note>Page number 91</note>

But those who were able to escape fled into the wilderness and high mountains to protect themselves; however, they were driven out from there, and a very great multitude was captured, consisting of both men and women. Among these, the queen <note>Chiomara, wife of the Galatian noble Orgetorix</note> was also seized. 

Now, when the captain of the captives—who had been appointed by Manlius <note>original: "manleo"; referring to the Roman consul Gnaeus Manlius Vulso</note>—noticed the beauty and blooming youth of the queen, he was inflamed with such inordinate lust that, forgetting Roman honor, he violated the queen by force <note>original: "notzwangen"; a term for rape or forced carnal knowledge</note>. Because of this, she was so deeply burdened and so greatly troubled in her heart that she spent all her time thinking more of how to avenge this evil than of how to free herself from her captivity. Yet, she waited quietly for the proper time to accomplish this. 

When the money arrived to ransom the prisoners, she grew fervent, and the anger within the chaste heart of the lady was renewed. And as soon as she was released from her bonds, she considered the path of her revenge. She went with her own people to a certain place and commanded them—speaking in their own language <note>the Galatian/Gaulish tongue, which was unknown to the Romans</note>—that as soon as the captain demanded payment of the money and had set his mind and eyes upon the gold, they should kill him instantly and cut off his head. 

This was all carried out according to her plan. She immediately took the head into her lap <note>referring to the folds of her garment used as a pouch</note> and went away successfully, in accordance with her careful forethought. And when she had returned home and come before the face of her husband, she recounted all the shame that had been inflicted upon her. She threw before his feet what she had brought in her lap and said, "Lord, look upon..."

She was the primary historical model for "defiled yet pure" womanhood in this era.</note> The prison still stood before her, and the chains—which were deadly weapons around her—yet she thought more of avenging the defilement <term>vermålgung; meaning the staining or pollution of one's reputation or body</term> of her body than of being released from prison. In the 15th and 16th centuries, describing a woman as having a "manly spirit" was a high compliment, suggesting she possessed courage and strength of will usually reserved for men.</note> into such great strength that she firmly commanded her servants to avenge the crime committed against her upon the rapist. Where could you ever find a bolder person, a more manly prince, or a harsher judge over evildoers? 

She would rather be captured again and choose death than return to her husband in unavenged disgrace. She also believed her defiled body could only be vindicated through great deeds and by demonstrating the purity of her soul; for through this, womanly discipline and honor are maintained, and that which was lost is restored, thus giving testimony to a pure soul. This is what women should look toward if they wish to strengthen the purity of their souls. For it is not enough to testify to the purity of the heart with tears, cries, and lamentations, and to show the violence suffered through words alone, but also, where possible, to carry out vengeance.

## **Concerning Tertia Aemilia, the wife of the first Scipio Africanus, the 74th chapter.**

**A**lthough Tertia was highly ennobled by her birth into the Aemilian lineage <note>original: "emiliorum"</note>, and also by her marriage to the most illustrious Scipio Africanus the Elder <note>original: "deß ersten"</note>; nevertheless, she appeared far more illustrious because of her own great character <note>original: "grossen geschicht," referring here to her personal conduct and story</note>. For, although this same Scipio, when he was younger, sent home a captured maiden—who was of blooming age and of surpassing beauty—to her husband, the prince Allucius <note>The text reads "lucceio," a reference to the Celtiberian prince Allucius. While "süberkait" usually means purity or cleanliness, in this context it likely refers to the "refined" or "excessive" nature of his lustful desires</note> lustful desires.

# CLAUDIA QUINTA

This woman always devoted greater effort to the particular adornment of her body than to any of her other activities. Because she was always seen to be too extravagantly and elaborately dressed compared to all others—even the most noble Roman women—she was judged by the most honorable and eldest Romans to be not only weak-willed and lacking in virtue, but she was even suspected of being shamelessly unchaste <note>original: "vnlutkait"; this refers to a lack of purity or ritual chastity, a serious social stigma for a Roman woman of her rank.</note>.

And in the days when Marcus Cornelius and Publius Sempronius <note>Marcus Cornelius Cethegus and Publius Sempronius Tuditanus, who served as Consuls in 204 BCE.</note> ruled in Rome—which was during the fifteenth year of the **second Roman war against Carthage** <note>The Second Punic War, the famous conflict involving the Carthaginian general Hannibal.</note>—it happened that the Mother of the Gods <note>The goddess Cybele, also known as the *Magna Mater* (Great Mother).</note>, a goddess of the Romans, was at that time being brought to Rome along the Tiber River.

To receive her, the man judged to be the very best in the city, named **Nasica** <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio Nasica, who was selected by the Senate as the most virtuous citizen to welcome the goddess.</note>, was sent to the riverbank by the Senate of the entire city, accompanied by all the noble Roman women. When they arrived there, the ship carrying the sacred relic <note>original: "hailtum"; referring to the black stone or statue representing the goddess Cybele.</note> became so firmly stuck on the riverbed that the sailors could not move it forward in any way. However, when all the young men, using all their strength...

Among the other women was Claudia; <note>Claudia Quinta (3rd century BC), a Roman matron who became a legendary figure of chastity.</note> recognizing her own virtuous heart, she fell down on her knees, humbly praying to the goddess <note>Cybele, the "Great Mother" (Magna Mater), whose cult was being brought from Phrygia to Rome.</note> that if the goddess recognized the purity of her heart, she would then let the ship follow her belt.
