And it is better that we let them think evil against us than that we actually commit evil. <note>This concluding sentiment reflects a common Stoic-Christian theme in medieval literature: that internal virtue and God’s judgment matter more than public reputation.</note>

-> ### Of Hypsicrathea, Queen of the Great Sea, the 75th Chapter. <-

**H**ypsicrathea was the spouse of the great Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI of Pontus (135–63 BCE), a famous enemy of the Roman Republic.</note> and a queen of the sea, very beautiful and possessed of such unconquerable love toward her husband—so great that her name has become renowned <note>original: "gezwiget," literally "branched out" or spread abroad like a tree.</note> because of it. For her husband Mithridates was in constant, great wars against the Romans; and although he had many wives according to pagan custom, she nevertheless was ignited so fervently by the torch of love above his other wives that she never wanted to leave him, neither at sea nor on any military expeditions; for she believed no one could attend to him but she alone. She also knew the service of servants, the majority of whom are unfaithful. And although to accomplish such a thing is rightly difficult for a queen, she nevertheless set her mind firmly upon it. And so that she might do it more appropriately, she had her gold-colored hair cut off, laid aside her rings, brooches, and other feminine adornments, and covered her head with a helmet, and clothed her whole body with a <term>cuirass</term> <note>a piece of armor consisting of a breastplate and backplate fastened together.</note>, <term>coat of mail</term> <note>flexible armor made of interlinked metal rings.</note>, leg armor, and down to her feet in knightly fashion. Thus she exchanged golden rings for bows, precious clasps for shields, veils <note>original: "kükel," referring to a woman's hood or headcovering.</note> for lances, and soft pillows <note>original: "pfulgē," from the Latin 'pulvinus'.</note> for the hard saddle; so that immediately, a gentle, softly-living queen was seen transformed into a stern knightly form.

<summary>This page describes the transition from a discourse on virtue to the story of Queen Hypsicrathea, who famously disguised herself as a warrior to remain by her husband Mithridates' side during his wars with Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Hypsicrathea, Mithridates VI, Queen of Pontus, marital devotion, armor, gender transformation, Roman wars</keywords>

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Hypsicratea, Queen of Pontus, known for her extraordinary devotion to her husband, King Mithridates VI, during his wars against Rome.</meta>

-> . 105 . <-

And she went out, in diligent service with her husband, over rough mountains and valleys, always tireless, dwelling in caves and wildernesses—she who before had lived in a soft bridal chamber. She also never allowed herself to be driven from him by any counsel, whether in victory or in flight. She was also <term>manly</term> <note>The original term "manlich" refers to the classical virtue of *fortitudo* or courage, which was considered "manly" in the 15th century even when displayed by a woman.</note> enough to see and hear all the works of military strife—wounds, bloodshed, and killings—and to endure them without fear. This was so true that when her husband was nearly captured by <term>Gnaeus Pompey</term> <note>original: "gneo pompeio"; Pompey the Great (106–48 BCE), the Roman general who finally defeated Mithridates.</note>, she never wanted to leave him, but followed him with a few of her friends through Armenia and many regions and wildernesses, to comfort him and incline him toward good hope.

O sweet sanctity of this marriage! O bottomless power of true friendship! With what great strength you have fortified the mind of this woman; and no doubt no woman has ever suffered so much for her husband! Therefore, it is not unreasonable that if the ancients praised her works, those who come after should wonder at them.

But for this exceeding great service, she received an unseemly reward. For after he had come home again, in anger he ordered the death of their son whom she had conceived by him. For this reason, he was also hated by the Romans and driven to the main castle of his kingdom, which his son <term>Pharnaces</term> <note>Pharnaces II of Pontus, who led a rebellion against his father, Mithridates.</note> also besieged with the help of the Romans, to drive him out and end his fury against his children and friends. But when Mithridates realized the destruction of his kingdom, as well as the downfall of himself, his wives, concubines, and children, he had Hypsicratea—who had done him so much kindness—killed with a poisoned drink, so that she would not survive him! 

But without doubt, that was a great ingratitude of Mithridates. Yet he could not diminish the honor and glory of this woman; though he took her mortal body with the poison of an untimely death, her name is not silenced, and through the permanence of the written word, it shall nevermore be extinguished.

<summary>This page recounts the legendary devotion of Hypsicratea to King Mithridates VI, detailing her hardships in war and her eventual tragic death by his hand to prevent her capture.</summary>

# Concerning Sempronia, the Roman woman: Chapter 76

**S**<note>The original manuscript features a large initial 'S' decorated with a green dragon or serpent.</note>empronia was far surpassing many other noble Roman women in her beauty. And in her wit and reason she was so great that her equal could not be found. For in the Greek and Latin tongues she was so masterfully learned and practiced that she could create and compose artful poems in both languages. Whatever she heard or saw, she grasped quickly. Furthermore, she was so elegant in her speech that, through her fine delivery, she could move anyone to jest or seriousness, to joy or to sorrow—especially toward sensuality and vain desires <note>The original German terms "waichmütikait" and "vpplich begird" suggest a soft-heartedness or luxury and idle, perhaps lustful, desires, reflecting the historical moralizing about her character.</note> whenever she wished, as she was particularly inclined toward those things. She could also sing praiseworthily to all stringed instruments <term>stringed instruments: likely referring to the lyre or cithara</term>, a skill that is well-suited to a young woman when it is practiced rightly and becomingly.

<summary>This page describes the intellectual and artistic talents of Sempronia, a noble Roman woman known for her mastery of languages, poetry, and music, despite a noted inclination toward luxury.</summary>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Sempronia, likely from Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women), focusing on the contrast between vice and virtue in Roman women.</meta>

->.106.<-

...of mind. Likewise, excessive generosity <note>The original term "miltikait" refers to the virtue of liberality, but here the author warns of its excess.</note> is almost to be condemned. For whenever such wasteful generosity possesses a stingy heart (as is the nature of all women) against its own nature, then no hope of well-being can be expected, other than falling into poverty. Honor and property are completely given away, for they cannot stop until they have reached the greatest disgrace and poverty. Women should be frugal; and it is their duty to faithfully preserve what has been earned by men. And just as much as the excessive kindness and wasteful generosity of women is to be scolded, so much is the diligence of women praised for keeping well the wealth earned by men. For such diligence virtuously increases wealth; it blessedly honors the household; it is a true witness of a whole and faithful mind; a reward for labor; and a strong foundation for descendants.

Yet let the whole history of this Sempronia <note>This refers to Sempronia, a woman involved in the Catilinarian conspiracy, described by the historian Sallust as talented but morally bankrupt.</note> be entangled in an evil end. At the time when the outrageous and wicked Catiline <note>original: "kathelina"; Lucius Sergius Catilina, a Roman senator who attempted to overthrow the Roman Republic.</note> had planned destructive discord against the Romans with many others, she mixed herself among them to better fulfill her unchastity. But when he and his followers—through God's grace and the help of Cicero <note>original: "ciceronis"; Marcus Tullius Cicero, the consul who suppressed the conspiracy.</note>—were suppressed along with his accomplices, and some of them were killed, she was struck down with a shameful death among them. 

There was another Sempronia, a daughter of Titus Sempronius Gracchus and Cornelia (daughter of the great Scipio Africanus), and a sister of Tiberius and Gaius <note>The Gracchi brothers, famous Roman reformers.</note>. She was of such a steadfast mind that she would rather die than allow herself to be forced by the judge to take a man who was not her equal <note>This likely refers to an incident where she refused to acknowledge an impostor claiming to be her brother.</note>. Of this, I shall leave off writing and be brief.

->**THE CIMBRIANS**<-

**Of the women of the Cimbrians <note>original Latin: *cimbrorum*</note>, who are the Kurwalhen <term>Kurwalhen: An archaic term used by the translator Steinhöwel to identify the Cimbrians with certain Germanic or Celtic-speaking peoples</term> or, as some would have it, the Flemings; the 77th chapter.**

[Illustration: A hand-colored woodcut depicting four women and two children. Three women are shown hanging from gallows. In the center, a woman holds a child, while another child lies on the ground. This scene illustrates the mass suicide of the Cimbrian women after their defeat by the Romans.]

**V**oice has never heard of a greater, more constant, more praiseworthy, faithful, or more heavenly-exalted devotion of wives <note>original: *gemahellschaft*</note> than that of the great number of women who remained widows after the slaughter and flight of their husbands, the Cimbrians <note>original: *Cimbrorum*</note>—whom we call Kurwalhen—which occurred in a hard-fought battle against Gaius Marius the Roman. For as much as their number was greater, so much more are they to be highly praised; for while we read of individual women who remained in the proper protection of chastity, we have never or rarely heard of so many being gathered together for such a purpose.

In the times when the Romans were in their flourishing reign, the Germans—who are called Cimbri in Latin <note>original: *zelatin genemet werden*</note>—and several other hostile regions rose up and swore an alliance against the Romans so powerfully that no one thought it possible to withstand them. They also brought with them their wives, children, and old household goods on wagons, and fell upon them suddenly...

<summary>This page introduces the Cimbrian women, praising their collective loyalty and chastity following their husbands' defeat by the Roman general Gaius Marius. It sets the scene for their mass suicide, which was seen by Boccaccio and his translator as a supreme act of marital devotion.</summary>

But these women, with steadfast spirits, chose to keep their honor even unto death, so that through them the glory of their people would not be diminished.

# Concerning Julia, the daughter of Gaius the Emperor; the 78th chapter

**A**lthough Julia was, by her lineage and her marriage <note>Julia was the daughter of Julius Caesar and the wife of Pompey the Great; her marriage was a key political alliance in the First Triumvirate.</note>, one of the most illustrious women in the entire world...

<meta>This page continues the history of the Roman Republic, transitioning into the life of Julius Caesar and the tragic fate of his daughter Julia, who was married to Pompey the Great. It follows previous accounts of Roman leadership and genealogies.</meta>

.118.

Gaius Julius <note>original: "gap july" — the text refers to Gaius Julius Caesar, here titled "the Emperor" by the 16th-century author, though he was technically Dictator.</note> the Emperor, and Cornelia, whose father was Cinna <note>Lucius Cornelius Cinna, a dominant Roman politician who served as consul four times.</note>, who was four times the highest Roman official, of the kind they called <term>consuls</term> <note>the two chief magistrates of the Roman Republic.</note>. This same Julius had his origin from the most ancient Trojans—Aeneas and his heirs—descending through many kings and other rulers. 

But Julia <note>the daughter of Julius Caesar.</note> was married to the great Pompey, who at that time was the most illustrious Roman. In the governing of the state and in victorious battles against many lands, against kings, princes <note>original: "p’ch" — an abbreviation for *Fürsten* (princes).</note>, and monarchs of the whole world—whom he powerfully established, deposed, or newly elected according to his will—nothing like him had been seen before. Through this, he could easily win and keep the eternal favor of all Romans. 

The high-born lady Julia (even though she was young and he was older) loved this same Pompey so passionately that she met an untimely death because of it. For at one time, as Pompey was offering a living sacrifice in the temple according to their custom, and as the animal was struck while in his hands, it thrashed so violently back and forth from the wound that it heavily splattered his clothes and stained them with blood. Therefore, he sent the clothes home to put on others. By chance, the pregnant <note>original: "schwåger" — meaning pregnant; historical accounts note Julia was pregnant at the time of this shock, which led to a fatal miscarriage.</note> Julia encountered the servant who was carrying them. 

As soon as she caught sight of her husband’s bloody clothes, before she could even ask what had happened, she fell into a terrible suspicion that some violence had befallen her husband. Immediately her vision failed her, as if she could not bear to live after the death of her most beloved husband. With clenched hands and a stifled heart, she suddenly fell down and gave up her spirit, to the great grief not only of the Romans but also of the whole world.

<summary>This page describes the lineage of Julius Caesar and the tragic death of his daughter Julia, who died from shock after seeing her husband Pompey's blood-stained clothes.</summary>

# Of Porcia, daughter of Cato of Utica, Chapter 79

-> [Illustration: A hand-colored woodcut depicting the assassination of Julius Caesar in the center, with Brutus and Cassius stabbing him. On the left, Porcia is shown kneeling, swallowing hot coals. On the right, she is shown again kneeling by a fire. Labels in the image: • BRUTUS • CASSIUS • JULIUS • BRUTUS • at the top; PORCIA on the left and right sides.] <-

**P**orcia was the daughter of Cato <note>Marcus Porcius Cato Uticensis, a Roman statesman famous for his Stoic virtue and opposition to Julius Caesar</note>, who was a follower of Pompey. During the time when Cato led the remains of Pompey’s army out of Egypt through the burning heat of the sun across Libya into Africa—and upon hearing that Julius Caesar had fought and defeated Pompey with great force—he became so unwilling to endure Caesar's victory that he killed himself at the city of Utica. For this reason, he was called "Uticensis" <note>original: "vticensis," a Latin title meaning "of Utica"</note>. 

This same highly noble woman never wavered from her father’s strength and steadfastness. To say nothing of her many other illustrious virtues: she held her husband Brutus <note>Marcus Junius Brutus, one of the primary assassins of Caesar</note>, to whom she had been given by her father, in such great honor and love—so completely and with such purity—that her devotion to her husband far surpassed all other womanly diligence and care. She also could not hide the proper flames of love in her heart when a fitting time and place presented itself; she opened her spirit with

...yet they did not go unpunished. For although things went according to their will for a time, the murderers were nonetheless condemned by the remaining part of the Senate, because of which they were forced to become fugitives <note>The German "landrůmig" implies being driven from the land or becoming outlaws</note> and were scattered into many lands. But Brutus and Cassius traveled with a great multitude to the East, intending to set themselves against Octavian the Emperor <note>Referencing Octavian, later known as Augustus, though he had not yet formally taken the title of Emperor at this point in the history</note> and Antony, who were the heirs of the Emperor Julius. Octavian and Antony led great armies against them, fought against them, overcame them, and scattered their host. 

When Portia <note>Portia Catonis, the wife of Brutus, known for her devotion to her husband</note> heard of this, she considered that death was no harder to suffer than the wounds she had once endured from the falling knife <note>This refers to an earlier episode where Portia stabbed herself in the thigh to prove she was strong enough to keep Brutus's secrets</note>. And she quickly returned to her old resolve; and as she sat by the fire, and because she could not find any kind of weapon to kill herself as quickly as she desired, she undauntedly took the burning coals in her hands and threw them into her throat. She so burned her gullet and throat that her heart was suffocated from the lack of breath. Who would doubt that, as strange and unusual as this death was, so much greater is this love for her husband to be esteemed, and the more worthy of eternal remembrance.

### Concerning Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius, the 80th Chapter

**O**rtensia was a daughter of the eloquent Hortensius <note>Quintus Hortensius Hortalus, a famous Roman orator and contemporary rival of Cicero</note>, and she is rightly to be highly exalted with worthy praise; for she not only kept her father's arts <note>Specifically the art of rhetoric and public speaking</note> in her mind, but knew how to speak them forth with a clear voice—so elegantly, so masterfully according to the true art, and so lovely and receptive—that it would often have been difficult even for many of the most highly learned in that same art. It happened that...

When the Roman government was placed under the rule of three men, it happened that these same men—during a time of necessity concerning the common good—imposed a large sum of money to be paid by the noble Roman women. They were so burdened by this that even their clothes and jewelry would not have been sufficient to pay such a tax. They could also find no man to entreat, for no one was willing or dared to speak a word against the decree of the three men to mitigate the burden. Only Hortensia was so daring that she undertook to act as an advocate for the women, shielding them before the three men with a courageous spirit. She spoke so well, so graciously, and so effectively that those who marveled at her believed her father, Hortensius <note>Quintus Hortensius Hortalus (114–50 BCE), a famous Roman orator and rival of Cicero. ## Concerning Sulpicia, wife of Truscellio, the 81st chapter.

**S**ulpicia, the noble Roman woman, is rightly placed among other illustrious women because of her great love for her husband. For at the time when the three-man rule <term>Triumvirate: A political regime dominated by three powerful individuals, in this case Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus</term> governed in Rome, her husband Truscellio <note>original: "truſtellio"; historical sources usually refer to him as Lucius Cornelius Lentulus Cruscellio</note> was also proscribed <term>proscribed: To be officially declared an outlaw, whose property is confiscated and who can be killed without trial</term> and condemned by them. With a quick flight, he escaped to Sicily; there he remained in misery and poverty. When Sulpicia became certain of this, she strengthened her resolve, willing to suffer hardship and toil alongside her spouse. For it seemed quite unseemly to her to sit in great honor, to live in luxury, and to maintain a happy existence while her husband perished in misery and hunger. 

Yet it was not easy for her to carry out her intention, for she was guarded with the greatest diligence by her mother, Julia, to prevent her from following her husband into his wretched state. But there was never a guard so great that true love could not deceive it. Therefore, she waited for a suitable time for these things and put on the clothes of a servant; taking with her no more than two maids and as many men-servants, she deceived the watchfulness of her mother and the other guards. She left her father's house and all her possessions and followed after the misery of her spouse—even though, according to the established laws, she could have been divorced from that man and could have celebrated a wedding with a new husband and lived in high honor. But this illustrious woman...

<summary>This page concludes the story of the orator Hortensia and begins the narrative of Sulpicia, who disguised herself as a servant to escape her mother's guard and join her exiled husband in Sicily.</summary>
<keywords>Hortensia, Sulpicia, Truscellio, Roman history, Triumvirate, exile, marital loyalty, Julia, Sicily</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on feminine virtue, likely concluding a biography of a devoted wife (such as Sulpicia, who followed her husband into exile) before beginning the life of Mariamne.</meta>

.111.

...would rather, out of special love for her husband, disregard the storms of the sea, endure the heat of the burning sun, and not be frightened by the wilderness of the deserts, mountains, and valleys. She would travel through many unknown regions and suffer thousandfold worry and anxiety to find her husband, who had been cast out by fortune, rather than spending her time in her house in luxury, soft-heartedness <note>The original term "waichmütikait" refers to a delicate or weak-spirited nature, contrasted here with "manly" strength.</note>, and a gentle life. This is not evidence of a womanly spirit, but rather a strong indication of manly, illustrious wisdom. 

It is not always best to appear in gold, gemstones, and other ornaments, nor to always behave with courtly manners in a gentle life, nor always to flee the heat of the sun or the snow and rain of winter. It is not always best to indulge in the desirable pleasures of the bedchamber, but also to suffer toil, labor, and poverty with a strong spirit alongside the men whom the wheel of fortune <note>The "Wheel of Fortune" (Rota Fortunae) was a common medieval and Renaissance metaphor for the unpredictable nature of fate.</note> has driven into misery. 

This is the true knighthood <term>rittischafft: the conduct and virtues expected of a knight, applied here to women's spiritual struggles</term> of women; these are the battles, these are the highest victories through which they overcome soft-heartedness. By this, they become illustrious, highly praised, and honored, because they overcome physical lust, a gentle life, and pride with steadfastness, honor, and purity. From this comes to them higher renown and eternal glory.

### Concerning Mariamne, the 82nd Chapter.

Mariamne the Jewess was a daughter of Aristobulus, the King of the Jews, born of Alexandra, the daughter of King Hyrcanus. She was possessed of such great, unseen beauty that she not only surpassed all the most beautiful women of that same time, but was also esteemed by everyone more as a heavenly image than a mortal one, as even Mark Antony, a triumvir <note>original: "dryman"; a member of the Second Triumvirate of Rome</note>, well testified.

<summary>This page concludes a moral reflection on the "chivalry" of women who endure hardship for their husbands and begins the biography of Mariamne, the Judean princess renowned for her transcendent beauty.</summary>

<keywords>Mariamne, Aristobulus, Hyrcanus, Mark Antony, feminine virtue, Wheel of Fortune, Judea, steadfastness</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
For Mariamne had a brother by the same father and mother named Aristobulus, who was of equal beauty and age with her. And when Alexandra desired from King Herod that he should grant that same Aristobulus the high office of the priesthood, she sought to achieve this—following the advice of one of her friends—by having the likenesses of both children masterfully depicted by the best portrait painter and sent to the unchaste <term>Triumvir|One of three leaders of the Roman Republic; here referring to Mark Antony.</term> Antony, then in Egypt. She did this to move him to physical desire, so that her request might be granted all the sooner. When Antony looked upon them, he stood for a long time in wonder before them! Afterward he said: "These are truly, in terms of beauty, the children of God; and I have never (never once) seen anything not only more beautiful, but even their equal." 

But to speak of Mariamne alone: although she was radiant with unheard-of beauty, she appeared far more so because of the great strength of her spirit. For when she had come to marriageable years, she was wedded to that wretched man Herod, son of Antipater, King of the Jews; she was at this time quite unhappy! Yet he loved her excessively for her delightful beauty. And he was determined that he alone should have possession of this heavenly beauty, which caused him to fall into such anxiety that no one else should ever share in her, so much so that he planned to ensure she would not outlive him. 

And to accomplish this, when he was first summoned by Antony to come to Egypt to explain why he had killed Aristobulus, Mariamne’s brother; and also afterward, when he was summoned by Octavian <note>The future Emperor Augustus.</note> after Antony had died, to answer for why he had sent aid to Antony against him—he left her with his <note>original: "baſe" — This term usually means a female relative (cousin or aunt) in Early New High German, but here refers to Herod's mother, Cypros.</note> Cypros and several of his other friends. [He ordered them] that if they heard anything from Antony or Octavian, or anything else...

...how it happened that, should things lead to his death, they were to kill Mariamne immediately and without delay, so that after his death she would belong to no one else, as Josephus describes more fully. And although this had been done very secretly, it was nevertheless made known to Mariamne.

And since she had already conceived an unspeakable hatred against Herod because of the murder committed against her brother <note>Herod had her brother, the High Priest Aristobulus III, drowned in a pool at Jericho</note>, she well realized that her husband loved her for nothing other than to perform carnal acts <note>original: "üppige werk", referring to sexual intimacy</note> with her. And she was moved to even greater anger when she thought of how he had twice ordered her to be killed, should he perish at the hands of Antony or Octavian <note>referring to Herod's political precariousness during the Roman civil wars between Mark Antony and Augustus</note>. She carried this so heavily <note>literally "melancholically"</note> in her heart that she no longer wished to be obedient to her loving husband in carnal acts, although she had borne him two noble youths, Alexander and Aristobulus. 

She also began to despise him in many ways and to speak shamefully against him, saying openly and proudly before everyone: Herod was not worthy of his power; he was a man of common birth <note>literally "un-born," meaning he lacked the royal Hasmonean bloodline Mariamne possessed</note>; he was no Jew, but an Idumean <term>Idumean: a person from Idumea (Edom). Herod's family were forced converts to Judaism, leading many Judean aristocrats to view him as a "half-Jew" or usurper.</term>, and unworthy of a royal wife. Furthermore, she called him a violent, unfaithful, murderous, and inhuman tyrant <note>original: "wůtrich"</note>—attributing everything to him in the harshest way. 

Now, although Herod bore this with difficulty, his fervent love forced him to endure these and other insults from her without punishment. But at last, as these things grew daily in bitterness, Herod’s mother <note>Cypros</note> and his sister Salome (who were always at odds with Mariamne) thought to tame her. --- Page 256 ---
Herod’s cupbearer accused Mariamne <note>Mariamne I (c. 54–29 BCE) was the second wife of Herod the Great. Their marriage was famously volatile, ending in her execution.</note> to him, claiming that she had asked him to help her give Herod certain drinks she had prepared for him. Furthermore, he claimed she had recently sent her portrait to the high Roman leaders to entice them into an illicit love, intending thereby to drive Herod out. When Herod was told these things by the cupbearer, he easily placed faith in them, for the turbulence of her words and deeds had already shown him great ill-will. He was harshly provoked by this and ignited with such an anxious rage that he accused her before all his friends, and by the common counsel of that same group, he condemned her to death as one who had grievously sinned against the <term>royal majesty</term> <note>Lese-majesty or treason against the crown.</note>.

When she heard this, her noble spirit showed itself, so that she completely despised death. With an untroubled face, boldly and with a man’s courage, and with dry eyes, she looked upon all the others who were weeping. She did not merely face it without trembling, but even eagerly, like one who had triumphed in her struggle. Abandoning all hope of life, she stretched out her neck for the executioner to strike off her head. By doing so, she has piled up more years for her memory than she could have earned if she had obtained a stay of her life by begging Herod for mercy. Moreover, she secured an eternal vengeance upon Herod, who, because of his envy, was forced to live forever after in mourning.

### Of Cleopatra, the 84th Chapter

**C**leopatra was an Egyptian woman. Although she traced her lineage through many kings back to Ptolemy, the king of the Moors <note>original: "mören künig." In this period, "Moor" was often used as a general term for North Africans.</note>, who was the son of Lagos, and was the daughter of Dionysius Ptolemy or (as others claim) of King Minaios, she nevertheless came to the government of the kingdom through roguish wickedness.

For Dionysius <note>original: "dionisi'"; likely referring to Ptolemy XII Auletes, who styled himself the "New Dionysos."</note>, her father, was the chief friend of the Romans and of Julius the Emperor <note>Julius Caesar</note> during the first year of his reign. ...had him killed with a poisoned drink. Through this, she ruled the kingdom alone. But shortly thereafter it happened that the great Pompey conquered almost all of Asia and marched with his army into Egypt. There, he made the younger son king and ruler of the realm of Egypt; because of this, Cleopatra was so distressed that she prepared her weapons to fight against him. 

During these events, Julius the Emperor <note>original: "Iulius der kaiser"; while historically a dictator, early modern texts frequently title Caesar as the first Emperor</note> marched out against Pompey and defeated his army in Thessaly <note>The Battle of Pharsalus, 48 BC</note>. The youth who had previously been made king by Pompey was then brought low near Egypt. However, when Julius the Emperor arrived there, he found them still waging a great war against each other. When he summoned the parties <note>The rival factions of Cleopatra and her brother, Ptolemy XIII</note> to be heard (to say nothing of the brother's death), Cleopatra arrived armed with such cunning charms and royal adornments that she might well hope to move the ruler of the whole world to lustful desire. 

By this means, she might easily have her desires granted, for she was beautiful beyond all women and knew how to display her eyes with an artful glance and lovely words, so that she could easily move him to a softened heart, however she wished. Thus, with little effort, she brought the unchaste prince into her company. And they shared many nights together in the midst of the Alexandrian war, and she bore a son by him, whom she had named Caesarion <term>Caesarion</term><note>Meaning "Little Caesar," he was the last pharaoh of Egypt, reigning briefly with his mother.</note> after his father’s name.

...killed with poison, [and] his sorrowful sister Arsinoe <note>Cleopatra’s younger sister and rival for the Egyptian throne</note> he strangled with his own hand in the Temple of Diana <note>The Temple of Artemis in Ephesus, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World, where Arsinoe had sought sanctuary</note> in the city of Ephesus, where the unfortunate woman had fled to find refuge. This was the first reward Cleopatra received from her new lover <term>paramour</term> and from the adultery she committed! She did this so that she would have less cause to fear any unrest during her reign.

And when that wicked woman recognized Antony’s character, she was not ashamed to demand from him the kingdoms of Syria and Arabia. But yet, though it seemed too much and excessively great to him to fully satisfy the desires of the woman he loved, he gave her fine portions of land, people, markets, and cities in both kingdoms! And everything that lies between the great river Euphrates <note>original: "waſſer eietheu"</note> and Egypt at the edge of Syria. Except only the king...

So she traveled with these and other great gifts from **Herod** back into Egypt. Afterward, however, when **Antony** returned from the **Armenians**—whom he had forced into flight—and summoned her to meet him, she went to him. She did this so that she might receive him more eagerly with her embrace. He presented her with **Artavasdes** <note>The text reads 'Artarcanem,' referring to King Artavasdes II of Armenia.</note>, the King of Armenia, along with his sons, all his advisors, and all his treasure. He had captured them all through treachery and wicked cunning, at which the greedy <note>The original German 'gÿtig' here implies a sense of avarice or greed, fitting the author's critical view of Cleopatra.</note> woman was exceedingly rejoiced. 

She received her impassioned lover so desirably, so tenderly, and with such enticing flattery that he wished to leave his lawful wife **Octavia**—the sister of the Emperor **Octavian**—and divorce her. He did this because he loved **Cleopatra** so deeply and wanted no one else as his wife. 

And once, as they sat together at the table and lived according to every luxury, with such abundant food and drink that nothing for bodily pleasure or desire could be imagined that was not sought out and provided, **Antony** said to her: "What could ever equal our daily splendor and costly hospitality?" It was as if he were saying that no more expensive meals could possibly be made than those prepared for them daily. 

The unchaste and impure woman answered him: she knew well how to prepare a course for a single supper that would alone cost a thousand times more than the meals they were currently eating. **Antony** believed this to be impossible, yet he wished to see it and experience the delight of it. He appointed **Lucius Plancus** <note>A Roman senator and one of Antony's supporters.</note> to serve as a judge to decide the matter. And when, on the following day, the King's meal was not increased beyond the usual custom, **Cleopatra** laughed and commanded her servants that they—

...should bring her food, the servants brought nothing but a vessel with very sour vinegar, as they had been instructed by her beforehand. Immediately she took a pearl <note>original: "berlin" — an Early New High German diminutive for "Perlein," meaning a small pearl</note>, which she wore in her ear according to her custom as an ornament, the cost of which was beyond telling, and placed it into the vinegar. And as soon as it dissolved therein, she drank it off. And immediately she wanted to have the other pearl, equally costly, from the other ear, also consumed in the same way. Except that Lucius Plancus <note>original: "lucius plautus" — likely a scribal error for Lucius Munatius Plancus, who historically officiated the bet</note> stood before her and delivered the judgment: that Antony was defeated.

Thus the other pearl was kept, and the Queen won. That same pearl was afterwards divided into two, brought to Rome into the highest temple called the Pantheon, and there hung in the ears of the goddess Venus <note>original: "veneri" — the Latin dative form of Venus</note> as a long-lasting testimony to that banquet of Cleopatra.

But furthermore, as her insatiable greed <note>original: "göttikait" — literally "divinity," but contextually implies her god-like hubris or perhaps a misspelling of "gierigkeit" (greed)</note> grew even more from day to day in an unchaste manner—for she entangles all things together—after this costly banquet, she asked the drunken Antony to make the Roman Empire subject to her, in the same way as if it were in his power to give it away. He also promised her to give it, considering little his own power and that of the Romans, or what he truly had to give, for his reason was stopped up by the wine. 

O good God, what great audacity <note>original: "durſtikait" — literally "thirstiness," here used metaphorically for a bold or "thirsty" ambition</note> to ask! The folly of the giver was also no less. O wretched generosity! To give away such property, which had been acquired over so long a time with such great effort and labor, with so much bloodshed, and with the slaying of so many highly noble men, and with so much distress...

**Of Antonia, the daughter of Antony, the 84th Chapter:**

**A**ntonia the Younger—so named "the younger" because she had an older sister who was also named Antonia—was a shining example to all women of future generations who wish to maintain their status as widows in pure integrity. She was the daughter of the late Mark Antony, the Triumvir <term>Triumvir: one of three men sharing joint control over the government of Rome</term>, yet she was born of Octavia, the sister of Octavian Augustus. She was given as a wife to Drusus, the brother of Tiberius Nero and stepson of Octavian Augustus. And she bore him Germanicus and Claudius <note>Claudius later became Emperor</note>. 

But when this same Drusus was sent out into our German lands to keep them in subjection to the Romans, he was (as some say) poisoned in Mainz <note>original: "zemencz"</note> by the plotting of his brother Tiberius. His corpse lies buried to this day in Mainz, behind St. Alban’s on the field, in the stone tower <note>This refers to the "Drususstein," a 20-meter high Roman masonry block still standing in Mainz today</note>. The shape of this tower is like the Augsburg pine cone <note>original: "bÿer," referring to the "Zirbelnuss" or pine cone found on the coat of arms of the city of Augsburg</note> in their heraldic shield, which Drusus himself is said to have given to them. 

However, Titus Livius <note>The Roman historian Livy</note> writes in his final volume that Drusus fell from a horse and died, and was escorted to Rome by Tiberius and honorably interred in the tomb. When Antonia was placed in the state of widowhood after her husband's death, being of great beauty and in the bloom of her age, she believed it was enough for an honorable woman to enter the married state once. And she could be moved by no one to marry again. Instead, she lived all her days in the house of her mother-in-law <note>Livia, the wife of Augustus</note>, in her husband's bedchamber, so chaste, so pure, and so holily that her state of widowhood surpassed the lives of all other widows of whom anyone has ever written. She is so much more to be praised because she remained steadfast despite the most unchaste lineage and the impurity of her father, Mark Antony.

## About Agrippina, the wife of Germanicus, the 85th Chapter

**A**grippina was a daughter born of Marcus Agrippa and Julia, the daughter of the Emperor Octavian <note>The Emperor Augustus</note>. However, Gaius Caligula, the son of this same Agrippina, at the time he became Emperor, wished to be ashamed that he should be born from the peasant lineage <term>gebure geschlecht</term> <note>Literally "peasant lineage." Marcus Agrippa was of humble, non-aristocratic origins, which Caligula found socially embarrassing despite Agrippa’s military success.</note> of Agrippa. He invented a shameful birth for his mother and said: she was born of Julia but not by Agrippa, but rather was sinfully conceived by Octavian, her own father. And that foolish speaker <note>Boccaccio refers to Caligula as a "foolish speaker" for preferring the idea of incest over "low" birth.</note> believed it better that his mother were born through such a notorious act of a father with his daughter, than through a low-born man in a holy marriage performed according to the laws. 

But I shall let that be, whose daughter she may have been; she was wedded to a valiant and noble youth of tender age who always promoted the common Roman good, named Germanicus, whom the Emperor Tiberius had taken as his own son. Because of this, she was seen as sufficiently illustrious by everyone; but

But this had no effect on her; she wished to remain steadfast. Because of this, Tiberius was so moved <note>original: *bewegt*—here meaning moved to a state of extreme agitation or anger</note> that he ordered food to be forced down her throat. Yet the more she was pressured and coerced, the less she was willing to abandon her resolve—so much so, that she eventually overcame the injustice of that evil prince through her own death. She demonstrated that while he could easily kill many people whenever he wished, even with all the power of his empire, he could not keep a person alive who was determined to die. Through this death, although Agrippina earned great praise, honor, and glory among her own people, she left behind even more dishonor and words of shame for Tiberius.

Greatly distressed, she revealed the deception of Mundus and the priests to her husband.

### Concerning Agrippina, the mother of the evil Emperor Nero, Chapter 86.

**A**grippina, the mother of the Emperor Nero, has become widely famous, not only because of her high birth and the notorious deeds of her son, but also because of her own personal history and the things she accomplished. For she was a daughter of Germanicus <note>A beloved Roman general and father of Caligula and Agrippina the Younger</note>, the best and most noble of rulers, who conquered Germania. She was born to the Agrippina <note>Agrippina the Elder</note> who was described in the previous chapters, and Julia...

<meta>This page continues the biographical accounts of Roman imperial figures, specifically focusing on the lineage and ascent of Agrippina the Younger. It follows the typical format of a 15th- or 16th-century chronicle.</meta>

...named Agrippina. And she was the sister of the Emperor Gaius Caligula. And she was given as a wife to a disagreeable, melancholic, and repulsive man named Gnaeus Domitius, by whom she gave birth to that villain of all the world, **Nero**, whose foot also came out of the body first during birth. <note>A breech birth—where the feet appear first—was traditionally viewed as a dark omen in the ancient world, signaling a life of trouble or inversion of the natural order.</note> But after Domitius had died of dropsy <note>original: "wasser-süchtiger"; a historical term for swelling caused by excess fluid, now known as edema.</note>, she was defiled through shameful sin by her brother Gaius, the most vile of men, for she was still exceedingly beautiful.

And when this same Gaius was elected emperor, in the fourth year [of his reign], he was sent by the Senate—along with his sister and little of his wealth—to an island because of his misdeeds; and shortly thereafter, he was killed. Then **Claudius** was elected emperor, who had Agrippina called back from exile. For this same Claudius was the brother of her father, Germanicus. 

Shortly thereafter, it happened that his wife, named Valeria Messalina, and her kinsman, Silius <note>The OCR "layvschuld" is likely a phonetic rendering or corruption of the name Gaius Silius, who was executed alongside Messalina.</note>, were stabbed to death. Immediately, she [Agrippina] began to consider how she might find a way by which the government of the whole world would fall to herself and her son (who was still small). And through her beauty and various mediators and go-betweens, she drew the same Claudius into an exceeding desire to take her and marry her as his lawful wife. 

Yet the customary honor of the Romans was against this, for she was his brother's daughter. And to justify this <note>original: "zuverglimpfen"; to put a good face on a scandalous matter.</note>, Vitellius (who was allied with Agrippina and her son) brought it about that the highest Roman authority, called the **Senate**, was begging and pleading with Claudius to take Agrippina. And a general permission was granted so that it would be fitting for any man to take his niece as a wife. 

Thus, through the prayers of the Romans and the eager will of Claudius, Agrippina was wedded to him. And she was carried in a carriage to the Capitol and named **Augusta** by...

It reached such a point that he held only the title, while she possessed the actual order and rule. 

Thus, all Roman power was seen to reside in a woman. Yet this great splendor was stained by a shameful mark. ...yet she was killed by a deserved death. For she became difficult and contrary to her son Nero in many things, which is why she fell into such great hatred from him that he completely stripped her of all honors and imperial majesty. Because of this, she became resentful and often spoke out of womanly provocation <note>The original German "wypplicher raiczung" suggests a gendered view of her anger or incitement common in historical chronicles of this era.</note> that since she had raised her son to his high position with her cunning, she could also surely ensure that he was brought low. Nero was terrified by this, for he knew her to be malicious; additionally, she held much favor among the people because of her father Germanicus <note>Germanicus (15 BC – 19 AD) was a beloved Roman general and the father of Agrippina, making her a symbol of the prestigious Julio-Claudian lineage.</note>, so he did not wish to kill her openly.

At last, when Nero noticed that she could not be caught in any of the traps he had manifoldly set for her, he thought it necessary to seek a more cunning way.

This man told him how a weakened ship could be built that would be fatal to Agrippina and yet appear unsuspecting. This advice pleased Nero.

Afterward, when the ship was prepared for her death, she sailed with several of her servants one evening out toward a city located not far away. After that, she was stabbed to death by a centurion <note>a professional officer of the Roman army</note>, and burned that same night. She was given a miserable funeral and was shamefully buried with very little earth along the road near the city of Misenum, which Emperor Julius had founded. Some say that after she was killed, Nero went to her, inspected all her limbs, and praised some while criticizing others; only after this did he allow her to be buried.

## **E** Concerning Epitharis, the 88th Chapter
<note>Historically known as Epicharis, a freedwoman who played a central role in the plot to assassinate Nero in 65 AD.</note>

Epitharis was illustrious neither by lineage nor by any other kind of beauty; for she was not from Rome but was a foreigner, born of a merchant <note>original: "verköfftren," likely referring to a dealer or procurer</note> who was later freed. She also had no desire for the "good and subtle works" <note>a reference to traditionally feminine domestic arts like embroidery or weaving</note> which is usually considered a fault. However, at the end of her life, she demonstrated what a noble and strong, masculine spirit she possessed. For in those times, when the violence of Nero toward the Romans and the entire land began to grow, it happened that many from the Senate conspired together against him. And while they were considering by what means these things should be accomplished, I know not from whom...

# **EPITHARIS**

All matters were made known to Epicharis, including the names of all the conspirators <note>original: "zesamen geschwornen," meaning those who have sworn an oath together</note>. However, when it seemed to her that the business was being delayed too long, she traveled to Campania as if she were vexed that the plot against Nero was not yet fulfilled. While she was at Puteoli <note>A major Roman port city near modern-day Naples</note>—so that the time would not pass in vain—she summoned Volusius Proculus, who was the supreme commander of the entire imperial fleet. He was also one of those who had assisted in the murder of Agrippina, the Emperor's mother. 

She believed she could be of great assistance to the conspirators if she could persuade him to join their company. She began to recount to him at great length the evils and misdeeds, as well as the murderous works and wicked character of Nero. She also spoke of the Emperor's ingratitude, noting that for the great deed performed against Agrippina <note>Nero famously orchestrated the assassination of his own mother, Agrippina the Younger</note>, Nero had never rewarded him, nor granted him any promotion according to his merit.

Where would there be a man who, to preserve his own life, could suffer so much torture without confessing as this woman suffered to keep other people alive? Chapter 25.

...was of an enlightened mind. This same Seneca (as the greatest wise men have said) was killed by the Emperor Nero <note>The Roman Emperor Nero, notorious for his cruelty and persecution of his perceived enemies.</note> through his tyranny <note>original: "wüterÿ"</note>, even though he was in no way guilty. He had been suspected by Nero of having known about a sworn conspiracy <note>Referring to the Pisonian conspiracy of 65 AD, a plot to assassinate Nero; Seneca was likely innocent but was forced to commit suicide regardless.</note> against his life, as was written previously. And although that was not the case, Nero nevertheless rejoiced that he had found a reason to kill this virtuous and most honorable man. For he harbored an old hatred toward him because of his wisdom, his teaching, his discipline, honor, and virtue—to all of which Nero was an enemy by his innate malice. <insert>Therefore he commanded Seneca that</insert> <note>This section is highlighted in yellow in the original manuscript.</note> he should choose for himself the manner in which he wished to die. 

As soon as Pompeia <note>Pompeia Paulina, Seneca’s wife.</note> noticed that he was preparing to fulfill the Emperor’s command, she set aside all coaxing and words of comfort meant to give hope for his life. Through the compulsion of ardent love for her husband, she steeled her spirit to drive out her great sorrow and dismay by a shared death: wishing to die willingly with her husband, just as they had lived together in such harmony. And in the same way as her husband, she undauntedly and with a strong spirit let her veins be opened in lukewarm water <note>Suicide by opening the veins in a warm bath was a traditional Roman method for those of high status ordered to die, intended to speed the process and numb the pain.</note>, letting her blood flow out until death. 

But when Nero heard this, he immediately commanded that she should be kept alive, for he bore no particular ill-will toward her, and also so that his tyranny might not be spoken of so widely. Yet the servants could not arrive soon enough to stop her bleeding; she had bled so much that her pallor gave eternal testimony that she had shed much blood and the spirit of life with her husband. She mourned him thereafter with honorable memory for a few years, remaining in a pure widow’s seat <note>A poetic term indicating she remained a faithful widow and did not remarry for the rest of her life.</note> as she remembered her husband and ended her days. 

**O** <note>Large red initial in the original.</note> God, what is nobler or greater than the sweet love of the holy...

This lady followed the ancient, holy custom of married women, who shunned not only a third, fourth, or perhaps even a sixth marriage, but even a second; for after the death of their first husband, they quickly turned to holy chastity and a worthy, praiseworthy, and eternal memory. Not like so many of our own women, who immediately take one husband after another, as if they had lived and learned their ways in brothels <note>The original text uses "offnen hůsern" (open houses), a contemporary euphemism for houses of prostitution.</note>, a behavior that should surely be curtailed for the sake of womanly honor.

<note>This page number is written in Roman numerals as .cxxvi.</note>

...she was well-proportioned. Her constant habit was to outwardly maintain a modest demeanor, while secretly indulging in unchastity <note>original: "unkůschait"</note> (as unfortunately many do who desire to be highly honored). And although she went out in public only rarely, she nevertheless employed her enticing cunning. For when she noticed that many men—and especially the high-ranking lords—were moved to physical desire by her beauty, she always kept the greater part of her face covered. This was not because she did not wish to be seen, but rather so that they would not be satisfied by the sight of her beauty, and would thus be enticed more and more to see her fully. 

To pass over many of her other habits: she reached a point where she cared little for her own honor, and wherever the profit was greatest, there she inclined her unchaste mind. In these matters, this infamous woman had great success—so much so that in a short time she acquired such great wealth that the status of her noble birth (as her ancestors had done) could be well maintained. 

She was first married to a knightly youth named Rufus Crispus <note>This refers to Rufrius Crispinus, a Roman eques and commander of the Praetorian Guard.</note>. After she had borne a son by him, she was moved toward the love of a wealthy and powerful youth named Otho <note>Marcus Salvius Otho, who would later briefly become Emperor.</note>, who was a favorite of the Emperor Nero. Shortly thereafter, once she had often known him illicitly, Rufus died, and Otho took her as his wife. 

Not long after this, Nero was enticed by the words of this same Otho into a love for Sabina <note>Poppaea Sabina.</note>. Whether this happened because of Otho's reckless words spoken out of passionate love for his wife, or because he could no longer tolerate her fickle and unchaste ways and wished to be rid of her, or whether it was simply Poppaea's assigned fortune, I do not know. He valued these as great and shining virtues, though such things are actually a poisonous injury to the minds of men. She possessed no greater goodness than this.

### About Triaria, the wife of Lucius Vitellius, the 91st chapter.

**T**riaria was the wife of the brother of the Roman prince Aulus Vitellius. She is famous solely for her extraordinary love and "manly" <note>The author uses "manly" (manlich) as a compliment to her courage, reflecting the gendered expectations of the 15th century.</note> support of her husband. During the time when Emperor Vitellius and Vespasian were struggling against one another for control of the Empire, and a great multitude from both parties had gathered at the city of Terracina <note>original: "tarracinam"</note> and prepared for battle, Triaria armed herself. She did so with such boldness, such knightly spirit, such manliness, and such defiance to protect her husband and make him victorious, that much blood was shed by her own hand. Because of this, her memory is highly praised and established for eternity. Since nothing else is written regarding her other deeds, I have kept this account of her "unwomanly" fighting brief.

...memory most worthy/ for (from whom she might have learned/ I do not know) she was a great master of the <term>seven liberal arts<note>The standard medieval curriculum consisting of the Trivium (grammar, logic, rhetoric) and the Quadrivium (arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy).</note>/ as can be clearly seen from her poems. And above all/ she was so thoroughly and completely learned in the books of Virgil/ that they could never depart from her memory/ as her book named the <term>Cento<note>original: "centona". A "Cento" is a "patchwork" poem made up of verses or fragments taken from other authors (in this case Virgil) and rearranged to create a new narrative.</note> clearly proves/ in which she describes the <note>original: "alten vnd nůwen ee", literally "the Old and New Law," a common term for the Testaments.</note> Old and New Testaments/ so clearly/ so lovely/ so sweetly/ and so pleasantly: that it would be a wonder/ for a woman to even conceive of such things: to say nothing of bringing them to completion. And so it recounts from the creation of the world/ and of the first man/ until the holy Pentecost/ the sending of the Holy Spirit to his disciples. And this is done solely from the verses of the master Virgil/ now whole, then half/ now taken from the beginning, then the middle/ or perhaps the end/ according to how they served her purpose. And she did this so cleverly/ that anyone/ who was ignorant of these matters/ might easily take her for a prophet or an evangelist/ or even Virgil himself/ from whom she collected this book.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of a remarkable woman of antiquity, likely Faltonia Betitia Proba, a 4th-century Roman poet. The author is praising her intellectual achievements—specifically her ability to repurpose pagan literature for Christian themes—and using her as a moral example for contemporary women.</meta>

She also wrote other books on Holy Scripture, researching them so well that many of the most highly learned doctors <note>original: "doctor"; here referring to Doctors of the Church or university theologians</note> approved of them. Thus, it is right to mourn that men are so lazy in their studies that they should be surpassed by a woman. And the more I read this little book of hers, called the <term>Cento</term> <note>original: "centonam"; a poem made by piecing together verses from other authors</note>, the more I find that she and her book are worthy of eternal remembrance. I cannot help but think that, had she lived longer, she would have written many other books which have perhaps—to our misfortune—been despised, neglected, and lost to time. 

Yet some claim she used the same skill to create a <term>Homericento</term> <note>original: "homericentonam"; a cento made from the works of the Greek poet Homer</note> from the works of Homer, just as she made the Cento from Virgil <note>original: "virgilio"</note>. If this is true, her praise should be rightly increased, for it would be certain proof that she was as well-versed in the Greek arts as she was in the Latin. 

It is a marvelous thing to say that a woman should possess such high intellect that she could create her own books about the life of Christ—so orderly, so elegant, and without any error in the meter, syllables, or words—drawing from all the Christian books. But it is even greater and more to be prized that she took these verses from pagan poems! 

Oh, how diligently she set aside all the laziness of her spirit so that she might seek out the eternal light! Oh, that the women of our own day—who are slaves to every pleasure—would look upon this example! From morning until night, they do nothing but chatter, engage in vanities, and perhaps indulge the pleasures of the body. If only they would consider the difference between those women who spent their time on praiseworthy works and those who conducted their lives in such a way that their names will be buried along with their dead bodies, just as if they had never lived at all.

Furthermore, she was of such exquisite beauty that it was esteemed by everyone to be some divine adornment mixed with mortality. And so that this same beauty would not fade or perish through old age or death, it happened that her likeness, from the time of her blooming youth, was struck onto gold, silver, and copper coins <note>original: "pfening," here referring generally to minted currency</note> which are still seen to this very day, and from which one may well observe her beauty. 

But as widely as her beauty was spread throughout the whole world, she was stained just as much by shameful marks of unchastity. For it is held of her that she was not satisfied with a single lover <note>original: "bůlen"</note> or husband, but that she came to the unchaste embraces of many men. Among those, some were notorious by name for their adultery with her, such as Vertilus Orphicus and Moderacius <note>These names likely refer to Tutilius, Orfitus, and Moderatus, figures mentioned in the "Historia Augusta" as Faustina's alleged lovers</note>. But the one who was loved above all others was Troculus, whom the Emperor once even caught with her during dinner. Among these, Marcus Verus <note>This refers to Lucius Verus, who was Marcus Aurelius's co-emperor and husband to Faustina's daughter, Lucilla</note> was also counted, even though he was her son-in-law. 

And what is worse than all of this, it is said of her: that she was so inflamed with love for a gladiator <term>gladiator <gloss>original: "rüter," literally a rider or knight, but used here in the context of the Roman arena</gloss></term> that she fell into a deadly illness from excessive desire for him. And so that she might regain her health, she revealed to the Emperor, her husband, the fervor of her love for the gladiator. Upon the advice of a physician, he had her anointed with the warm blood of that same gladiator—drawn from his heart—to extinguish the violent heat of his wife. 

Yet some say she was pregnant at that same time and bore a son named Commodus Antoninus, whom everyone considers to be the son of that same gladiator and not the Emperor’s. When the shameful conduct of Faustina was thus spread abroad, Antoninus's friends advised him...

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Emperor Marcus Aurelius Antoninus, specifically addressing the scandals surrounding his wife, Faustina the Younger, and his reaction to her infidelity and eventual death.</meta>

->.130.<-

He should kill her, or if he wished to act more humanely, he should separate from her. But Antoninus <note>Marcus Aurelius Antoninus, the Roman Emperor known for his Stoic philosophy.</note> was kind, and although he bore his wife's adultery with a heavy heart, he nevertheless resisted the advice of his friends. Only so that no greater shame should arise from it, he preferred to endure this. And he gave no other answer to his friends who had advised him to separate from her, except that he said one must return the dowry <term>haimstůr: the "Heimsteuer" or dowry; legally, if a husband divorced his wife, he often had to return the wealth she brought into the marriage.</term> to divorced women. With these words, he gave them to understand that he possessed the empire through Faustina <note>Faustina was the daughter of the previous Emperor, Antoninus Pius; Marcus Aurelius's legitimacy was tied to his marriage into her family.</note>; therefore, many such things must often be endured in hopes of improvement. For the honorable are often seen in difficult cases, which nevertheless emerge from dark clouds back into the light. 

When Antoninus had traveled at one time to the Eastern kings to arrange many things for the common good, it happened that Faustina, at the foot of the mountain called Taurus, ended her final day due to a great illness. Through the prayers of Antoninus, she was sanctified by the Romans and was henceforth always called the **Divine Faustina** <note>original: "diva faustina"</note>, which means the divine or holy Faustina. This honor had not been granted to any woman by the Romans before her. Then Antoninus had her named **Mother of the Camp** <note>original: "matrem caſtrorum"; a title given to Empress Faustina to honor her for accompanying the army on campaigns.</note>, which means a mother of the fortresses or castles. And he built a temple for her at the place where she had passed away and had beautiful images carved in her likeness to honor her. He also established a gathering of maidens called the **Faustinianas** <note>This refers to the *Puellae Faustinianae*, a charitable foundation for orphaned girls established in her memory.</note>. And so, for a time, she was honored as holy, because her unchaste misdeeds were turned into goodness.

But furthermore, what else happened? Elagabalus never went into the council unless his "holy" mother went with him; and blind fortune <note>original: "sâlikait"</note> so endowed her that she was held in such high regard by the common rabble, and so honored, that the wise Sibyl <note>A legendary prophetess of antiquity whose books were central to Roman religion.</note> was valued less than she by the fickle people. He further ordered that these same women should be rewarded most generously from the public treasury. 

He also decreed that all children were to be raised in common; no man should own either a wife or a child as his own, along with many similar rules. I shall leave those aside, for here I am writing about the lives of women, not of men.

### Concerning Zenobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes, the 95th Chapter.

**Z**enobia, the Queen of the Palmyrenes <note>Palmyra was an ancient desert city in modern-day Syria</note>, was of such high virtue that she far surpassed other pagan women. Her origin was from the royal lineage of Egypt, who were all named Ptolemy. From childhood, she despised all feminine works <note>original: "wÿpliche werk," typically referring to domestic tasks like spinning or weaving</note>; and as soon as she had developed some physical strength, she spent her time in the forests, well-equipped with a girded quiver and all the gear for archery, so that she might hunt stags, roe deer, and all kinds of wild game. In this, she became so masterful and certain of her mark that few equals to her could be found.

And in a short time she became so courageous <note>original: "manlich," literally "manly," used here to describe her adoption of traditionally male martial virtues</note> that she dared to fell, catch, and kill bears, leopards, tusked boars, and even lions. Furthermore, she was without any fear in riding, running, climbing, and moving through formidable mountains and valleys, stone walls, and all marshes. She slept at night in the fields, enduring heat, cold, rain, wind, and snow better than anyone else. She despised the common company of many people and honored the purity <note>original: "rainikait," implying both chastity and a disciplined, uncorrupted body</note> of her body above all other things. Yet she was so hardened in these strenuous works that in wrestling, fencing, and other knightly and masculine deeds, she far surpassed other youths of her age. When she had reached her mature years, by the advice of her friends, she was [given] to one of the noblest...

To the young **Odaenathus** <note>original: "adanatus"; he was a ruler of Palmyra who carved out an empire in the East</note>, who was in every way practiced in the skills of knighthood, she was given as a wife. She was of beautiful form, though brown or black as all those people are <note>The author is referring to the stereotypical medieval European view of Middle Eastern complexions as being darkened by the sun</note>, owing to the burning heat of the sun; she was well-adorned with black eyes and teeth as white as snow. At one time, her husband Odaenathus was captured by **Shapur**, the King of Persia <note>original: "sapore"; Shapur I, the Sassanid King</note>, and held in harsh servitude. As soon as she heard of this—not forgetting the old hardships she had practiced in her youth—she set aside her feminine finery and went out well-armed with the strength of an army, with the help of her stepson **Herodes**, to fight against Shapur. And she did not desist until she had captured him, along with all his wives and children; she freed her husband and made all of Mesopotamia subject to her commands. 

She also won many Roman lands among them, which she held and ruled so powerfully that the Emperor **Gallienus** and, after him, **Claudius** <note>Emperor Claudius Gothicus</note> did not easily dare to attempt to take them back from her; the same was true of the Eastern kings, whether from **Egypt**, **Armenia**, or **Arabia**. Everyone feared her power and desired nothing more than to keep what they still held. Her husband Odaenathus was eventually murdered along with his son Herodes by the queen’s cousin; some believe it happened so that her own children could rule the empire after their father’s death. Since the rule would otherwise have fallen to Herodes—I will let that be <note>The author is hinting at a scandal or suspicion of Zenobia's involvement in the assassination without confirming it</note>. 

**Zenobia** was such a protector of purity <note>term: "rainikait" — refers here to sexual modesty and marital chastity</note> that she was not only guarded against strange men, but she was also never intimate with her husband Odaenathus except for the hope of offspring. <meta>This page continues the biography of Queen Zenobia of Palmyra, specifically discussing her personal virtues, her disciplined lifestyle, and the beginning of her military conflict with Rome.</meta>

...she had no desire to be touched by him anymore until after the customary time of purification following her childbirth <note>Referring to the ancient custom where women remained sexually chaste for a set period after giving birth, often associated with religious "cleansing."</note>.
**O** praiseworthy wisdom of this lady, who could so well recognize that nature has instilled desire in us mortal humans solely so that the human race might remain in existence and not perish, and that whatever happens beyond that is superfluous, useless, unclean, and shameful. But where are more such women to be found? Not here. 
She maintained a magnificent and costly court, according to royal estate; yet she did not have daily servants unless they were old and all castrated <term>eunuchs</term>, so that any frivolous temptation from anyone might be all the better avoided. She also desired to be worshipped by her people (as was the custom of the Queens of Persia). She also held banquets according to the custom of Roman emperors with golden vessels, adorned throughout with many a precious stone after the custom of Cleopatra, as she had heard of her; and although she was a great preserver of treasures, there was never found anyone more generous in spending for the sake of honor. 
**A**nd although in her youth she was diligently devoted to the hunt, she nevertheless learned Greek, Egyptian <note>original: "haidniſch" (heathen); likely referring to the Egyptian or Palmyrene language used in her court</note>, and Latin so well that she could read and understand all the ancient histories in every language. She also desired that the common language of her children should be Latin. What shall I say? She reached the point where she intended to suppress the power of the Romans; however, after the Emperors Gallienus, Aurelian <note>original: "aurelian⁹"</note>, and Claudius had died, Marcus Manicus <note>likely a corruption of a Roman name, possibly referencing Probus or a general under Aurelian</note>, who was chosen as emperor there, prepared himself against her and sent out Aurelian with the strength of an army. He came to Emesa <note>Modern-day Homs, Syria</note> to besiege her. Zenobia marched out against him, unafraid, with the help of Zabdas <note>original: "zebonis"; Zenobia's famous commanding general</note>, who stood by her.

<summary>This page describes Queen Zenobia’s personal discipline, her multilingual education, and her royal court, concluding with the start of the Roman military campaign against her at Emesa.</summary>
<keywords>Queen Zenobia, Palmyra, Roman Empire, Chastity, Eunuchs, Cleopatra, Education, Emesa, Zabdas</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Zenobia, Queen of Palmyra, following her defeat by the Romans, and begins the account of the legendary Pope Joan.</meta>

->134<-

...but she was put to flight. After long wars, she was captured by Aurelian <note>Lucius Domitius Aurelianus, Roman Emperor from 270 to 275 AD, who defeated the Palmyrene Empire</note> along with her sons. Aurelian took no less glory and pride in this capture than if he had captured the most powerful prince or the greatest enemy of Roman power. He led her and her sons toward Rome, bound with golden chains around her neck, hands, and feet, with a golden crown upon her head. Her royal garments were so heavily weighted with pearls and gemstones <note>The text describes her being "beschwäret" (weighed down) by her own finery</note> that she sometimes had to rest under the weight of her ornate clothing. Finally, she was placed within the company of noble Roman women. The Roman authorities provided her with an honorable residence, which for a long time afterward bore the name "Zenobia" after the queen; it was located near the palace of the Divine Hadrian <note>original: "diui adriani"; refers to the Emperor Hadrian's villa or complex</note>, and there she ended her life.

.c.xxxv.

...which was never granted to any woman of the Christian order to perform. She held that same papal dignity for several years, presenting herself as a **Vicar of Christ** <note>original: "verweser cristi"; a title for the Pope as the earthly representative of Jesus.</note>. This continued until God the Lord from above had mercy on His people, seeing that such a highly venerable office was being held in this manner, and that the people were being governed and so greatly deceived in error by a woman. He did not wish to leave such power in her hands any longer. 

Therefore, through the counsel of the Devil—who had previously instilled such arrogance in her—she was passionately incited toward unchastity. It reached such a point that all the skills she had used so successfully to acquire the papacy could no longer help her extinguish the internal fires, until a man was found who tamed her passion. He "hallowed" <note>The author uses "helliget" (sanctified) ironically here to describe the act of impregnation.</note> the occupant of Saint Peter’s throne until the Pope became pregnant. 

O, unworthy sin! O, great patience of God! What happened next? This woman, who for a long time was able to cloud the eyes of men, was unable—despite all her arts—to hide her labor. For once, after she had celebrated Mass and while a public procession was moving between the **Colosseum** and the old hall of **Pope Clement**, she gave birth to a child in front of all the people, without the help of midwives. Because of this, she was cast by the authorities into the "outer darkness" <note>A biblical reference to being cast out of God's presence or society.</note> and she perished along with the child on that spot. 

And to keep the memory of such shameful impurity alive, during the times of the public processions <term>krüczgeng: religious processions where a cross is carried</term> of the Pope and all the people, when they reach the place of the birth, they shrink away from it. They turn from that path into other ways and streets to curse that sinful place, before returning to the route from which they came.

<summary>This page recounts the climax of the legend of Pope Joan, describing her pregnancy and the public birth of her child during a papal procession, which led to her death and a ritual change in Roman procession routes.</summary>
