# About Irene the Roman Empress, the 97th chapter

**I**rene was the noblest woman of Athens, shining with exceptional beauty. At one time, the Emperor Constantine <note>original: "Constantinus"; refers to the Byzantine Emperor Constantine V (reigned 741–775).</note> summoned her and betrothed her to his son, who was named Leo. And after the death of Constantine, she became Empress and bore a son to her husband Leo, whom she named Constantine <note>Constantine VI</note> after her father-in-law. 

Finally, when Leo had lived out his days, she reigned with her young son Constantine for ten years, magnificently and well. But when he had grown into the age of a young man, he believed that the empire should belong to him alone, and he cast his mother out from his company for eight years. 

Eventually (since this high-minded <note>The German "großmütig" here implies a spirit that is both proud and ambitious.</note> woman was eager to rule), when she had fallen into discord with her son, she devised through womanly guile <note>original: "wýplich list"; a common medieval literary trope used to describe the clever, often deceptive, strategies attributed to women in power.</note> how she might return to the empire. She considered how she might capture him and, having deposed him from power, keep him locked away in prison; all of which [was carried out]...

<summary>This page begins the biography of the Byzantine Empress Irene, detailing her rise to power through marriage and the subsequent political conflict with her son, Constantine VI.</summary>
<keywords>Empress Irene, Constantine VI, Leo IV, Athens, Byzantine Empire, female regency, power struggle</keywords>

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Byzantine regency, specifically the power struggle between Empress Irene and her son, Constantine VI. The previous page likely detailed Irene's initial seizure of power or the deposition of her son.</meta>

.clxxxvj. <note>Page 186</note>

...happened, and she came to occupy the throne alone, before which all the world—shortly before—had received the laws. She reigned, indeed, far above other mortal princes in great glory for five years. After that, however, it came to pass—through the petitions of his friends and the help of the people of **Armenia**—that **Irene** <note>original: "Prenes"; referring to Empress Irene of Athens (r. 797–802).</note> was again removed from the imperial throne, and **Constantine** <note>Constantine VI, Irene’s son, whom she had previously deposed and imprisoned.</note> was released from prison and elevated to his father's throne. 

He was also much kinder toward his mother than she had been toward him; for he did not have her thrown into a dungeon. Instead, in the **Eleutherios Palace** <note>original: "palaſt elenteri"; a palace in Constantinople commissioned by Irene.</note>, which she herself had built, he provided her with all her necessities in abundance, according to royal honors. However, he sent all her friends and followers into **exile** <note>original: "ellend"; in this period, the word specifically denoted being in a foreign land or forced banishment.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the temporary restoration of Constantine VI to the Byzantine throne after his mother, Irene, had ruled alone for five years. It highlights Constantine's perceived mercy in placing his mother under house arrest in her own palace while exiling her supporters.</summary>

<keywords>Empress Irene, Constantine VI, Byzantium, Armenia, Eleutherios Palace, exile, succession, Constantinople</keywords>

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the Byzantine Empress Irene, following her struggle for power with her son Constantine VI, and introduces the story of Engeldruda of Ravenna.</meta>

It also happened, through the influence of gifts, that those who had previously deposed Irene captured Constantine and deprived him of his sight; thus Irene was left in possession of the empire again, which had been taken from her before. Shortly thereafter, Constantine fell into a fatal illness and ended his days. 

But afterwards, when she had reigned for five years, Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus I, who reigned from 802–811 AD</note> set himself against her and besieged her palace, the Eleutherium <note>The Palace of Eleutherios in Constantinople</note>. He had by then received the crown of the empire from the patriarch at Constantinople, with the consent of the highest councilors whom Irene had previously enriched with many kinds of gifts. And it happened that Nicephorus, appearing in a humble manner, went to the Empress and—with soft, kind words and many vows and promises—persuaded her to reveal all her hidden treasures to him. 

But the rogue, as soon as he had learned where they were, sent the deceived lady away to Lesbos <note>original: "lescam"</note> into exile. There she spent her old age honorably and occupied herself with good works. However, some maintained that because the mother and the son were at odds, and because a certain division of the Roman Empire had grown from this, the Romans called upon Charlemagne <note>original: "karolũ den grossen"</note>, the great king of France, and gave him the empire to bring it back into unity. Thus, for the first time, imperial authority passed out of the hands of the Romans <note>The author here refers to the transfer of the Roman title to the Franks in the West</note>. Irene was even to be betrothed to Charlemagne; yet in the end, she was forced by Aetius and Nicephorus into a nunnery.

### Of the Virgin Engeldruda. The 97th Chapter.

**E**ngeldruda, of the most noble lineage of the Lords of Ravenna (who had once come from Florence), is also not unjustly counted among the virtuous women because of the greatness of her spirit, through which she bravely protected her purity <term>purity: refers here to her virginity or chastity</term> against the Roman prince. For

<summary>This page describes the downfall of Empress Irene, her exile to Lesbos by Nicephorus, and the rise of Charlemagne as Emperor in the West. It begins the next chapter regarding the virtuous Engeldruda of Ravenna.</summary>
<keywords>Empress Irene, Constantine VI, Nicephorus I, Charlemagne, Byzantine Empire, Roman Empire, Lesbos, Engeldruda of Ravenna, Florence, chastity</keywords>

--- Page 305 ---
138

At the time when the Roman Emperor Otto the Fourth <note>Otto IV (r. 1198–1215), the only member of the Welf dynasty to become Holy Roman Emperor.</note> arrived in Florence by chance, many of the most honorable women were assembled in St. John's Church <note>The Baptistery of San Giovanni in Florence.</note>, which was once built as a temple to the idol Mars. And so that the Emperor might make the festival more solemn and worthy with his presence, he entered there as well with a vast multitude of his princes and lords. 

When he was seated in the highest place in accordance with his dignity, he observed the decoration of the temple, the gathering of the citizens, and especially the women sitting around; however, he fixed his eyes longer upon Engeldruda <note>Historically known as Gualdrada dei Ravignani (c. 1160–1226), a famous figure of Florentine virtue.</note> than the others. When he had noticed for a while her beauty, her simple and modest clothes compared to the other women, her honorable behavior, and maidenly seriousness <note>original: "junkfröliche ſchwermůtikait"</note>, he praised her with wonder. 

And he asked one of the citizens of the city, named Bilicio <note>Bellincion Berti, a prominent Florentine nobleman mentioned by Dante in the *Divine Comedy*.</note>, an honorable and knightly man who had been assigned to him by the city, who the maiden sitting across from him was—the one who, in his opinion, was the most skilled, the most beautiful, and the most honorable of all the women. 

Bilicio answered him, smiling with a certain praiseworthy eloquence: "Most illustrious Prince, whoever she may be, she is such a person that she would grant you a friendly kiss if I were to command her." 

As soon as the maiden heard these words, she became indignant and took it heavily that her father should expose her constancy and maidenly shame in such a manner. And she could no longer endure it; before the Prince could give an answer to his words, she stood up, her face flushed with a sudden rose-redness, and with her eyes raised slightly toward her father and then lowered toward the earth

<summary>Emperor Otto IV visits a Florentine church and is struck by the beauty and modesty of a young woman named Engeldruda (Gualdrada). When her father, Bilicio, boastfully suggests she would kiss the Emperor at his command, the girl is deeply offended and embarrassed.</summary>
<keywords>Otto IV, Florence, St. John's Church, Gualdrada dei Ravignani, Bellincion Berti, virtue, modesty, chivalry</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>The text continues from a scene where a daughter is addressing her father in the presence of an emperor regarding her betrothal and her commitment to her intended husband.</meta>

...with a completely gentle voice, and spoke thus: "Stop, my father; do not speak so. For unless violence is done to me, by God, this thing which you so kindly offer for me will be given to no one except him to whom you have sacredly betrothed me."

Oh good God, how could such a beautiful saying be erased from a wise man's heart? The emperor stood for a while in wonder; yet at last, he understood from those words the maidenly, chaste spirit of this young woman and the steadfastness of her holy purpose. 

And when he had praised with many words the maiden’s conduct toward her father and her answer, he called for <term>Guidone</term> <note>original: "Gwidone"; a version of the name Guido or Guy</note>, a noble youth. And so that the young woman would not long be without one to whom she might offer her chastity in proper friendship, if she so desired, he betrothed him to the maiden in the presence of her father’s great favor and grace. He was a man of great status and well-endowed, truly worthy of her great virtue and the steadfastness of her pure heart. 

Thus she, who had entered the temple as a maiden for the sake of the complete purity of her modest mind, returned home as a bride amidst the great joy of all friends and every person. Afterward, she bore many children by him, who even to this day have kept their names from the lineage of Guidone. I wished to write this in praise of maidenly steadfastness, which is justly rewarded according to its virtue, and as a rebuke to many frivolous young women who cast away their chastity so lightly that I do not know whether their behavior more resembles the customs of <term>public houses</term> <note>original: "offner frowen huͤſer"; a contemporary term for brothels</note> or maidenly constancy.

<summary>This page concludes a narrative about a virtuous maiden who remains steadfast in her betrothal to Guidone despite other offers, earning the Emperor's praise. The author uses her story as a moral example to contrast her loyalty with the perceived frivolity of other women.</summary>
<keywords>Guidone, maidenly virtue, chastity, betrothal, Emperor, moral example, constancy, marriage</keywords>

## Of Constance, the Queen in Sicily, the 9th Chapter <margin>99</margin>

**Constance** appeared as an empress. But since such a title of dignity is granted to so many women that people might hold them in some disregard unless other notable deeds accompany them, I say this: she has been placed into long-lasting memory because of one single birth. 

She was the daughter of William <note>William I (The Bad) or William II (The Good) of Sicily; the text refers to him as "formerly the best king," likely William II.</note>, formerly the best king in Sicily. At her birth, it happened that **Abbot Joachim of Calabria** <note>Joachim of Fiore (c. 1135–1202), a famous theologian and mystic known for his influential and often controversial apocalyptic prophecies.</note> had arrived, a blessed servant of God. He said to King William: "This birth is the destruction of the kingdom of Sicily." 

At such a prophecy, the king was stunned and terrified in his heart, for he believed in it. He pondered in his mind by what means such a thing could happen through a woman, and he could find no way it would be possible except through her future husband or her son: if...

<summary>This page introduces Constance of Sicily and recounts a dark prophecy made by Abbot Joachim of Calabria at her birth, foretelling that she would bring about the destruction of the Sicilian kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>Constance of Sicily, King William, Abbot Joachim of Calabria, prophecy, Kingdom of Sicily, Henry VI, Frederick II</keywords>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the succession of the Kingdom of Sicily, specifically focusing on Constance of Sicily and the attempts to prevent her from producing heirs to the throne.</meta>

...and he considered how he might prevent any hope of her having a husband or children. Therefore, he had her enclosed in a nunnery while she was still a child, where she was vowed to maintain eternal chastity. Such a plan was not to be dismissed, for it would have been practical; yet no one can prevail against God, who justly punishes the sins of foolish men. That which we believe we have carefully considered, He can completely overturn with but His slightest will. 

Therefore, many years later, after her father King William <note>Historical sources typically identify Constance as the daughter of Roger II; William I was her brother and William II her nephew. The author here may be conflating these generations.</note> and her brother had also ended their days, and the kingdom had fallen to her—now an old woman—by right of inheritance, it happened that Tancred, the petty king <note>original: *regulus*. A term often used by chroniclers to diminish Tancred of Lecce's legitimacy as a ruler.</note>, and after him his son William, seized the title of the kingdom. This led to great wars and discord... <note>The text cuts off at "hellig," likely "helligkeit" or "helligclicher krieg," referring to the ensuing strife and clamor of war.</note>

<summary>This page describes how Constance of Sicily was forced into a convent to prevent her from marrying, a plan that failed when the male line died out and she became the rightful heir, leading to a succession crisis with Tancred of Lecce.</summary>
<keywords>Constance of Sicily, King William, Tancred of Lecce, succession, nunnery, inheritance, Sicily, Middle Ages</keywords>

--- Page 309 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Constance of Sicily, who was taken from a convent to marry Emperor Henry VI to secure the succession. The previous context likely described the political necessity of this union despite her religious vows.</meta>

.139.

<note>The number 139 likely refers to the chapter or folio number.</note>

...given to the emperor, and she went into the princely hall, to the bridal bed, and laid down her maidenly purity, which she had previously offered to Christ the Lord, but yet she surrendered it forever, though unwillingly. From this it happened (not without great wonder from all who hear of it) that the old woman, in the fifty-fifth <note>Historical records suggest Constance was actually around 40 years old at the time, but the chronicler emphasizes her advanced age to heighten the miraculous nature of the birth.</note> year of her age, brought forth a child. And as this was unbelievable to many for some time, and was considered to be some kind of trickery <note>There were widespread rumors that the child was a "supposititious" child—a commoner's baby smuggled in—because of Constance's age.</note>, it was carefully considered how this suspicion might be removed from the people. 

And as the time of the birth approached, it was arranged by the emperor's command that all the noblest women of the Kingdom of Sicily were summoned, who wished to be present at this strange birth. Thus many women, even from far away, were gathered there, before whom the old empress gave birth to the child named Frederick <note>This is Frederick II, Holy Roman Emperor, known as "Stupor Mundi" (the Wonder of the World).</note>. He afterwards grew up to be a wondrous man, and was believed—according to the prophecies of the Abbot <note>Referring to the mystical prophecies of Abbot Joachim of Fiore.</note>—to be not only the destructive scourge <term>schelm: a term here implying a plague or ruinous figure</term> of the Kingdom of Sicily, but also of Italy and Calabria. 

Who would not wonder at, and consider marvelous, the pregnancy and the birth of Constance, as (except for this) such has never been seen in our times. Also, since the time Aeneas came from Troy <note>original: "tropa"</note> to Italy, nothing like it has been heard. Except for one Elizabeth, the wife of Zechariah, from whom by special work of God John <note>John the Baptist</note> was born, whose like—and even greater—shall never again come from the children of women.

<summary>This page describes the miraculous birth of Frederick II to the aging Empress Constance, who was forced to leave a convent to marry. To dispel rumors of fraud, the birth was conducted publicly before the noblewomen of Sicily.</summary>
